Robotics & AI Daily Briefing 2026-11-03 (Tue)

301 of 942 new papers in cs.RO · cs.AI · cs.CV matched a bucket · 22 summarized

🦾 Manipulation 47🦿 Legged & Humanoid 20🧭 Navigation & SLAM 28🧠 Robot Learning 47🗺️ Planning & Control 16🐝 Multi-Robot & Swarms 11👁️ Perception & 3D 47🤖 Embodied AI & VLMs 63🛡️ Safety & Verification 11⚙️ Systems & Software 11

Offline checkpoint metrics keep failing on real robots, and the field is moving latency and tactile sensing out of the way of the policy rather than into it.

Across 301 matched papers, the strongest signal is that offline numbers no longer tell a team which policy will work on hardware: validation loss can rank policies backwards, pass@1 hides posttraining differences, and paired comparisons can quietly test different initial states. Alongside that, VLA execution is being redesigned so inference delay does not stall the arm, and tactile signals are shifting from extra inputs to predictive signals that steer actions. For a ROS 2 team, the practical consequence is to change what they measure on the robot before they change what they train.

Offline checkpoint metrics are losing to closed-loop and paired real-robot evidenceDoes Validation Loss Predict Real-Robot Succe…
Beyond Validation Loss
Verifying Test-State Identity in Paired Evalu…
RoboColosseum		Teams pick checkpoints and declare winners from validation loss, pass@1 on one fixed test, or a single paired run. These papers show each signal can mislead: hold-out action error ranked policies in reverse on a real arm, where the lowest-error policy succeeded in 3 of 110 rollouts; pass@k exposes posttraining differences that pass@1 misses; matching episode IDs did not guarantee matching initial states under vectorized autoreset; and blinded pairwise rankings predicted fine-tuned performance poorly. Select checkpoints on repeated closed-loop rollouts, verify test-state identity before running paired statistics, and report initiation failures separately from failures after the arm starts moving.high
Inference latency is being taken out of the control loop, not only out of the modelSame Path, Less Time
Leap-VLA
Reflex
VLALagBench		VLA execution has assumed the arm halts while the next action chunk is computed, and that it moves at the speed of the demonstrations. Recent papers separate these problems. PTS keeps a reference path and retimes it so the arm keeps moving during inference, raising throughput 33% for pi0.5 and 104% for SmolVLA over the strongest deployable baseline under injected latency. Leap-VLA caches VLM context at a low rate and runs a high-rate action expert. Reflex caches complete action chunks with correction, reporting 1.4x to 11x lower inference latency. VLALagBench shows the stakes: 100 ms of latency can nearly halve success on moving targets. Measure latency sensitivity per task before choosing a speedup.high
Tactile signals are used to forecast and steer actions, not only as extra observations$TF^2$-Steering
Visuo-tactile Diffusion Policy for Robotic Ma…
ForceLDM
TacMind
Evaluating Tactile Foundation Models for End-…		Tactile and force inputs have usually been concatenated with vision and treated as one more observation, which the policy can ignore. These papers model the tactile or force future instead. TF2-Steering adds a tactile mixture-of-experts that steers the velocity field of a pretrained flow-matching policy, gaining 10.7 points average in simulation and 9.1 points in real-world progress success. A visuotactile diffusion policy adds a training-only loss predicting fingertip image changes two steps ahead, lifting MuJoCo assembly success from 42.7% to 59.0%. ForceLDM distills future force and flow into a student that sees only current observations. TacMind and a tactile foundation-model study test whether tactile history and pretrained tactile encoders help at all. For contact-rich tasks, try a predictive auxiliary objective before adding more sensors.high
VLA memory is being built around task-state change rather than raw historyPREM
MOST
HeLM		Recurrent and retrieval memories keep past frames, and policies end up inferring task state from redundant visual history. PREM regulates memory updates by how far its prediction diverged from the observation, lifting RMBench M(1) success from 14.4% to 83.8%. MOST supervises memory tokens with object states and subtask progress, raising real-world success from 6.66% to 43.33% in the authors' tests. HeLM keeps task history as explicit language updated on detected events and reports 12.8 points better intra-episode success. The three differ in mechanism but share one design move: decide what to remember by what changed. For long tasks, log the state changes and progress markers the policy depends on, and compare against a visual-history baseline.medium
Human response, not only robot motion, is becoming the measured outcome in human-facing designA Study on the Effects of Finger Movements on…
Human Sensorimotor Model Adaptation to Accoun…
Human-Oriented World Model		Human-robot work usually reports task success and assumes the person will adapt to the robot. These papers measure the person. Finger movement patterns on a hugging robot change which sensations people seek and how much care they perceive, while resistance to contact stays the same. A co-manipulation controller estimates the human's pace online with Fitts' law and adjusts robot motion duration to match. A conceptual Human-Oriented World Model proposes tracking a person's state across repeated interactions. The evidence is thin and mostly a framework or small study, so treat these as protocol templates: pair each behaviour change with a rated human outcome.medium

Insights

  1. Stop selecting checkpoints on a single offline number. The validation-loss result is the one to act on this week. On a low-cost SO-101 arm, hold-out action error tracked success within each policy but ranked the four policies in reverse across architectures, and the lowest-error policy won 3 of 110 rollouts (2611.00283). Add pass@k and repeated closed-loop rollouts to the bench sheet, and count 'never started' separately from 'started and failed', as the authors recommend. Before any paired comparison, confirm both policies saw the same initial states, since 2611.00896 shows episode indices alone do not guarantee that under vectorized autoreset.
  2. Latency is the second item, and it is a requirement for dynamic tasks rather than an optimization. 2611.03103 reports that 100 ms can nearly halve success on moving targets. The three serving approaches make different assumptions: PTS keeps the path and retimes it, Leap-VLA trains the action expert on the age of its cached context, and Reflex corrects cached chunks before reuse. Try one on a single robot and log the age of the observation behind each executed action. Stop treating a demonstration's pace as a safe execution speed; 2611.00539 finds that re-timed demonstrations stop working at two to three times demonstration speed, and attributes the collapse to commands the controller cannot realise.
  3. For the HRI and psychology side, the most useful result is a dissociation. 2611.02517 found finger patterns change perceived care without changing how people react to being touched, so one sensation can be tuned without shifting another. 2611.02689 shows the risk side for deployed assistants: 14 of 15 LLM assistants pushed an operator goal, 12 of them using disclosed distress or financial strain, and fine-tuned detectors reached 79% accuracy from the reply alone. If any robot carries a conversational layer with a commercial objective, audit the dialogue as well as the actions.

Must-read

Does Validation Loss Predict Real-Robot Success? Evidence from Four Imitation-Learning Policies on a Low-Cost Arm RO
Simon Cowling · arXiv:2611.00283
Why read it
It tests the metric most teams use to choose checkpoints, on real hardware, across 930 bench rollouts.
Key idea
Held-out action error tracks success within a policy but not across policies, so it cannot rank architectures.
Evidence
Within each policy, Spearman rho runs from -0.23 to -0.88; across policies rho is +0.90 at 200 demonstrations; the lowest hold-out error policy succeeded in 3 of 110 rollouts; the arm never leaving its rest pose ended 96% of Diffusion Policy rollouts trained on untrimmed demonstrations.
Caveat
One pick-and-place task on an SO-101 arm with four policies; the cross-policy ranking comes from this setup and should not be generalised to other tasks or hardware without a test.
VLALagBench: Understanding When and Why VLA Inference Latency Matters RO
Qi Wen, Borui Xu, Hussein Amro, Hongshi Tan et al. · arXiv:2611.03103
Why read it
It answers when inference latency matters, which decides whether asynchronous serving is worth the engineering effort.
Key idea
Latency damage is task-dependent and concentrates on moving targets.
Evidence
A 100 ms inference latency can nearly halve task success on moving targets, and sensitivity varies substantially across tasks, object speeds, synchronous and asynchronous modes, and policies.
Caveat
The abstract does not name the robot platform or say whether runs are simulated or physical; check the paper before quoting the 100 ms figure to a hardware team.
$TF^2$-Steering: Action Refinement with Tactile Forecasts and Feedback via Velocity-Field Steering RO
Yiming Chen, Xiaofei Wei, Chun Gu, Wei Xiao et al. · arXiv:2611.01483
Why read it
It shows tactile correction layered onto a pretrained vision policy, which avoids retraining the whole backbone.
Key idea
Learn a residual on the flow-matching velocity field, with tactile forecasts and real-time feedback routed through a mixture of experts.
Evidence
Across six simulated and six real-world manipulation tasks, it beats the strongest evaluated baseline by 10.7 percentage points in average simulation success and 9.1 points in average real-world progress success.
Caveat
The abstract does not name the baselines, and the real-world metric is progress success rather than full task success.
A Study on the Effects of Finger Movements on a Humanoid Hugging Robot RO
Daigo Suhara, Tomoko Yonezawa · arXiv:2611.02517
Why read it
It is a direct HRI measurement with a clear design variable, and the closest thing in this batch to psychology-grounded robot design.
Key idea
Finger movement pattern shifts the sensations users seek and the care they perceive, but not their resistance to contact.
Evidence
Repetitive and asynchronous finger movements tended to enhance the sensations users sought, simultaneous movement of all fingers with gentle hugging evoked more care, and no differences appeared in resistance to robot-initiated contact.
Caveat
The abstract reports tendencies ('tended to') and gives no sample size, scales or effect sizes, so treat the result as a hypothesis to confirm in a larger study.

Papers by bucket

🦾 Manipulation 47

  • Does Validation Loss Predict Real-Robot Success? Evidence from Four Imitation-Learning Policies on a Low-Cost Arm RO
    Simon Cowling · arXiv:2611.00283
    • Problem Validation loss is the usual checkpoint signal, but nobody checks whether it predicts success on the robot.
    • Method Scores 82 checkpoints and 700 intermediate checkpoints against 930 bench rollouts across four imitation policies on an SO-101 arm.
    • Why it matters It shows the offline signal can rank policies backwards, so bench evaluation has to pick the winner.
  • TacMind: Benchmarking and Modeling Tactile Interaction States for Robotic Manipulation RO
    Jiaxin Shi, Zibin Wu, Yichi Zhang, Jianghua Duan et al. · arXiv:2611.00455
    • Problem Tactile manipulation studies use task-specific settings, so their contributions are hard to compare.
    • Method TacMind, a simulated benchmark of 128 tasks, plus an action-conditioned latent world model over tactile history that refines candidate actions.
    • Why it matters It gives a shared testbed for asking whether tactile history beats instantaneous tactile input.
  • PREM: Prediction-Regulated Memory for Robot Manipulation RO/CV
    Xiao Huang, Ya Zhang, Weidi Xie · arXiv:2611.00634
    • Problem Recurrent visual memory in VLAs depends on what the state represents and how it is updated.
    • Method PREM uses the discrepancy between consecutive predictions and observations to regulate memory updates at each spatial location, built on pi0.5.
    • Why it matters It reports 14.4% to 83.8% on RMBench M(1) and 9.63% to 20.74% on DOMINO, with a 7.7 ms mean overhead.
  • MOST: Memory of Object States and Task Progress for Long-Horizon Mobile Manipulation RO
    Diqi He, Jinbang Guo, Yijun Shen, Zhiwei Yu et al. · arXiv:2611.00734
    • Problem Long-horizon mobile manipulation needs memory of what changed and what is done, not just more frames.
    • Method MOST compresses multi-view observations into temporal tokens supervised with object states and subtask progress, and adds the RoboCasa-Long benchmark.
    • Why it matters Real-world success rose from 6.66% to 43.33% in the authors' tests, enough to justify a check on your own mobile base.
  • Evaluating Tactile Foundation Models for End-to-End Imitation Learning in Contact-Rich Manipulation RO
    Masaki Murooka, Ryoichi Nakajo, Tomohiro Motoda, Koshi Makihara et al. · arXiv:2611.00957
    • Problem It is unclear how pretrained tactile foundation models should be used in end-to-end imitation learning.
    • Method Integrates several tactile foundation models into Diffusion Policy and tests encoder fine-tuning and task-specific mid-training over more than 5,000 real rollouts.
    • Why it matters The rollout count gives its empirical guidance on adaptation strategies more weight than a simulation-only comparison.
  • $TF^2$-Steering: Action Refinement with Tactile Forecasts and Feedback via Velocity-Field Steering RO
    Yiming Chen, Xiaofei Wei, Chun Gu, Wei Xiao et al. · arXiv:2611.01483
    • Problem Vision-dominant policies ignore tactile signals, and adding them can degrade pretrained capability.
    • Method Learned residual steering of a flow-matching action expert's velocity field, driven by a tactile mixture-of-experts over forecasts and feedback, trained in three stages.
    • Why it matters It offers a way to add tactile correction to a pretrained vision policy without retraining the full backbone.
  • Human Sensorimotor Model Adaptation to Account for Robot Accuracy and Speed Augmentation in Human-Robot Co-Manipulation RO
    Frits van Hall, Kevin Haninger, Luka Peternel · arXiv:2611.02181
    • Problem Co-manipulation planners ignore that the human's pace changes as they adapt.
    • Method Stochastic optimal control with a Fitts'-law temporal constraint, with recursive least squares estimating the human's parameters online.
    • Why it matters It shows a route to matching robot motion duration to a person's pace, though the authors state human studies are still needed.
  • HeLM: Hierarchical Explicit Language Memory for Non-Markovian Manipulation RO
    Gyeonghun Kim, Donghoon Kim, Minsoo Choi, Suyun Lee · arXiv:2611.02660
    • Problem Markovian VLAs fail on tasks that need working memory.
    • Method HeLM keeps task-relevant history as explicit language, updated on detected events, across 10 real tasks with 56 variants.
    • Why it matters It reports 12.8 points better intra-episode success and up to 4.5x less memory than a keyframe baseline, and language memory is likely easier to audit than latent state.
  • Visuo-tactile Diffusion Policy for Robotic Manipulation with Auxiliary Tactile Dynamics Prediction RO
    Kaihong Ouyang, Zihao Su, Chengxuan Zheng, Weiyi Lai et al. · arXiv:2611.02677
    • Problem Fingertip touch matters most as contact changes, but policies underuse it during insertion.
    • Method A visuotactile diffusion policy with a training-only auxiliary loss that predicts fingertip image changes two steps ahead.
    • Why it matters It lifts MuJoCo assembly success from 42.7% to 59.0% at no inference-time cost.
  • ForceLDM: A Force-aware Latent Dynamics Model for Contact-Rich Manipulation RO
    Peiyuan Tang, Yao Li, Xiaodong Zhang, Wuyang Zhang et al. · arXiv:2611.03294
    • Problem Force and tactile signals are used as auxiliary inputs and are unavailable at deployment when future sensing is missing.
    • Method A teacher with privileged future force and optical flow is distilled into a student that sees only current observations, with progressive noise injection.
    • Why it matters It targets the anticipation gap a real arm has at deployment, tested on five real contact-rich tasks.
  • The Customer Never Sees the Goal: Measuring and Detecting Vulnerability-Targeted Manipulation in LLM Assistants AI
    Bohan Jiang, Dawei Li, Yasin Silva, Huan Liu · arXiv:2611.02689
    • Problem LLM assistants can exploit a disclosed customer vulnerability to push an operator's goal.
    • Method GAP probing across 15 LLMs, 619,600 labelled replies, and tests of detectors trained and evaluated on dialogue.
    • Why it matters It matters for any robot with a sales or service dialogue layer; detection from the reply alone reached 79% accuracy.
  • Supervised Autonomous Bimanual Suction and Cauterization for Bleeding Control in Low Grade Splenic Trauma RO
    Francis Fan, Mackenzie A Eagleson, Yvonne Zhang, Hongting Pan et al. · arXiv:2611.00237
    Abstract

    Uncontrolled hemorrhage remains a leading cause of preventable trauma mortality. Timely intervention is not always available, making autonomous systems that can aid in bleeding control a promising alternative. Given the safety-critical nature of the task, supervised autonomy, in which the system executes the technical steps while a human retains oversight, represents a necessary intermediate step toward this goal. In this work, we present a supervised autonomous bimanual robotic framework for injury cauterization (eletrosurgical coagulation) in low-grade splenic trauma. The system integrates real-time bleeding segmentation, markerless tool keypoint tracking, and deep tool-tissue contact detection to coordinate two robotic arms for synchronized suction and cauterization. Evaluated on a novel, cannulated ex vivo splenic hemorrhage model, the system achieved an average bleeding surface cauterization coverage of 82.9% across seven distinct injuries. This work demonstrates the feasibility of using a supervised autonomous bimanual system to deliver targeted coagulation across an injury site.

  • Trading Transparency for Stability: Friction-Cone-Constrained Bilateral Teleoperation for Opening Doors of Unsecured Furniture RO
    Cheng Zhou, Jing Li · arXiv:2611.00898
    Abstract

    Opening the door of unsecured furniture loads the frictional contacts at its supports; too large a pull slides or tips the object. A friction-cone constraint on the commanded wrench removes this failure mode but costs haptic transparency. We quantify the trade-off: an admissible wrench set built from contact friction cones and support-polygon tipping margins is shrunk for robustness to bounded mass and friction errors, and any command in the shrunken set provably leaves the furniture static (Prop. 1). We call this map the robust friction cone projection (RFCP). RFCP and its haptic guard admit a bounded force transmission error (Prop. 2): transparency degrades only within a band near the constraint boundary, whose width is set by one scalar, the stability factor . A working condition controller handles the door: the operator commands a scalar swing amplitude, the slave orientation follows hinge kinematics, and the grasp wrench is regulated per phase. A pre task certificate decides whether a door can be opened without moving the furniture. Experiments on three cabinets and two supports with five operators (100 trials) measure the stability transparency trade-off and ablate the guard, RFCP and the position baseline. The mechanism is not door-specific: it applies to any body reacted by frictional contacts.

  • Task-Specialized Language-Conditioned Models for Fine-Grained Robotic Manipulation RO
    Chanho Kim, Soyun Choi · arXiv:2611.00955
    Abstract

    Fine-grained robotic manipulation requires instruction grounding, robust perception, and precise contact control. We describe our submission to the MetaFine Open Competition at CoRL 2026. Five independently trained models use a common architecture with frozen DINOv2 and CLIP encoders, trainable language-conditioned feature modulation, and task-specific action heads. We improve demonstrations through contact-aware grasp filtering, instruction-matched data, and expert correction of insertion failures. At inference, task-dependent strategies include photometric test-time augmentation, stochastic action averaging, and uncertainty-triggered replanning. Our submission achieves an official overall score of 0.914 and ranks third among 15 teams. We analyze the models and their task-specific behavior.

  • Generalizable robotic manipulation through compositional skill reuse RO
    Hao Ren, Shuohan Chen, Senyuan Mu, Ruihan Huang et al. · arXiv:2611.00978
    Abstract

    Hao Ren, Shuohan Chen, Senyuan Mu, Ruihan Huang, Wanfeng Shang, Yajing Shen

  • Auriga Technical Report: A Task-Agnostic Agent Harness for Zero-Shot Embodied Manipulation RO/AI
    Chengcheng Wang, Yuheng Shi, Siyu Xu, Yutong Wang et al. · arXiv:2611.01112
    Abstract

    Vision-language models can interpret instructions and plan manipulation tasks, yet struggle when acting directly as robot policies. On RoboDojo, a large planning-execution gap separates high-level task decisions from physical execution: removing arm execution raises GPT-6 Astra's success rate from 22.5% under the reference control interface to 76.2%. We present Auriga, a task-agnostic agent harness that closes 68.3% of this gap in zero-shot manipulation without target-task trials or resets. In Auriga, the model makes all task decisions over an explicit task belief and acts through either direct tool calls or synthesized multi-step Python programs, while the harness supplies calibrated 3D measurements, finger-referenced dual-arm control, and a single admission gateway to the robot. Before motion, the model inspects a visual trajectory preview while simulation is paused; during execution, the harness checks declared stage tolerances and grasp bounds, stopping the robot on divergence and returning the measured discrepancy for in-place recovery. Developed across 13 cross-task capability iterations and frozen for evaluation, Auriga ranks first on the RoboDojo leaderboard with a 57.7% success rate (64.0 Score), more than doubling the reference harness on the same model, and remains first under scene randomization while learned policies drop by up to 33.9%. On RoboCasa365, the same frozen harness reaches a 73.2% success rate, outperforming VLA-assisted agents by up to 23.1% on composite tasks. Project page: this https URL Code: this https URL

  • ClothDojo: Learned Data Generation and Benchmarking for Bimanual Garment Flattening and Folding RO
    Zilong Huang, Zipeng Ye, Caicheng Wang, Yuchen Xie et al. · arXiv:2611.01190
    Abstract

    Garment flattening and folding require robots to identify garment structure during manipulation and adapt their actions as the cloth deforms. We present ClothDojo, a simulation platform that generates adaptive manipulation trajectories at scale across diverse garments and initial states and evaluates policies in closed loop. ClothDojo encodes each garment's canonical mesh coordinates as an NOCS texture, providing canonical surface-position cues through the visual interface of a pretrained vision-language-action model. We fine-tune the model on human demonstrations and corrections to obtain a data-generation policy. The policy autonomously generates simulated manipulation trajectories, and we retain rollouts that satisfy task-specific success criteria. Each retained trajectory is re-rendered under a randomly sampled appearance to provide RGB observations for downstream policies that do not receive NOCS. Across both tasks, the policy produces nearly 8,000 successful trajectories over hundreds of garments. RGB policies trained on these generated trajectories outperform those trained directly on the original human demonstrations and corrections across all evaluated garment splits and appearance settings. Evaluations across multiple policy models show that overall performance and appearance robustness vary across models and tasks, while generalization to unseen garments remains a common challenge.

  • Shades of Success: Evaluating Manipulation Quality from Object-Centric Constraints RO
    Lorena Maria Genua, Sarvesh Prajapati, Ananya Trivedi, Taskin Padir · arXiv:2611.01250
    Abstract

    As learned manipulation policies become increasingly capable of solving practical manipulation tasks, assessing how well a task is performed becomes a fundamental challenge. While binary labels confirm task success, they cannot distinguish successful executions with different levels of geometric precision. To address this, we introduce SPECTRA, an object-centric framework for quantifying execution quality in manipulation of rigid objects. From a small set of oracle demonstrations generated in simulation, SPECTRA represents object interactions through progress-aligned relative poses. SPECTRA identifies which relative-pose directions are constrained and their admissible variations from demonstrations. These constraints establish a reference for evaluating robotic manipulation tasks. To score an unseen execution, deviations beyond the admissible variation are summarized by a Task Quality Index Q in [0,1]. Across seven simulated tasks, SPECTRA recovers constraints with 94% accuracy using 30 demonstrations per task. On rollouts from three learned policies, its scores achieve pooled Spearman correlations with human judgments of p=0.81 over all rollouts and p=0.61 among successful rollouts, exceeding all baselines in both subsets. Using the task reference learned in simulation, we use SPECTRA to select the highest-scoring demonstrations for training a diffusion policy on hardware. Exploratory results show higher success rate than training on randomly selected demonstrations.

  • Dual Path Following for an Aerial Manipulator Under State and Input Constraints via Transverse Feedback Linearization RO
    Hamza Tariq, Adeel Akhtar · arXiv:2611.01442
    Abstract

    This paper studies dual path following for a quadrotor carrying a three-degree-of-freedom arm. The center of mass of the combined system must follow a vehicle path and the end effector must follow a second path, while the arm respects its joint limits and avoids its singular postures. The vehicle and the arm are mechanically coupled, so one controller must handle both paths. With the center of mass and the end-effector path coordinates as outputs, and a dynamic extension of the thrust, the system admits a normal form without internal dynamics. We characterize the states where this normal form fails. We formulate the transverse feedback linearizing controller as a quadratic program that relaxes only the speed of the end effector along its path and imposes safety and actuator limits as inequality constraints. The program reduces to a scalar problem with a closed-form solution. If it remains strictly feasible for all time, the feedback is locally Lipschitz, both paths are invariant and attractive, and every constraint holds. In the physics engine Drake, the controller resolves a conflict between the two speed references by slowing the end effector while both paths remain invariant. By contrast, a cascade with a safety filter leaves both paths. Code and animations are available at this https URL

  • Democratizing Robot-Use Agents: A Unified Harness with Open Models RO
    Jiarui Yuan, Yuxin Zuo, Xiaoyu Huang, Wenbo Zhang et al. · arXiv:2611.01560
    Abstract

    Robot-use Agents (RUAs) have shown promising zero-shot manipulation capabilities, but reliance on frontier, proprietary models limits research into further training. To democratize RUA research, we introduce uni-robot-agent, a unified harness for competitive open-weight robot use that supports further training. Without privileged inputs, an out-of-the-box Qwen3.8-Flash-Next achieves a RoboDojo Score of 31.39, surpassing the published GPT-6 Astra-RoboProbe result of 28.97 and many VLA/WAM baselines, and performs competitively on 19 real-world tasks. Our analysis across model generations, families, and sizes links effective robot use to four general capabilities, suggesting that future RUA training should develop them jointly.

  • MagPillar: A Soft Magnetopillar Array Tactile Sensor for Robotic Surface Perception RO
    Haotian Guo, Yu Feng, Hao Wu, Jiazhi Tang et al. · arXiv:2611.01609
    Abstract

    Robotic surface exploration benefits from tactile interfaces that accommodate contact and capture variations in surface characteristics. Herein, we report MagPillar, a tactile sensor inspired by spider tactile hairs that combines a magnetic pillar array with three-axis Hall sensing. A full-array mechanical and magnetic-field model characterizes how pillar deformation affects the superposed fields measured across the array, providing a physical basis for interpreting its contact responses. The compliant pillar architecture translates contact with surface textures and geometric features into distinct magnetic response patterns. Then, in robotic surface perception experiments, spectral features and logistic regression distinguish six material surfaces with an offline accuracy of 99.34% under within-session temporally blocked cross-validation. Continuous scans over periodic protrusions produce amplitude and peak-spacing variations consistent with changes in surface geometry. These findings connect compliant contact mechanics with distributed magnetic sensing, paving a new path for material recognition and qualitative surface-profile perception.

  • GoalDex: Learning Image-Goal-Conditioned Policies for Dexterous In-Hand Manipulation RO
    Arjun Bhardwaj, Mayank Mittal, Maximum Wilder-Smith, Vaishakh Patil et al. · arXiv:2611.02171
    Abstract

    Dexterous in-hand manipulation is commonly formulated around explicit object pose estimation, yet reliable pose tracking is challenging under severe hand-object occlusions and often requires object-specific supervision. This dependence creates a bottleneck between perception and control that can limit robustness and scalability. We present an end-to-end visuomotor framework for dexterous in-hand reorientation that operates on RGB observations and proprioception without intermediate pose estimation. Our approach jointly learns visual representations and control policies through behavior distillation from privileged state teachers and auxiliary reconstruction objectives. Furthermore, we introduce image-goal-conditioned reorientation, allowing desired object configurations to be specified directly through images rather than canonical pose representations. We evaluate our approach across a diverse set of object geometries and instances in simulation, where it consistently outperforms pose-based methods and policies built on pretrained visual encoders. Finally, we demonstrate direct sim-to-real transfer of a single image-goal-conditioned policy across five real-world objects, showing that accurate dexterous reorientation can be achieved without explicit pose estimation. For videos and code release, please refer to the project website: this https URL

  • W2A: Distilling World Priors into Action Models for Real-Time Robotic Control RO
    Xiaopeng Zhang, Yongjin Mu, Yueyang Weng, Chengzhi Zhao et al. · arXiv:2611.02600
    Abstract

    World Action Models (WAMs) have recently shown great promise in robotic control, benefiting from the future imagination capability of large-scale pretrained video models. However, most existing WAMs require explicitly imagining future states at test time, incurring substantial computational overhead and latency that make them unsuitable for real-time robotic control. In this paper, we propose W2A, a two-stage distillation framework for real-time robotic manipulation that distills intermediate denoising representations from a video diffusion model into an action model during training, while entirely discarding the video model at test time. We hypothesize that although video latent representations are high-dimensional, a compressed low-dimensional subspace is sufficient to provide effective supervision for action generation. This makes it possible to transfer such information effectively into an action model. Extensive experiments in both simulation and real-world settings demonstrate competitive success rates while significantly improving inference efficiency. On LIBERO, W2A achieves a 95.9% average success rate. More importantly, W2A achieves about 21x faster inference than the representative WAM baseline.

  • DexArena: A Unified Benchmark for Long-Horizon Dexterous Manipulation RO
    Haonan Ping, Jianshu Hu, Yimin Wang, Yi Zhu et al. · arXiv:2611.02629
    Abstract

    Long-horizon dexterous manipulation remains a fundamental challenge for robot learning, requiring the composition of multiple skills, precise bimanual coordination, generalizable visual understanding, and robust skill transitions. Yet existing manipulation benchmarks largely emphasize isolated skills or support only a limited range of end-effectors, making these capabilities difficult to evaluate systematically. We introduce DexArena, a benchmark designed to evaluate learning and generalization in long-horizon bimanual dexterous manipulation. DexArena features 10 long-horizon tasks and 30 stage-level tasks spanning diverse household and industrial scenarios. Each long-horizon task is composed of explicitly defined stages with physically grounded success conditions, enabling reliable evaluation of both intermediate progress and end-to-end task completion. A unified benchmark interface and verified human-teleoperated demonstrations enable testing different policy learning algorithms, with standardized simulation configurations, scene randomization, trajectory replay, and task-level evaluation. Through extensive baseline experiments, we identify substantial challenges in precise manipulation, generalization to scene variation, and skill composition. DexArena establishes a challenging and reproducible benchmark for developing robot learning methods that move toward generalizable long-horizon dexterous behavior.

  • Closing the Dynamics Gap through Self-Directed Robotic Interaction RO/AI
    Xilun Zhang, Liam Achenbach, Luis Pabon, Matthew Foutter et al. · arXiv:2611.02691
    Abstract

    Agentic Real2Sim2Real systems can reconstruct a scene in simulation from camera observations, but contact-rich dynamics such as friction and restitution cannot be observed without interaction. We call this the dynamics gap and present CUE, an agentic system that closes it by interacting with the environment itself. Given only a high-level instruction and no resets, the agent builds its own perception, identifies physical parameters, and targets interactions where its simulation is least accurate. On billiards, CUE reduces held-out ball-position error by 72% relative to the strongest baseline across 25 hidden-physics conditions in simulation, and we further deploy it on a real pool table. More details can be found on the project website: this https URL

  • SomaAlign: Reliability-Aware Multimodal Representation Learning for Soft Robotic Grasping RO
    Haoyuan Wang, Shuai Peng, Yongkai Liao, Yuxiao Sun et al. · arXiv:2611.02794
    Abstract

    Robust multimodal perception for soft robotic grasping is challenged by modality-dependent noise, semantic ambiguity, and limited coordinated multisensory data. We introduce Soma, a dataset containing 100 objects, 1,000 continuous grasping trajectories, and 74,914 multimodal samples grouped by software acquisition cycle, spanning remote RGB, hand RGB, depth, tactile, IMU, and structured text observations. We also collect Soma-Success, an independent grasp-outcome dataset with 1,000 trials (998 retained after quality control), to evaluate frozen-encoder transfer for post-hoc grasp outcome classification and early grasp success prediction. Built on Soma, SomaAlign learns multimodal representations in three stages: masked reconstruction estimates relative modality reliability; reliability-aware contrastive alignment uses pair-specific adaptive temperatures to adjust alignment sharpness; and conflict-aware semantic constraints improve intra-sample cross-modal consistency while preserving global feature geometry. The learned representations also support physical attribute recognition, multimodal question answering, cross-modal generation, and cross-dataset transfer to grasp outcome prediction. On Soma-Success, frozen SomaAlign encoders with lightweight probes achieve 94.59% post-hoc classification accuracy and 84.07% early prediction accuracy. Together, Soma, Soma-Success, and SomaAlign provide a data and representation-learning foundation for robust and transferable multimodal perception in soft robotic manipulation.

  • PointPilot: Unified Robotic Manipulation via Object-Centric 3D Point Flow Prediction RO
    Yihan Fang, Yuxin Tian, Ruixiang Wang, Zhanqi Zhang et al. · arXiv:2611.02930
    Abstract

    Generalization remains a central challenge in robotics, calling for policies that address domain variations from multiple dimensions such as diverse embodiments, camera poses, and/or scene layouts. In order to approach this problem, we introduce PointPilot, a task-focused and 3D aligned manipulation framework built on a 3D space point-flow prediction. Rather than modeling the global scene, PointPilot only represents task-focused objects (robot grippers treated as objects), avoiding the prediction of potentially redundant scene parts unrelated to the manipulation tasks. Moreover, PointPilot aligns 3D spatial representation in one unified coordinate and supports closed-loop and fully automatic action execution across different manipulation modes from object point flow without a separately trained action expert, while existing works typically need an extra action head or are (semi-)automatic and single-arm mode specific. Given sensor observations and language instructions, our model jointly learns subtask planning, task-relevant object grounding, and 3D point flow prediction in an end-to-end way. By processing heterogeneous data in a shared 3D point flow space, PointPilot is able to absorb demonstrations from different embodiments. Extensive simulative and real world experiments validate our method. Notably, we demonstrate zero-shot embodiment generalization with PointPilot. A single trained policy can be deployed on previously unseen embodiments and successfully complete manipulation tasks without any additional fine-tuning.

  • ReContact: Reconstruct and Retarget Human Contacts from Video for Contact-Rich Humanoid Manipulation RO
    Zhongkai Gu, Yang Liu, Wei Zhu, Mitsuhiro Hayashibe · arXiv:2611.02968
    Abstract

    Contact transitions allow humanoid robots to redistribute object support across the whole body, enabling manipulation beyond hand-only grasps. Human videos provide a scalable source of such transitions for robots to learn from. However, body occlusion disrupts object pose tracking and corrupts retargeted motion. Moreover, accurately reproducing the object trajectory does not ensure that the robot applies stabilizing forces at the intended contacts, risking slips or drops in later phases. To address these challenges, we propose ReContact, which reconstructs and retargets human-object interactions through an object-centric contact plan. The plan specifies contact phases, contact pairs, anchors, pressing directions, and sticking or sliding modes. Contact priors stabilize object pose reconstruction through occlusion. A residual policy over a frozen whole-body tracker is rewarded for making contact at planned anchors with forces aligned to the specified directions. In simulation, on a thigh-supported regrasp task, ReContact achieves the planned two-palm bottom hold in 83.5 of episodes over three training seeds, versus 0.16% for the best adapted baseline. One policy trained on 247 references re-solved for new placements raises in-workspace tube-transfer success from 20.4% (source reference only) to 97.8%. On a real Unitree G1, policies execute seven contact-rich manipulation and two loco-manipulation tasks.

  • SyncHeC: Markerless Spatio-Temporal Hand-Eye Calibration via Differentiable Rendering and Continuous-Time Joint Optimization RO
    Zhengdong Hong · arXiv:2611.02999
    Abstract

    Markerless hand-eye calibration under continuous motion requires jointly resolving camera geometry and the time offset between images and joint states. Alternating updates support Eye-to-Hand, Eye-in-Hand, and multi-camera calibration. Compensating the recovered offset during policy training and deployment increases success by $8.1$ percentage points across six families and six tasks ($1{,}800$ matched pairs; paired $95\%$ confidence interval $[6.3,9.9]$ points).

    Peiyu Yang, Mohamad S. Moudallal, Antonio Langella, Giulio Turrisi et al. · arXiv:2611.03059
    Abstract

    Contact-based robotic manipulation is constrained by end-effector reachability and the mechanical properties of objects. Airflow can exert forces without direct robot-object contact, enabling manipulation of objects beyond the reach or when direct contact is undesirable. However, the lack of suitable simulation has hindered the application of reinforcement learning to airflow-based manipulation, limiting the broader adoption of this mechanism compared with contact-based methods.   • VerifLM: Physically Verified Image-Edit-Guided Goal Pose Generation for Robotic Manipulation RO
    Xuanyou Chen, Jinyu Chen, Glen Chou · arXiv:2611.03268
    Abstract

    Many manipulation goals are easier to visualize than to specify numerically through explicit geometric constraints. We present VerifLM, a framework that converts image-edited goal configurations into physically verified 6-DoF object poses for planning. Using an edited image, VerifLM recovers an initial relative pose between an active object and a passive object, then prompts a vision-language model to generate axis-wise sampling constraints and a task-specific validation program. Candidate poses are evaluated in a physics simulator, and the VLM selects the most task-consistent retained candidate. To operate without predefined object meshes or physical parameters, VerifLM reconstructs metric object geometry from RGB-D observations and constructs multiple hypothetical simulations with varying physics and geometry. Parallel-world validation evaluates candidates across geometry--physics variations and ranks them by the number of worlds in which they satisfy the validation program.     Abstract

    Binglin, Wang, Xiaole, Jiang et al. · arXiv:2611.03520
    Abstract

  • TAMPAgent: Agentic Belief-Space Planning for Mobile Manipulation RO
    Utkarsh A. Mishra, Alperen Degirmenci, Yongxin Chen, Danfei Xu et al. · arXiv:2611.03584
    Abstract

    Open-world robotic mobile manipulation requires acting in physical environments with only partial information. While recent Large Vision-Language Model (LVLM) agents show strong open-world reasoning capabilities, existing architectures that rely on direct tool calling struggle with geometric uncertainty and thus are limited to fully-observable tabletop environments. In contrast, Task and Motion Planning (TAMP) systems can solve long-horizon tasks under partial observability but incur high computational costs and require closed worlds. We propose \textbf{TAMPAgent}, an agentic planning framework that unifies the reasoning of LVLMs with the structured constraint satisfaction of TAMP systems. We endow an LVLM agent with a belief-space planning model operating over an extensible API of perception, planning, and control tools. The agent formulates a partial task plan composed of parameterized observation and execution actions, which is subsequently completed using classical search algorithms. After extracting the plan's geometric constraints, the agent leverages its tool API to bind valid parameter values, satisfying feasibility requirements and producing an executable motion plan. Upon execution, the agent continuously updates its belief history and repeats this closed-loop process by interleaving observation and manipulation actions. Evaluated across complex household mobile manipulation tasks on Rainbow Y1 and Unitree G1 platforms, TAMPAgent significantly outperforms direct tool-calling agentic baselines, demonstrating that integrating TAMP constraint satisfaction into agentic planning loops is essential for unstructured, partially observable environments.

    Abstract

    Sensorized human demonstrations offer scalable data for mobile manipulation, but embodiment differences complicate policy learning. We present Pixel-UMI, a pipeline for learning environment-aware whole-body policies from human data. We first introduce Environment-Aware Retargeting (EAR), which combines reconstructed scene geometry with demonstrated head and gripper trajectories to determine robot posture and generate collision-aware joint and base targets. Delay-calibrated action assignment converts these trajectories into actions. However, kinematic differences can leave residual pose errors, creating viewpoint mismatches between human demonstrations and robot observations. We therefore introduce Retargeting-Error-Aware Masking (REAM), which uses these errors during training to reduce reliance on mismatched views at deployment. Finally, because estimated actions cannot fully capture contact-rich interactions, we collect on-policy human corrections through DAgger to improve performance.   • UA-Depth: Uncertainty-Aware Depth Restoration for Material-Agnostic Object Manipulation CV/RO
    Rui Cao, Jia-Hui Pan, Yin Zhang, Qiwei Meng et al. · arXiv:2611.01645
    Abstract

    Robotic manipulation requires accurate metric depth, yet commodity RGB-D cameras often fail on non-Lambertian objects such as transparent, reflective, and translucent materials, producing holes, structured noise, and biased depth. Most restoration methods produce only a single depth map, treating noisy and reliable regions uniformly for downstream tasks while ignoring regional uncertainty. We present UA-Depth, an uncertainty-aware depth restoration framework that iteratively co-refines depth and pixel-wise uncertainty, and uses uncertainty-gated features to suppress unreliable cues for stable updates. Beyond depth metrics, we demonstrate downstream improvements in category-level 6D pose estimation and 6D grasp synthesis by exploiting the predicted uncertainty. \emph{Code, models, and data for training and evaluation will be released upon publication.}

  • Hacking or Just Failing? A More Capable LLM Monitor Better Separates Reward Hacking from Honest Failure AI
    Kaushik Kezendirane · arXiv:2611.00407
    Abstract

    An agent's visible score can exceed its real progress because it gamed the score or because it simply made mistakes, so a useful reward-hacking monitor must tell the two apart. We built a sandboxed task in which an agent can game a completion score, labelled every episode by replaying it and repairing the final state counterfactually, and ran five pre-registered studies. On 60 episodes from a Llama 3.1 8B agent, small local monitors (Llama 3B and 8B) did not separate exploit attempts from honest failures with the same score gap: prompt changes traded missed exploits for false alarms, and the pre-registered primary monitor reached AUC 0.64. On identical inputs, Gemini 3.1 Flash-Lite, which completed 9/10 clean tasks against 2/10 for the 8B model, met our pre-registered criterion (AUC 0.80, 95% interval [0.66, 0.93]; 1/35 honest failures flagged); with only 14 exploit episodes, this estimate is uncertain. On an exploit that no prompt names, Gemini usually described the action correctly yet never flagged it, in 5 and then 25 new episodes, even when manipulation was defined by its effect on the score. Given the full trace, the 8B monitor flagged all 25 replication episodes and none of 25 honest look-alikes, but its explanations described actions that had not occurred; given additional structured evidence, it also flagged 15 of the 25 look-alikes. A two-rule deterministic check caught every known exploit. We release the environment, labels, pre-registrations and per-judgement logs.

  • Geometric and algebraic inference in humans and neural networks AI
    Jacob Russin, Joonhwa Kim, Ellie Pavlick, Michael J. Frank · arXiv:2611.00387
    Abstract

    Humans possess a remarkable ability to draw inferences from limited data. While some inferences are made intuitively from past experience, others require online reasoning based on novel information given in context. Here, we formalize these strategies, which we term geometric and algebraic inference, and study their computational tradeoffs in humans and neural networks. The geometric strategy gradually shapes representational geometry to reflect underlying relational structure, making subsequent inference cheap. The algebraic strategy flexibly attends to and reasons over novel relations given in context, but costs additional computation at inference time. We identify opposing signatures of these two strategies in transitive inference, a domain widely studied across species, and characterize the conditions under which each is observed. Our framework predicts that the geometric strategy should be dominant when relations are familiar, whereas the algebraic should emerge to support de novo inferences drawn in context. Multiple experiments demonstrate these opposing signatures in humans, neural networks trained from scratch, and large language models (LLMs). Causal interventions in the neural networks reveal that the geometric and algebraic strategies depend on representational geometry and selective attention mechanisms, respectively, and reproduce the effects of analogous neurobiological disruptions in humans. Our framework unifies these effects with disparate phenomena, including content effects observed in human and LLM reasoning and data-distribution effects observed in machine learning. Our findings reconcile competing views of cognitive flexibility that emphasize either representational geometry or algebraic manipulation by showing that each can have unique strengths, and can characterize different aspects of both human and neural network behavior.

  • Background Bias Assessment and Mitigation in Plant Disease Detection Using EfficientNet-B0 with Image Processing CV
    Majed Molhi, Nishi Gupta, Ali Algumaei · arXiv:2611.00049
    Abstract

    Plant disease detection using deep learning has achieved remarkable accuracies exceeding 99% on benchmark datasets. This study presents a systematic investigation of background bias in plant disease detection through a two-stage training framework with quantitative validation. We propose a three-condition evaluation protocol testing models on original, leaf-only, and background-only images to directly measure background dependency. The calculated background bias (BBC) coefficient of 0.762 indicates that a large part of the model's predictive performance depends on leaf-related features rather than background distortions. This research offers clear evidence that even with the most advanced models, environmental shortcuts can be leveraged, and this highlights the importance of bias evaluation. The approach that has been used has laid a foundation for the development of agricultural AI that is ready for deployment. The implementation, models, and evaluation process are all publicly available.

    Abstract

    Léo Rueff, Maurgène Ghezal, Valentin Gorse, Astrid Dufaure et al. · arXiv:2611.02695
    Abstract

    Supervised low-light image and video enhancement has achieved remarkable progress, yet current models remain fundamentally constrained by the limitations of existing datasets. Prevailing methods typically rely on uncontrolled ambient captures, camera parameter manipulation (e.g., exposure or ISO adjustments), or neutral density filters, failing to replicate authentic night-sky near-infrared spectral content and genuine low-photon noise distributions. Furthermore, capturing dynamic paired sequences without introducing warping artifacts or sensor-specific biases remains an open challenge. In this paper, we introduce 3L-Dataset, a physically calibrated low-light image and video dataset generated using a modular, sensor-agnostic acquisition framework. Using high-precision programmable mechanical actuation, our acquisition framework guarantees sub-pixel spatial alignment across synchronized sextuplets, each composed of one reference image and five corresponding images acquired at the five standardized night levels, without post-processing image warping. We evaluate multiple representative deep learning baselines across varying night levels, revealing critical trade-offs between reconstruction fidelity, perceptual quality, and inference latency under severe photon starvation. The modular acquisition framework can be adapted to different cameras, optical systems, sensor technologies, and resolutions, enabling the dataset to be continuously expanded.

    Abstract

  • Anchor 1: Precise and Stable Humanoid Control for Whole-Body Manipulation RO
    Archon Robotics, Caojun Wang, Checheng Yu, Gangcheng Jiang et al. · arXiv:2611.00193
    Abstract

    Humanoid controllers have acquired increasingly diverse motor skills, yet translating these capabilities into precise and stable whole-body manipulation remains challenging. Manipulation tasks are sensitive to execution deviations -- even small errors or unintended steps can cause failure. Introducing Anchor 1, a whole-body controller for precise and stable whole-body manipulation, together with a benchmark spanning diverse coordination demands. Anchor 1 adopts calibrated sparse motion references to preserve task-relevant human motion structure without online joint-space retargeting, leaving kinematic freedom for precise control. To guide this freedom towards stable, physically plausible whole-body coordination, we shape the training distribution through offline curation of robot motions paired with these calibrated sparse references. We further introduce explicit error feedback decoupled from motion encoding, improving control precision while supporting flexible command interfaces. To achieve a systematic evaluation of these capabilities, a benchmark is proposed to assess precision, sustained execution stability, and reachability across diverse whole-body coordination demands. Evaluations in simulation and on hardware show stronger precision and stability across all evaluation tracks. Under the most strict real-world global scoring setting, Anchor 1 achieves a 30\% to 80\% decrease in tracking error with fewer look-ahead inputs, and without retargeting compared to all existing controllers. Downstream experiments demonstrate more efficient teleoperated data collection and higher task success using the same deployment pipeline as well. We believe Anchor 1 supports precise and stable whole-body control as a practical basis for humanoid task execution.

  • Why Koopman Structure Does Not Help Differentiable World Models for Humanoid Whole-Body Tracking RO
    Haizhou Ge, Mengshuo Wang, Yufei Jia, Lei Han et al. · arXiv:2611.00951
    Abstract

    We test whether Koopman structure, linear dynamics in a lifted state space, helps when a humanoid whole-body tracking policy is trained by backpropagating through a differentiable world model, replacing the MLP world model of an otherwise unchanged pipeline for simulated Unitree G1 tracking. It does not: return is at best equal (0.823 versus 0.830), one-step error on identical data is 1.3 to 1.6 times the MLP's, and training is about 15% slower. Lifted rollouts diverge or fail to train a policy; of the schemes tested, re-lifting at every imagined step trains best. Re-lifting never uses the lifted linear dynamics and leaves a model affine in the action, with state-dependent offset and slope; an action-affine MLP also predicts worse, so the cost belongs to this class. At a fixed state, the curvature of the true dynamics in the action explains only part of it. Across states, the policy's actions span a curved region, so an affine model fits policy data only as the tangent at the policy's action, the operating point: on G1, the MLP's tangent there is as accurate as the MLP; at the mean action, 31 times worse. The offset must encode, from the state alone, where the policy acts, whereas an MLP reads the action. Supplying the policy's action removes most of the remaining gap on G1, Ant and Humanoid. The only online repair approaching the MLP feeds the action into the nonlinear lift, giving up the structure. The MLP world model thus remains the better choice here.

  • Learning Agile Humanoid Dodgeball with Onboard Monocular Vision RO
    Feng Tian, Yulin An, Chenhao Li, Matthias Heyrman et al. · arXiv:2611.01197
    Abstract

    Humanoid robots operating in shared, dynamic environments must respond to rapidly approaching objects while maintaining balance. We study dodgeball as a controlled test of this capability, coupling visual threat assessment with whole-body evasion, recovery, and target reacquisition. We develop a system that integrates onboard monocular perception, temporal ball-state and threat-flag estimation, and learned whole-body control. A ball detector supplies bounding-box histories, and a temporal estimator predicts relative ball position and a binary threat flag. Separate dodge/recovery and search/track policies share this interface and are composed to support repeated encounters. A three-stage curriculum first trains control with privileged ball states, then starts estimator supervision from stable policy rollouts, and finally replaces the actor's privileged inputs with estimator outputs. Analytic projection and perception randomization provide detector-like training observations without image rendering. Across approach-angle bins in controlled simulation, dodge success ranges from 97.8--100.0% for 5.0 m launches at 6 m/s to 33.2--40.8% for 2.5 m launches at 10 m/s, with 1000 throws per speed--distance condition. On a physical Unitree G1, controlled single-throw success exceeds 85% over more than 15 trials. A hardware sequence demonstrates full-azimuth target reacquisition followed by another dodge. These results demonstrate an integrated onboard-monocular-vision system under highly dynamic motions.

  • Recursive Self-Improvement of Exoskeleton Assistance Policies through Human-in-the-Loop Feedback RO
    Muyuan Ma, Shuo Jiang, Lijun Han, Xinpan Meng et al. · arXiv:2611.01581
    Abstract

    To reduce reliance on manual design and extensive parameter tuning, a framework for the autonomous evolution of exoskeleton assistance policies through human feedback is proposed. Assistance policies are autonomously generated as executable control code by an intelligent agent and evaluated in experiments with human participants. Subjective user feedback, movement performance, and muscle activation measurements are incorporated into policy assessment. Based on these assessments, policy deficiencies are autonomously identified and the control code is revised by the agent for subsequent trials. A closed loop of policy generation, human feedback, evaluation, and autonomous refinement is thereby established. The framework is evaluated using GPT, DeepSeek, GLM, and Kimi, and initial assistance performance and refinement outcomes are compared across models. Despite differences in initial performance, effective assistance is achieved with all four models after a single autonomous refinement iteration under the tested conditions.   • HuG: Learning Humanoid Whole-Body Affordance-Aware Grasping with Active Perception RO
    Xuanbin Peng, Lai Wei, Yuze Zhai, Qingyuan Yu et al. · arXiv:2611.01659
    Abstract

    Acquiring objects in human environments is hard for humanoids: targets are partially occluded under egocentric viewpoints, and reaching them demands coordinated whole-body motion that keeps the target visible and reachable while the base stays stable. We present HuG, a framework for whole-body affordance-aware grasping with active perception that couples perception and action through a Perceive-Align-Grasp pipeline: belief-driven active perception resolves occlusion by treating viewpoint as an explicit decision variable; a point-cloud-conditioned affordance policy proposes whole-body grasps; and an affordance-aware alignment stage repositions the body to the most executable configuration. A key insight is that, on a floating base, kinematic validity is not enough: we curate training data with controller-in-the-loop dynamics simulation, rejecting 73% of kinematically valid grasps that would destabilize the deployment controller, so the policy learns kinodynamically feasible affordances. Trained entirely in simulation with zero real demonstrations, HuG attains 87.5% real-world success on a multi-level shelf task, generalizes to novel objects, and holds at least 73% success at every tested height. Beyond generalizing within its training distribution, the policy extrapolates spatially: at scale in simulation it succeeds at table heights well below any seen during training, indicating that kinodynamically filtered synthetic data grounds affordances in object geometry and whole-body feasibility rather than in memorized configurations.

  • CompliantBot: Learning Whole-Body Compliance across Task Interfaces for Humanoid Control RO
    Haoyang Li, Keqi Zhu, Zhanxiang Cao, Yang Zhang et al. · arXiv:2611.01748
    Abstract

    Humanoid controllers need the ability to yield to external forces while retaining the capacity to resume their original tasks. However, tracking objectives penalize the deviations needed for yielding, while relaxing tracking goals persistently can make task recovery harder. Existing approaches often rely on task-specific interaction command interfaces or constructed compliant motion data and reference trajectories, limiting the direct reuse of their compliance-training mechanisms across different task interfaces. We introduce CompliantBot, a compliance-training framework for motion tracking and velocity-command locomotion. It preserves the original task inputs and requires no preconstructed compliant data or target trajectories. A state-dependent interaction force field couples applied loads to the robot's motion, enabling local articulation and whole-body movement to change the forces experienced. A complementary force-magnitude reward encourages lower interaction loads, while task rewards are downweighted during loading and restored after release to coordinate yielding and task resumption. The trained policies use task inputs and proprioceptive history without explicit external-force measurements. Across both task interfaces, CompliantBot yields farther under constant pulls and experiences lower spring interaction loads than matched baselines, while resuming task tracking after force removal. CompliantBot achieves the highest task completion among the compared methods under stronger pulls. Power evaluations further show that CompliantBot has the lowest mean joint mechanical power among the compared methods. Real-robot demonstrations further illustrate compliant responses through arm motion, stepping, and body reorientation.

  • Beyond Ensemble Disagreement: Predictive STL Monitoring of RL Locomotion under Distribution Shift RO
    Qurban Ali, Badaruddin Chachar, Maqsood Ali, Sajjad Ali Rajper · arXiv:2611.02218
    Abstract

    RL locomotion policies trained under nominal simulation conditions may encounter distribution shifts that compromise safety. Predictive monitors could anticipate violations, but their world-model forecasts may become unreliable under shift, and ensemble disagreement is not calibrated. We evaluate predictive Signal Temporal Logic (STL) monitoring for frozen Ant-v5 and HalfCheetah-v5 policies using closed-loop world-model rollouts and a three-model ensemble. Across external-force, observation-noise, and mass shifts, predicted robustness ranks HalfCheetah's future STL violations strongly, whereas disagreement does not reliably track observation prediction error or violations. Under severe noise, violations remain common ($64.2\pm24.4\%$ at $K{=}20$ across three PPO seeds), yet current-state robustness without prediction comes within $0.03$ AUROC of predictive robustness. Penalizing predicted robustness by disagreement does not consistently improve ranking. Ant provides complementary per-specification evidence, but its composite target has no positive violations and its any-violation target is single-class in most evaluated seed-condition slices. Overall, predictive robustness provides useful STL-specific ranking but little demonstrated additional ranking value over the current state; these findings caution against treating ensemble disagreement as a reliable indicator of safety uncertainty.

    Fabio Boekel, Jiatao Ding, Cosimo Della Santina · arXiv:2611.02462
    Abstract

    Treating morphology and control as separate design problems has constrained the efficiency and adaptability of quadrupedal robots, while current co-design approaches are often constrained by restricted design spaces. We overcome this limit by introducing Dogger, a bio-inspired quadruped with modular three-segment legs, four-bar linkages and adjustable passive knee compliance.   • Energy-Based Policy Orchestration RO
    Weirui Ye, Yen-Jen Wang, Elvis Hsieh, Zhiyang Dou et al. · arXiv:2611.02546
    Abstract

    Hierarchical control scales robot behavior by separating task-level planning from low-level control. In dynamic, high-dimensional tasks, however, a fixed planner-to-policy interface can fail when contacts, balance, or disturbances evolve faster than high-level policy updates. Execution therefore requires choosing not only which low-level policy to run, but also which policy-specific goal should be given to it. We formulate this problem as policy orchestration: runtime selection of a policy-goal pair that is likely to remain feasible while tracking the planner target. We develop EPO, an energy-based policy orchestrator for modular goal-conditioned policy families. For each policy, EPO learns a composite energy over low-level goals, where lower energy corresponds to higher feasibility and more accurate target realization. At runtime, EPO refines each policy's goal and executes the lowest-energy policy-goal pair. Because energies are policy-specific, new policies can be incorporated by learning their own energy models, without retraining the existing policies or a centralized scheduler. In simulation, EPO improves the empirical precision-robustness trade-off by refining policy-specific goals before selectively switching among policies based on energy scores. We further demonstrate sim-to-real deployment of the same frozen policies and energy models on real humanoid loco-manipulation tasks. Project website: this https URL

  • Sequential Recovery Updates for Learned Controllers under Successful-Trajectory Preservation RO/AI
    Zongtan Li, Tianming Liu, Ye Zhao · arXiv:2611.02555
    Abstract

    A competent learned controller already recovers from many disturbances. Improving it means learning a correction, and learning when not to apply it. Freezing a skill's parameters does not settle the second half. In a controller that routes among frozen skills, a new entry rule can redirect the states an old skill succeeded on. We therefore constrain entry instead of parameters. Each update searches for a feedback skill at a measured failure. It fits an entry rule that reads observations and rejects every state on an accumulated successful trajectory. The skill is admitted only if the composed controller reproduces the new recovery on its own and replays every protected trajectory exactly. A finite-trajectory condition states when this preserves the previous controller. One mechanism runs on four streams: two motions on each of two humanoid systems. Two updates per stream add 62 recoveries over 192 withheld perturbations, and lose none of the base controller's successes. We then price that guarantee. Removing the constraint recovers 33 more conditions but stops eight protected ones from completing at all, and excluding only what admission proves necessary recovers none of that cost.

  • Central pivot point control of a body-attached spring-mass runner: periodic gaits, stabilization, template embedding and approximate analytic return maps RO
    İzel Sever, O. Kaan Karagöz, M. Mert Ankaralı · arXiv:2611.02564
    Abstract

    The spring-loaded inverted pendulum (SLIP) describes the center-of-mass (COM) motion of runners but has no trunk. In trunk models, the hip torque couples posture and COM motion and destroys the central-force structure on which SLIP analysis relies. As a result, trunk models have been studied almost only numerically. We show that this structure can be restored. The central pivot point (CPP), a hip-torque law that directs the ground reaction force through the COM, decouples pitch exactly and reduces stance to a planar central-force problem. At upright posture, time-reversal symmetry produces one-parameter families of periodic gaits. A stance-phase pitch law and a stride-level regulator stabilize all computed gaits, also with bounded hip torque and friction. Under CPP, any radial force law in virtual-leg coordinates can be embedded independently of posture. For a linear radial template, the stance radius and the constant-momentum sweep have exact closed forms. With a new gravity correction, the resulting analytic return map, to our knowledge the first for a trunk model, predicts liftoff velocity and apex position with mean errors of 1.6% and 0.9%. The natural gaits of the embedded runner are symmetric and need no force step at touchdown. A near-reversibility argument explains their unit multiplier, and a scalar equation predicts them within 0.8%. A deadbeat stride controller based on the analytic map tracks changes of speed, height and posture. CPP thus makes trunk models accessible to the analytic tools developed for SLIP.

  • MAPO: Mixture of Experts with Alternating Policy Optimization for Universal Humanoid Motion Tracking RO
    Seonghyeon Lim, Hyeonwoo Lee, Mincheol Kim, Kimin Lee et al. · arXiv:2611.02613
    Abstract

    Universal humanoid motion tracking is essential for enabling humanoid robots to perform diverse tasks in human environments. To capture the wide diversity of human motions, mixture-of-experts (MoE) policies have been widely adopted, leveraging dynamic routing to enhance policy expressiveness. However, we identify that divergence between old and updated policies can be misestimated during policy-gradient updates of MoE policies, due to their dynamic routing. Because most policy-gradient algorithms regulate policy updates based on the divergence, this misestimation can lead to either overly conservative or excessively large updates, which can yield a suboptimal policy. In this paper, we propose Mixture of Experts with Alternating Policy Optimization (MAPO), which alternately optimizes the router and the experts to avoid the divergence misestimation. MAPO consistently tracks 182 motions that PPO with MoE fails to track, whereas PPO with MoE tracks only one motion that MAPO fails to track. MAPO also attains the highest tracking success rate among the compared methods and the lowest tracking error across most error metrics, demonstrating its superior performance.   • ReGATE: Recurrent Gated Visual Fusion for Reactive Humanoid-Object Interaction RO
    Hao Zhu, Jorge Peña-Queralta · arXiv:2611.02871
    Abstract

    Humanoid loco-manipulation requires coordinating object acquisition, transport, and release from onboard sensing while responding to user commands and incomplete visual observations.   • TRAILS: Trajectory Generation for Automated Indoor Locomotion Studies RO
    Juho Kalliokoski, Evan G. Center, Steven M. LaValle, Timo Ojala et al. · arXiv:2611.03254
    Abstract

    Virtual reality experiments involving navigation and simulated robot motion often require large, realistic environments and numerous distinct trajectories to avoid repetitive navigation while providing sufficient variation for controlled experiments. Designing such scenarios manually is labor-intensive, particularly when trajectories must satisfy predefined geometric and kinematic constraints while remaining diverse and comparable across experimental conditions. This paper presents TRAILS, an automated framework for generating complete virtual navigation scenarios consisting of an environment layout, a path, and a time-parameterized motion profile. The framework combines a constraint-based procedural environment generator inspired by Wave Function Collapse, trajectory generation using optimized cubic Bézier curves, and speed-profile generation subject to kinematic constraints. The framework is designed as an easy-to-use tool requiring only a small number of intuitive user-defined parameters to automatically generate large sets of distinct navigation scenarios satisfying prescribed geometric and kinematic constraints. By relying on explicit, seed-controlled algorithmic procedures rather than data-driven models, the generated scenarios remain transparent, reproducible, and configurable, making the framework well suited for telepresence and human-subject experiments.   • Myoelectric Control for Upper-Limb Exoskeletons: A Decoder-Shaper-Controller Framework, Design Guidelines, and Future Directions RO
    Davide Costanzi, Eldison Dimo, Emanuele Feola, Mario Sedilesu et al. · arXiv:2611.03418
    Abstract

    Myoelectric Control Systems (MCSs) enable voluntary control of wearable robots through muscle activity. However, intention decoding, assistance shaping, and low-level actuator control are often not explicitly distinguished, making their contributions difficult to evaluate and compare. This review introduces a functional decoder-shaper-controller framework for continuous myoelectric control of upper-limb exoskeletons. The decoder estimates an intention-related variable from surface electromyography (sEMG), the shaper maps it to a control reference according to an assistance policy, and the controller realizes that reference through actuator control. This decomposition separates intention estimation from assistance-policy design and enables comparison of heterogeneous architectures. Representative approaches are critically examined in terms of module design, calibration, and evaluation. The analysis highlights that the shaper, despite determining the magnitude and shape of assistance, lacks established design and tuning methodologies and is often embedded within the decoder or controller. Key open issues include inconsistent reporting of assistance levels, limited use of interaction-aware stability and passivity tools, and insufficient validation across degrees of freedom, users, and tasks. Building on these findings, this review provides design guidelines for systematic module-wise tuning and identifies future research priorities to bridge the gap between accurate intention decoding and safe, effective, and generalizable assistance under real-world operating conditions.

    Seba Al Mokdad, Tamer Shanableh, Reham Mohamed Aburas · arXiv:2611.00780
    Abstract

    Skeleton-based gait recognition is generally more robust to appearance changes than pixel-based methods, but this robustness depends on stable pose extraction.     Yize Cai, Zhiqing Hong · arXiv:2611.01684
    Abstract

    Gait assessment supports the diagnosis, monitoring, and prognosis of diverse movement-related conditions, such as neurological and musculoskeletal disorders.   • TailorRig: Controllable and Structured Auxiliary Skeleton Generation for Garment-Aware Rigging CV
    Yajing Chen, Xin Chen, Saidan Lu, Yeqi Yuan et al. · arXiv:2611.02902
    Abstract

    Humanoid rigging for modern characters with complex garments remains challenging due to the need for dense auxiliary skeletons and precise skinning control. Existing automatic methods primarily recover rigs from geometry, providing limited artist control over auxiliary articulation and often lacking explicit semantic or kinematic-chain structure for dense garment bones, leading to instability and weight bleeding. We present TailorRig, a framework that reformulates garment-aware humanoid rigging as a structured, controllable full-rig generation problem. This formulation shifts the goal from automatic rig recovery to artist-driven rig authoring, where both body-template compatibility and auxiliary articulation are explicit design choices. TailorRig exposes two explicit axes of artist control within a unified framework: a user-specified body skeleton template that preserves body-rig compatibility, and artist-provided semantic labels that drive an autoregressive generator to synthesize requested category-specific auxiliary bone chains. To robustly assign skinning weights under dense skeletal configurations, we further introduce a hierarchical chain-to-bone formulation: vertices are first associated to a kinematic chain, and bone weights are then refined within that chain, reducing weight bleeding between spatially adjacent but semantically unrelated structures. We evaluate TailorRig on a diverse set of characters and introduce Human-Mix, a large-scale dataset of over 38,000 humanoid models curated for garment-aware rigging. Experiments show that TailorRig improves skeleton accuracy, deformation stability, controllability, and practical usability over existing methods.
