  • Emergent Control of Ant Learning Walks in a Mobile Robot RO
    Thomas Misiek, Andrew Philippides, Paul Graham, James Knight · arXiv:2611.00392
    Abstract

    Before foraging, desert ants perform learning walks (LWs) to acquire visual memories around their nest. With experience, these walks expand, become straighter, and contain fewer visual scans. We previously proposed a model in which these behaviours emerge from the angular mismatch between path integration (PI) and a mushroom-body-inspired visual compass (VC). Here, we test whether this mechanism can generate learning walks on a physical robot, despite the sensory and motor noise inherent to real-world conditions. We implement the model on a four-wheeled robot equipped with a panoramic camera, an IMU and wheel encoders providing compass and stride information for PI, and test it outdoors using a replicated desert-ant experimental setup. The PI--VC mismatch regulates walking speed, turning, pirouettes, and returns to the nest, while PI provides a teaching signal for visual learning. The robot autonomously decides when to acquire visual memories, while its behaviour gradually shifts from exploration towards foraging as visual learning progresses. The robot reproduces desert-ant LW features, including expanding and straightening trajectories, decreasing pirouette frequency, and improved visual homing. These results demonstrate that a simple error-driven mechanism can generate key features of learning walks under real-world conditions, supporting its plausibility as an explanation for active visual learning in desert ants. More broadly, this work illustrates how physical robots can be used to test biological models.

    Stefano Trepella, Andrea Ostuni, Mauro Martini, Irene Borgini et al. · arXiv:2611.02937
    Abstract

    Social navigation is essential for autonomous service robots operating in human environments, but progress remains constrained by the lack of extensive real-world benchmarks.   • Positive Safety Margins from Artificial Potential Field Reward Augmentation for Size-Robust Obstacle Avoidance under Partial Observability RO
    Ziyi Hao · arXiv:2611.02947
    Abstract

    Collision-free reaching is central to manipulation, yet real cells often leave the size and exact position of an obstacle unobserved, which excludes reinforcement learning controllers that take obstacle geometry as input. A reward limited to a collision penalty does not depend on the clearance outside the obstacle, so under a step cost its learned policy skims the obstacle surface and fails once the obstacle radius exceeds its training value. We investigate whether a single analytic reward term can induce a safety margin without exposing the obstacle to the policy. We augment the reward of a simulated Franka Emika Panda reaching task with a horizontal artificial potential field that penalizes proximity within a safety margin around each of two pillars (fixed nominal layout, random drift). Under hidden observation, policies trained with this term maintain a safe success rate above 88% across a radius sweep from 0.03 m to 0.055 m (30 seeds), while policies without it fall below 10% at the maximum tested radius; the term induces a minimum clearance of 47.9 mm against 9.6 mm without it and eliminates training collisions at least eight times earlier (median steps). The advantage is statistically significant at all out-of-distribution radii and holds across three reinforcement learning solvers and under full observation. Its safe success rate is comparable to that of a classical potential-field planner given the pillar positions. The margin is a fixed buffer rather than an adaptation to the radius, and it lengthens the path by about 19%.

  • MS-DOTT: A Multi-Modal Dataset for Off-Road Traversability Estimation RO
    Shivani Subramanyan, Saksham Sharma, Samahith SA, Ahaan Kotian et al. · arXiv:2611.02992
    Abstract

    Autonomous robots are gaining traction in off-road environments, driven by their rising demand in agriculture, defense, and search-and-rescue operations. However, these environments are inherently unpredictable and navigation remains a formidable challenge given the complex environmental conditions including challenging terrain and varying illumination. Off-road autonomy requires robust perception across multiple spatial scales and imaging modalities, yet most sensing setups struggle to capture both far-field environmental context and near-field terrain detail simultaneously across various illumination conditions. To facilitate development of robust off-road perception and traversability estimation systems, we propose MS-DOTT (Multi-Sensor Dataset for Off-road Terrain Traversability) a comprehensive multi-modal off-road dataset with long-range 3D sensing and short-range, fine-grained perception to facilitate high-fidelity terrain understanding by combining proprioceptive signals for tracking vehicle dynamics with exteroceptive imaging and pointcloud data. The dataset includes trajectories recorded across nominal lighting, low-light, and no-light conditions with diverse 2D and 3D sensing for both long-range large-scale and short-range high-resolution sensing, as well as 4D RADAR measurements that provide velocity estimates for detected pointclouds. We demonstrate the utility of the dataset by adapting a SOTA multi-sensor fusion method for the purpose of traversability estimation. This dataset will aid the robotics community in advancing research and development of methods for traversability estimation, off-road perception and multi-sensor fusion in unstructured environments.

  • A Tightly Coupled Magnetic-Inertial SLAM System for Field Robots RO
    Chuan Huang, Tongxing Peng · arXiv:2611.03106
    Abstract

    Field robots operating in mines, tunnels, and disaster areas require reliable localization despite GNSS denial and degraded camera and LiDAR measurements caused by dust, smoke, or poor illumination. Missions involving both ground and aerial robots further motivate complementary sensing modalities that do not depend on visibility, preinstalled infrastructure, or wheel contact. This letter presents MISLAM, a tightly coupled magnetic-inertial simultaneous localization and mapping system using an IMU and a nine-magnetometer array, without relying on external odometry or platform-specific motion assumptions. The system combines the inertial navigation model with local polynomial magnetic-field models for short-term motion estimation and a global Gaussian process model for drift correction using an error-state Kalman filter.   • Clinician-AI Collaboration in Endovascular Neurointerventions: In-Vitro User Study of Copilot Assistance Modes for Robotic Navigation Across the Autonomy Spectrum RO
    Nikola Fischer, Harry Robertshaw, S.M.Hadi Sadati, Xingyu Chen et al. · arXiv:2611.03446
    Abstract

    Mechanical thrombectomy (MT) is an effective treatment for acute ischemic stroke, yet access remains limited by the availability of neurointerventional expertise. Telerobotic and AI-assisted systems may help address this challenge, but it remains unclear how clinicians interact with different levels of autonomy and how autonomous navigation systems perform in realistic environments. This work introduces Copilot Assistance Modes (CAMs), a framework for clinician-AI collaboration in robotic endovascular navigation spanning the autonomy spectrum from manual operation to full autonomy. In the first in-vitro user study comparing clinician-AI collaboration strategies, 11 experts and two students evaluated six navigation modes in a patient-specific vascular phantom. Objective performance metrics were combined with subjective assessments, including workload and usability. Autonomous performance was additionally evaluated both in silico and in vitro to investigate sim-to-real transfer. Hands-on manual navigation achieved the strongest overall performance (100% success). While fully autonomous navigation achieved 81% success in simulation, performance dropped to 23% in vitro. In contrast, several clinician-AI shared-autonomy modes achieved success rates of 92-97%, approximately four times higher than full autonomy and comparable to direct robotic control (95%), demonstrating mitigation of sim-to-real domain shift through clinician-AI collaboration. Among the evaluated AI-driven approaches, user-directed task delegation and rotational shared control demonstrated the most favorable balance between performance and clinician acceptance. These findings suggest that effective clinician-AI collaboration and allocation of control authority may currently be more important for reliable robotic neurointerventional navigation than increasing autonomy alone.

  • A Straight Line Is Not a Camera Pose: Auditing Short-Clip Pose Benchmarks CV/RO
    Xinyi Shi · arXiv:2611.00482
    Abstract

    The absolute trajectory error (ATE) is the standard measure of camera-motion accuracy, from SLAM to feed-forward 3D reconstruction. On the common Sintel protocol, a constant-velocity straight line using no images has a lower mean ATE than 10 of 11 recent models re-run with official code. Two properties of ATE's alignment explain this. First, it maps any constant-velocity prediction onto the best-fitting line through the true path, so the line scores the path's regression residual. A residual signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) criterion predicts when moving a prediction towards its constant-velocity fit lowers ATE. It gives the outcome for 95% of 1056 model-clip pairs, 98% of 792 held-out pairs and 97% of 1034 ScanNet pairs, and it separates Sintel (median SNR 0.86) from TUM, ScanNet and full-length Bonn and Wild-SLAM (2.7 to 27), where straightening raises every model's mean ATE. Second, position-only alignment ignores where cameras look: rotating a trajectory by 30 degrees against its orientations leaves ATE unchanged, yet worsens the reprojection of static points for every model on Sintel, TUM and ScanNet in the main setting, and the line, second by mean ATE on Sintel, places them worst by geometric mean: a lower ATE need not mean better geometry. Fitting the alignment's rotation to the predicted orientations gives pose-anchored ATE, a one-function change in ATE's units costing 0.17 ms per clip (ATE: 0.11 ms). It ranks every image-free reference below every model on Sintel, tracks the reprojection error under rotation and straightening, where ATE does not (Spearman 0.61 vs. 0.32), largely preserves the models' order, and ties 10-frame translational relative pose error (RPE) and the discernible trajectory error (DTE) on our rotation and reference tests. Code, predictions and results will be released.

  • GURU: A Hierarchical Neuro-Symbolic Multi-Agent System for Verification-Aware Automation of Mission-Critical Digital Engineering and Operations AI
    Allan Grosvenor, Anton Zemlyansky, Abdul Wahab, Kyrylo Bohachov et al. · arXiv:2611.01001
    Abstract

    An AI can find a safe action faster than any human, but it must never decide what counts as safe.   • Recursive Reasoning Exploration: Mathematical Research through Evolving Reasoning Graphs : case study: An Exact Certificate-Degree Theorem for Two-Term Tensor Decompositions : Phase III of the Math Instruments Program AI
    Ying Xie · arXiv:2611.02186
    Abstract

    Large collections of manuscripts and research notes offer ways to reuse arguments, while making it harder to retain hypotheses, check claims, and understand how results were obtained. We propose Recursive Reasoning Exploration (RRE), a framework for cumulative mathematical research through evolving reasoning graphs. RRE links claims and jointly required premises to research targets, attempted arguments, counterexamples, checks, and revisions. Each investigation can generate further targets, and its supported outcomes become available to subsequent investigations. This is Phase III of the Math Instruments program: identifying mathematical instruments, reconstructing reusable reasoning, and organizing recursive research from that reasoning. The framework supports human-directed, collaborative, and goal-directed autonomous exploration, with open-ended exploration as a further mode. Its records also provide material for learning how mathematical investigations develop. A complexity research case study establishes an exact theorem: for every complex tensor of order n >= 2 and rank greater than two, the minimum Nullstellensatz certificate degree for its original two-term decomposition equations is 3n. This includes an infinite family whose every flattening has rank at most two. The exploration develops an explicit boundary certificate, a balanced transfer lemma, and an exhaustive argument using classical rank-two geometry. A parallel branch gives bounds 3m + 3 <= delta_m(I_m, N) <= 3m + 6 for every nonzero nilpotent m by m matrix. Six graph revisions record the progression from inherited results to these theorems, with exact arithmetic checks and a replayable exploration history. We present the RRE representation and protocol, the mathematical proofs, visualizations of graph evolution, and a research agenda for shared human-AI exploration, cumulative reuse, and education.

    Abtin Zandi, Sarvin Nami, Roza Ganjipour, Bardia Sabagh Kermani et al. · arXiv:2611.00040
    Abstract

    According to the World Health Organization, at least 2.2 billion people worldwide live with visual impairment, including tens of millions with severe vision loss.   • How Much Can Approximate Equilibria Change the Social Cost of Selfish Routing? AI
    Yu Cheng, Tianle Jiang · arXiv:2611.01234
    Abstract

    We study approximate Nash equilibria in nonatomic congestion games, with the goal of understanding how much the social cost can change when the equilibrium condition is relaxed. We consider $\epsilon$-approximate Nash equilibria ($\epsilon$-NE), in which each player uses a strategy whose cost is at most $1 + \epsilon$ times the minimum available cost. We compare the social costs of $\epsilon$-NE and exact Nash equilibria. This lets us quantify, for example, how much a navigation app can reduce total travel time when drivers tolerate routes slightly slower than the fastest available route. For degree-$d$ polynomial latencies in both general nonatomic congestion games and single-commodity routing games on series-parallel networks, we give tight lower and upper bounds on the relative change in social cost from an exact Nash equilibrium to any $\epsilon$-NE, with matching instances for every $\epsilon$. For small $\epsilon$, the largest possible relative reduction and increase in general congestion games are both $\Theta(\sqrt{d\epsilon})$, independently of the number of resources and populations. In contrast, on series-parallel networks of depth $h$, the largest possible relative reduction is $\Theta(h\epsilon)$, while the largest possible relative increase is known to be $\epsilon$. For the classes of games we study, these results give a tight and quantitative characterization of the possible changes in social cost and establish a separation between general and series-parallel networks.

  • Spectral-Convolutional Factorized Transformer for Joint Multipath Parameter Estimation: Applications to mmWave Positioning and Radio SLAM AI
    Yaya Etiabi, Wessam Ajib, Halima Elbiaze · arXiv:2611.01491
    Abstract

    Future 6G wireless networks are expected to evolve toward AI-native systems that support intelligent, environment-aware services, including positioning and mapping. Current research has already integrated AI into several aspects of communication, particularly wireless channel estimation. However, most learning-based channel estimators focus on communication-oriented objectives rather than explicitly estimating multipath parameters such as delay, angle of arrival (AoA), and angle of departure (AoD). These parameters provide essential geometric information for downstream tasks including positioning and radio simultaneous localization and mapping (SLAM). In this work, we propose a Spectral-Convolutional Factorized Transformer (SCFT) for joint multipath component (MPC) estimation from noisy complex channel estimates and physical beam-pair descriptors. SCFT combines local spectral convolution, permutation-equivariant beam-set attention, and a query-based Transformer decoder to detect and classify line-of-sight (LoS) and non-line-of-sight (NLoS) paths and estimate their observed delays, AoAs, and AoDs. Experiments on realistic ray-tracing data show that SCFT reduces the generalized optimal sub-pattern assignment (GOSPA) error by 13.4% compared with ResNet, the strongest competing baseline. We further demonstrate this advantage in independent snapshot positioning and radio SLAM, where SCFT reduces position RMSE by 18.5% and 65.0%, respectively, relative to ResNet. These findings highlight the importance of explicitly recovering multipath geometry, as improvements in MPC estimation can translate into substantial gains in downstream tasks.

    Sicheng Yu, Liu Liu, Xinjie Wang, Xinjiang Wang et al. · arXiv:2611.01543
    Abstract

    Lixing Tan, Qing Xia, Yuting Guo, Shuai Li et al. · arXiv:2611.01536

These results demonstrate CUFormer's effectiveness in aligning partial bone surfaces with preoperative CT across anatomical sites.

  • ReinSeg: Retrieval-Guided Bounded Multimodal Refinement for 3-D Road Anomaly Segmentation CV
    Haifa Zhang, Yijing Wang, Zhiqiang Zuo · arXiv:2611.02648
    Abstract

    3-D road anomaly segmentation is essential for risk-aware perception in autonomous driving. Its central challenge is to rank sparsely observed hazards ahead of abundant normal road structures while preserving point-wise metric localization. LiDAR provides accurate 3-D point support, whereas surround-view imagery offers complementary semantic context for separating unexpected hazards from challenging normal structures. We propose ReinSeg, a retrieval-guided bounded multimodal refinement framework for point-wise road anomaly segmentation. Occupied LiDAR bird's-eye-view cells retrieve scene-specific context from a shared memory of eight surround-view images without supplying calibration variables to the retrieval module. On the Spotting the Unexpected (STU) benchmark, ReinSeg improves validation average precision from 27.54\% to 35.67\% and reduces the false-positive rate at 95\% anomaly recall from 34.85\% to 10.61\%, shrinking the high-recall screening set from 67.54 to 20.55 million points. Matched interfaces and component isolation show that retrieved scene imagery and the relative-norm bound are both essential, and ReinSeg stays above LiDAR-only performance under missing, corrupted, or misaligned camera inputs while running at 21.8 frames per second. These results establish bounded retrieval of scene-specific visual evidence as an effective route to reliable 3-D road-hazard perception.

  • Ray-SCR: Generalizing Scene Coordinate Regression Across Camera Models CV
    Jaemin Cho, Oleksii Nasypanyi, Francois Rameau · arXiv:2611.03098
    Abstract

    Among visual localization strategies, scene coordinate regression (SCR) has emerged as an attractive approach for its compactness, accuracy, and speed. Despite these advantages, SCR models generalize poorly across substantially different camera models, such as omnidirectional or strongly rectified imagery. This limitation arises because, changes in field of view, lens distortion, or rectification-induced resampling can make the same scene content appear differently depending on its pixel position, degrading localization accuracy. To cope with this limitation, we introduce Ray-SCR, a lightweight extension to existing SCR architectures that conditions local visual features on their calibrated viewing rays and refines neighboring ray-conditioned features with a shallow local Transformer. We further formulate supervision directly in ray space and use sparse SfM points as metric anchors that link observations across views and camera models. Taken together, these components enable a single scene representation built from a panoramic mapping sequence to accurately localize query images captured with very different cameras, including pinhole, fisheye, and omnidirectional cameras. We evaluate Ray-SCR across multiple SCR architectures and on datasets with pinhole, fisheye, and omnidirectional cameras. The proposed ray conditioning substantially improves generalization across cameras and leads to state-of-the-art localization performance in these challenging cross-camera settings. The project page is available at this https URL.

  • From White Canes to Edge AI: A Survey of Assistive Navigation Technologies for Blind and Low-Vision Individuals CV
    Abtin Zandi, Arian Ashrafi, Sarvin Nami, Seyed Sina Negahban et al. · arXiv:2611.00671
    Abstract

    Independent mobility is a foundational requirement for the autonomy of blind and low-vision (BLV) individuals, yet the assistive technologies that support it have historically evolved slowly. This survey systematically reviews 108 peer-reviewed studies published between 2008 and 2026, organizing the field into four technological eras: (i) a foundational era of passive mechanical aids, namely the white cane and the guide dog; (ii) a hardware era of electronic travel aids built on ultrasonic, infrared, and satellite positioning sensors that alert users to proximity but lack semantic understanding; (iii) a deep learning era in which convolutional and transformer-based detectors introduced context-aware scene understanding, initially tethered to cloud infrastructure; and (iv) a contemporary edge-native era defined by model compression, quantization, hardware acceleration, and multi-sensor fusion that relocate intelligence onto resource-constrained wearable and mobile devices. For each era we analyze representative systems, sensing modalities, computational architectures, and reported performance, and we consolidate the field's model-optimization techniques into a single technical reference spanning four detector families and twelve compression strategies. Across the corpus, we find a persistent mismatch between rapid algorithmic progress and slow real-world adoption, driven by cognitive overload in user interfaces, fragmented evaluation protocols, and the scarcity of studies involving BLV participants. We conclude with open challenges, including seamless indoor-outdoor transition, standardized benchmarks, privacy-preserving perception, and the emerging role of multimodal foundation models, and outline a roadmap for socially acceptable, user-centered assistive navigation.

  • Shared-Pattern Collaborative Network for Multi-Source Remote Sensing Data Classification CV
    Chenfang Yang, Chuanzheng Gong, Feng Gao, Junyu Dong et al. · arXiv:2611.03069
    Abstract

    Multi-source remote sensing data classification has received growing attention recently. However, existing methods still struggle to jointly model long-range contextual dependencies and shared spatial patterns across heterogeneous modalities. To address this issue, we propose a Shared-Pattern Collaborative Network (SPCNet) for HSI and SAR/LiDAR joint classification. To enhance cross-modal collaborative learning, Shared-Pattern Collaborative Block (SPCB) is designed by introducing a shared large-kernel operator and a dynamic convolution branch, enabling the network to capture common spatial structures and content-adaptive local features. Furthermore, Region-Adaptive Modality Allocator (RAMA) is developed to assign spatially varying modality weights for reliable feature fusion. Experiments on Houston2013, Augsburg, and Houston2018 datasets demonstrate that SPCNet achieves superior classification performance over several state-of-the-art methods. The code will be made publicly available at this https URL

  • From Concepts to Actual Causes: Toward Closing the Concept-Malfunction Gap in Robot Foundation Models RO/AI
    Gihyuk Ko · arXiv:2611.00155
    Abstract

    Post-incident analysis of a robot foundation model poses a singular causal question: which internal components caused this failure, and to what degree? Mechanistic interpretability (MI), however, establishes general causal claims by decoding the concepts a policy has learned, yet knowing which concepts are encoded is insufficient for identifying the actual cause of a specific incident. In this position paper, we analyze this concept-malfunction gap by posing fault isolation in the framework of actual causality. Specifically, we formalize a fault-isolation query as a tuple of intervened internal components, counterfactual values, contingencies, and read-outs, and lay out conditions for it to be well-specified. Through this lens, we identify shortcomings of current MI approaches: they assume fixed contingencies and read out only immediate effects, letting backups obscure actual causes. We demonstrate on a toy example that these shortcomings can lead to conflicting verdicts. Lastly, based on the well-specification conditions, we outline four open research tasks toward closing the gap.

  • WorldSketch: Sketching World Models for Zero-Shot Mobile Manipulation RO
    Jiaming Wang · arXiv:2611.00513
    Abstract

    Most agentic robotic systems use vision-language models (VLMs) either to orchestrate predefined skills or to directly generate actions, constraints, and control programs. The former is limited by the available skill library, while the latter requires VLMs to reason about precise geometry and contact. In this paper, we propose WorldSketch, a new paradigm for agentic robotics in which a VLM constructs a world model rather than generating robot actions. The VLM acts as an agent that actively queries perception tools to build and visually validate a minimal kinematic world model, capturing only task-relevant objects, their interaction geometry, and possible motions. A geometric planner then jointly optimizes contact and whole-body motion on the resulting model. Moreover, the world model is self-improving: execution feedback verifies outcomes, updates uncertain hypotheses, and enables the agent to revise the model when its predictions are contradicted. We demonstrate the generality of this paradigm across RoboCasa, LIBERO-PRO, and R2R-CE navigation, achieving zero-shot success rates of 91%, 97%, and 76%, respectively. On RoboCasa, WorldSketch outperforms the next-best zero-shot agent baseline by 58 percentage points while using 10 times fewer VLM input tokens; on LIBERO-PRO, it exceeds CaP-X by 82 percentage points. In navigation, it matches the success rate of step-by-step VLM agents while using 6 times fewer input tokens.

  • Behavioral Steering of Vision-Language-Action Models in the Low-Parameter Regime RO
    James Tcheng, Feiyu Gavin Zhu · arXiv:2611.00522
    Abstract

    Reinforcement learning (RL) is increasingly used to adapt pretrained vision-language-action models (VLAs). We investigate the problem of behavioral steering: adapting a VLA's execution style to preferences such as carrying objects higher. To investigate how little trainable capacity such steering requires and how much pretrained capability can be retained, we freeze an OpenVLA-OFT 7B policy and optimize TinyLoRA adapters with 8 to 384 trainable parameters using either policy-gradient RL (GRPO) or evolutionary search (CMA-ES). With just 256 trainable parameters, we raise carry height by 120 mm on trained tasks at 92% success and by 74 mm on a held-out task at 80% success. By rescaling the learned update, we can tune the trade-off between style gains and capability retention without retraining. Increasing adapter capacity generally improves this trade-off, retaining more pretrained capability at comparable style gains. The choice of optimizer also depends on capacity, with CMA-ES being competitive at low parameter counts, while GRPO offers better trade-offs as capacity grows. These findings show that extremely low-dimensional updates can steer pretrained VLA behavior, with adapter capacity, optimizer choice, and update scaling offering practical control over adaptation and retention.

  • Roz: A Taste Test for World Model Latents RO
    Kenneth Ge, Dhruv Sheth, Kunvar Thaman · arXiv:2611.00612
    Abstract

    State of the art latent dynamics world models achieve impressive performance for robot planning. But do they actually encode the physics of the world? In this paper, we show that popular world models today achieve strong results on tasks that reduce to image similarity but forget key details about objects in the scene like mass, deformation, friction, and bounciness. We achieve this by defining precisely what ought to be encoded in such a world model and introducing two new evaluation methods that can directly tease physical properties and easily scale to new ones. Our evaluation, which we call Roz, includes 10 contrastive tasks and 3 continuous tasks, totaling 1734 individual scenarios. We validate our eval on control tasks. On our own model, AnonWM, we find that performance roughly improves with training steps, while not necessarily being correlated with reconstruction MSE. We argue current model failures are a symptom of evaluation strategies (probes and planners) that are opaque, model-specific, and high-compute. Roz requires only a model that can ingest still frames or video, a way to extract latents, and a distance function between them, and it can be run in one pass. We hope future evaluations will adopt Roz's evaluation techniques. More broadly, we hope Roz can be a case study for a future where benchmarks allow rapid iteration and comparison across models.

  • Learning from an Imitable Teacher: Competition Solution for the MetaFine Challenge RO/AI
    Yanze Zhang, Hao Liu, Haochang Hao, Zhixiang Shen et al. · arXiv:2611.00615
    Abstract

    The MetaFine Open Competition (RoboFineMani workshop, CoRL 2026) evaluates fine-grained manipulation beyond binary success. Its five simulated tasks, from grasping a bottle by an instructed part to inserting letter-shaped pegs with 4 mm clearance, are scored on perception under lighting and camera changes, instruction understanding, and behavior. This report presents Team Friday's solution, which got second place with an overall score of 0.947, more than twice the $\pi_0$ baseline. Instead of fine-tuning a large vision-language-action (VLA) model, we train one 19M-parameter policy per task with a separate instruction classifier and focus on the data, all generated in the simulator by a privileged scripted teacher, including its corrections of the student's mistakes. By analyzing failed episodes, we find that a teacher that almost always succeeds can still teach a memoryless student badly: its pauses become stalls, its fixed habits become shortcuts, and it never shows the errors the student makes. Making the teacher \emph{imitable} raised the official score on letter insertion, the hardest task, from 0.44 to 0.82 with 32\% more data and the same architecture, and finally to 0.88. We summarize practical lessons on demonstration data and evaluation, which we hope will help in imitation learning for precise manipulation.

  • STEER: Route-Aware Adaptive Reasoning for Autonomous Driving RO
    Yangang Zou, Pei Liu, Nan Song, Bozhou Zhang et al. · arXiv:2611.00718
    Abstract

    Vision-language action (VLA) models excel for end-to-end autonomous driving, yet their tendency to over-invoke chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning mirrors a human cognitive pitfall: overthinking. Just as deliberate reasoning can slow or mislead human judgment in routine tasks, excessive CoT in VLA incurs unnecessary compute overhead and can paradoxically degrade planning performance. Recent research tackles this via adaptive reasoning, which dynamically modulates inference complexity by selectively triggering CoT for complex scenes while responding directly to routine ones. While prior adaptive methods rely on implicit adaptation, recent work shows that explicitly optimizing the route between CoT and direct responses yields superior performance, assuming scene-difficulty annotations which are labor-intensive, poorly scalable, and prone to error due to the subjectivity of judging driving complexity. We explore, for the first time, whether adaptive reasoning with explicit route optimization can be achieved without any scene-difficulty labeling. We introduce STEER, a generic over-reasoning mitigation strategy featuring two key components: (i) routing uncertainty-aware rollout, which calibrates sampling to align routing diversity with model uncertainty; and (ii) cross-route advantage credit assignment, which introduces a differential metric to reinforce optimal routing decisions based on environmental rewards rather than subjective labels. Extensive experiments on NAVSIM v1/v2 demonstrate that STEER is competitive with state-of-the-art methods in both planning quality and the Pareto front of inference efficiency.

  • pico-WAM: Evaluating Counterfactual Prediction and Planning Error in a Compact World Action Model RO
    Keivalya Pandya · arXiv:2611.01039
    Abstract

    When a World Action Model fails at control, is the bottleneck proposal generation or trajectory scoring? We introduce pico-WAM, a compact model with supplied physical state for tracing policy-dynamics interactions. Using 206 Push-T demonstrations and 15 training seeds, we compare data regimes and search configurations. Counterfactual branch training improves short-horizon cost ranking and enlarges the coverage advantage of policy-restarted over warm-started search by 4.8 percentage points; restart wins under both data regimes. Replacing learned trajectory scores with simulator costs improves coverage by 12.2 points while retaining learned proposals. Substituting simulator states in proposal generation alone shows no detected gain. Component diagnostics and a separate door-opening study show why better ranking need not yield better control or outperform imitation. These controlled interventions identify scoring as an effective improvement target in the tested setting and provide a reproducible protocol for diagnosing compact world models.

  • SCOUT: Planning under Occlusion via Object-Centric World Model Rollouts RO/AI/CV
    Amir Hossain Raj, Xuesu Xiao · arXiv:2611.01600
    Abstract

    Robots manipulating in cluttered spaces must often plan from partial views where foreground objects occlude the target, surrounding geometry, and usable free space. Reactive policies often miss these hidden constraints, while exhaustive search becomes computationally prohibitive in dense clutter. We present SCOUT, an occlusion-aware planning framework that bridges high-level deliberation with grounded physical rollouts. SCOUT leverages an object-centric scene representation to reconstruct hidden geometry and derive a relational planning state capturing blocker hierarchies and accessibility corridors. To navigate the vast action space in clutter, an LLM proposes structured action candidates which are then rigorously evaluated via Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) using a learned action-conditioned world model. Evaluations on 'Reveal' and 'Placement' tasks show that SCOUT outperforms Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policies, embodied agents, and 3D world models, with significant gains in long-horizon tasks requiring reasoning over occluded geometry. We further validate the framework's robustness through real-world robotic manipulation in a cluttered shelf environment.

  • ODE Policy: Robot Learning via Action Dynamics, Not Actions RO
    Jindou Jia, Jingliang Li, Kuangji Zuo, Tuo An et al. · arXiv:2611.01729
    Abstract

    Most robot policies, whether trained by imitation learning (IL) or reinforcement learning (RL), map observations to action values at discrete control steps and leave the continuity of physical motion to be learned implicitly. We propose ODE policy, which learns the dynamics of the action rather than the action itself. A neural ordinary differential equation (ODE) learns the vector field of the action by matching the integrated actions to the targets over short action chunks in IL and single control steps in RL. A lightweight diversity head captures multi-modal behavior by randomizing the direction of the field. At deployment, each action is integrated from the previous one, so the executed trajectory is continuous by construction. We evaluate it as a visuomotor policy, as the action head of vision-language-action (VLA) and world action models (WAMs), and as the actor of model-based RL against 14 baselines on unimanual, bimanual, dexterous, locomotion, and loco-manipulation tasks. In IL, against flow matching, it reaches 5.9$\times$ the success, 5.4$\times$ the generalization, and 3.9$\times$ the mode balance after one epoch, and generates actions 10.8$\times$ faster. It mostly moves more smoothly than human demonstrations. On 14 real-world tasks across single-arm, dual-arm, dexterous, and humanoid robots, it achieves the highest success rate. In RL, it further speeds up SuperMimic for humanoid motion tracking and smooths its actions. Efficient in learning, data, parameters, and execution, ODE policy offers a promising path to scaling robot learning.

  • When Delay Meets Drift: Physical Safety of Robot Foundation Models Is a Cross-Layer Problem RO/CV
    Liangkai Liu, Bimal Thapa Magar · arXiv:2611.02101
    Abstract

    Robot foundation models now act in a closed loop, yet their safety is evaluated one variation at a time: robustness benchmarks perturb the scene while assuming instantaneous inference, and real-time execution methods study inference delay in nominal scenes. We argue that physical safety is not a property of the model alone, but emerges from how variations in the physical environment, the model, the computing platform, and execution interact in closed loop. We formalize this as cross-layer variation propagation across these system layers and use an interaction contrast to measure when joint variation creates more risk than the sum of its parts. In 4,000 paired closed-loop episodes of the pi0.5 vision-language-action model on LIBERO, a 400 ms inference delay raises the safety-violation rate by 3.5 percentage points under the training viewpoint but by 37 percentage points under a 60-degree camera shift: 33.5 percentage points (95% CI: 25-43) more than the two effects combined. We close with a research agenda toward predictable physical intelligence: predicting when variation becomes risk, and adapting compute, power, and control accordingly.

  • Neuro Inspired Robotics: What Robot Learning Can Borrow from the Sensorimotor Brain RO/AI
    Ruihan Zhang, Davy Deng · arXiv:2611.02510
    Abstract

    Robot policies still fail at everyday manipulation: an unfamiliar task, an object never held before, a scene that changes mid-motion, a skill that should improve with use. They fail despite pretraining on over a million trajectories, pooled from dozens of robots at great expense, while a child acquires all of these abilities within a few years of learning from a single body. This survey argues that the gap is one of design, and proposes designs for the next generation of robot models, drawn from how the nervous system solves the same problems. From the body we take compliance: the policy commands stiffness alongside position, so the controller yields where the task allows and presses where it must, which greatly lowers the demand for precise position output. From the visual system, where a fast pathway for action runs in parallel with the slow pathway for recognition, we take a dedicated fast vision system for motor behavior: it precomputes the quantities the policy needs and drives a reactive policy at camera rate. From the cerebellum we take a predictor beside the policy that forecasts the consequence of each command and recalibrates from its own error. From motor cortex we take a single model that composes action units from primitives, verifies each at a sensory boundary, practices the weakest unit with a hypothesis in mind, and reasons each attempt closer to the goal. Proven primitives are then consolidated from code into weights, so they run fluently and respond quickly the next time. Together these describe a robot that acts on its own initiative, the basis of deployment and recursive self-improvement, and we offer each proposal as a hypothesis to be tested.

  • Right Ranking, Wrong Scale: Reproducing the Robotic World Model's Dynamics Model and Testing the Uncertainty of Its Follow-up RO
    Joyjeet Singh · arXiv:2611.02699
    Abstract

    We reimplement the dynamics model of the Robotic World Model (RWM) and test the uncertainty estimate that its follow-up, RWM-U, uses to penalize reward in offline policy optimization. Our implementation matches the released model's gradients. On held-out data, training on the model's own rollouts beats one-step training at the preregistered long horizon for all ten episodes, but loses on short one-step windows. RWM-U's ensemble disagreement ranks rollout errors better than forecast step (Spearman rho = 0.90 at 100 steps), yet its spread substantially understates error: one-sigma coverage on the released checkpoint is 4.6% at 100 steps, versus 68% for a calibrated Gaussian. Rolling ensemble members on their own predictions raises coverage to 33%. The implemented squared-error loss on sampled predictions drives each member's variance toward zero. A horizon-specific rescaling restores one-sigma coverage under leave-one-episode-out evaluation, but not two-sigma coverage. We also identify two defects in the released evaluation pipeline. The study covers one robot, one gait and one dataset; it trains no policy.

  • Beyond Policy Patches: Distilling RL Specialists into Vision-Language-Action Policies RO
    Zhennan Jiang, Tingying Xie, Shilong Ji, Shu'ang Yu et al. · arXiv:2611.02777
    Abstract

    Vision-language-action (VLA) policies can handle noncritical stages of long-horizon manipulation yet fail during short precision-critical phases. Lightweight reinforcement learning (RL) specialists can address these failures without directly applying RL to the full VLA, but retain auxiliary controllers and switching mechanisms at deployment. We study how to transfer their capabilities into a single flow-matching VLA. Direct imitation of specialist rollouts can reproduce action jitter and leave the VLA unprepared for states encountered during its own execution. We introduce REPAIR, a value-guided distillation framework that combines offline initialization with on-policy refinement. At each critical-phase observation, REPAIR queries the corresponding specialist for multiple action candidates and weights them using its critic to construct a supervision distribution. A shared weighted flow-matching objective trains the VLA first on observations from specialist-assisted rollouts, then on observations collected through its own execution. Specialists provide supervision without taking control during on-policy collection and are removed at deployment. Across six real-robot tasks with 30 evaluation trials per task, REPAIR achieves 100% success on all six tasks, with lower mean end-effector jerk than specialist-assisted execution on every task.

  • An intelligent satellite must do nothing well RO
    Fabrizio Pilone, Giordana Bucchioni · arXiv:2611.02922
    Abstract

    Autonomous inspection of a resident space object is usually posed as a contest between classical next-best-view planning and reinforcement learning. We argue that the choice of algorithm is the least informative of the design axes. An inspector spacecraft has no rest state: when it stops thrusting, orbital dynamics carry it along a natural trajectory that can be used to observe the target at no fuel cost. On a controlled ladder in which one design axis is varied at a time, fuel efficiency turns out to be set by three things: whether the agent is allowed to drift, whether its state records where drifting has taken it, and whether its planning horizon is long enough for the benefit to appear. Removing any one of them doubles the fuel bill or more. The algorithm still matters, but through what it can represent: a model-based agent whose learned world model ignores fuel spends several times more of it. Finally, no learned policy is safe by construction, and a control barrier function filter beneath the decision layer removed every keep-out-zone violation in two thousand evaluation episodes.

  • KlaskTron-IL: A Contact-Rich Adversarial Imitation Learning Benchmark with a Validated Sim-to-Real Bridge RO

    Abstract

    KlaskTron is an open-hardware physical platform for two-player adversarial reinforcement learning, with a learned actuator model that supports zero-shot sim-to-real transfer of trained policies.   • Toward Uncertainty-Aware, Physics-Grounded Human World Models RO
    Yufei Zhang, Zijun Cui · arXiv:2611.03428
    Abstract

    A robot that acts around people needs a world model of the human: a belief over the person's body state and a dynamics model that predicts where that state goes next. This abstract argues that two published components form exactly these two halves, and specifies how to couple them. KNOWN [1] reconstructs the 3D human body while estimating both aleatoric and epistemic uncertainty in real time (11.6 ms per frame); its aleatoric term identifies unreliable observations, its epistemic term identifies inputs from low-density regions of the training data, and refinement driven by the epistemic term reduces the error on such inputs from 81.3 to 70.3 mm. PhysMoP [2] predicts future body configuration by solving the Euler-Lagrange equations of the body with learned dynamics terms, reducing the 80 ms prediction error from 9.6 to 2.1 mm on Human3.6M and from 12.1 to 0.7 mm on 3DPW. We define the interface between the two, propose three mechanisms by which KNOWN's uncertainty enters PhysMoP, and specify an evaluation protocol on in-the-wild video. No new experimental results are reported here.

  • When Robots Outweigh Objects: How Inertia Mismatch Shapes Robot Policy Learning RO
    Younghyo Park, Antonia Bronars, Pulkit Agrawal · arXiv:2611.03501
    Abstract

    Conventional robot arms can be extremely heavy compared with the everyday objects they manipulate, creating a significant inertia mismatch at contact. Even a small accidental bump from a heavy arm can knock a light object out of place. Modern learning methods have nevertheless demonstrated a wide range of successful manipulation behaviors on these platforms, suggesting that even extreme inertia mismatch can be overcome with sufficient data. However, it remains unclear whether high inertia imposes a hidden cost on learning, potentially worsening scaling with data or lowering the performance ceiling of modern robot learning methods. We investigate this question through controlled simulation experiments that vary robot and object masses. We find that reinforcement learning succeeds across a broad range of mass combinations, while behavior cloning learns more reliable policies when a lighter robot manipulates a heavier object, and more data narrows the resulting gap without closing it. Behavior cloning likewise degrades on a simulated robot arm as its motors reflect more inertia through their gearing, even though its weight stays the same. Finally, because new hardware is rarely an option, we explore ways to lower contact inertia on the arm one already has, either by choosing a lower-inertia workspace before collecting data or by steering the demonstrator toward lower-inertia solutions of the task. In both simulated cases, the lower-inertia demonstrations are imitated more reliably. Project website: this https URL

  • CalVLA: Shaping Native Confidence in Vision-Language-Action Policies with Outcome Supervision AI/RO
    Boniface Chukwuemeka Godwin · arXiv:2611.00394
    Abstract

    Can a vision-language-action (VLA) policy signal likely task failure from its own action probabilities?   • Scene-Adaptive Readout and Decoding in Diffusion Driving VLAs CV/RO/AI
    Seungjun Yu, Jiho Choi, Inha Kang, Hojun Choi et al. · arXiv:2611.02076
    Abstract

    Diffusion driving vision-language-action models repeatedly execute the backbone at every denoising stage, incurring substantial inference cost, while it remains unclear whether all scenes require the same decoding computation. To distinguish when a trajectory becomes readable from how much further refinement it requires, we analyze trajectory readout across network depth and decoding progress. We identify a depth-decoding dissociation in which late-layer representations already support useful trajectory readout after the first forward sweep, whereas early-layer representations benefit more from continued decoding. We further find that both the preferred readout layer and decoding depth vary across scenes. Based on these observations, we propose a scene-adaptive readout and decoding framework. A scene-conditioned readout MoE adaptively weights and combines trajectory predictions from layer-specific late-layer representations after a single backbone sweep, while an adaptive decoding policy applies additional native refinement only when required by the scene. We evaluate our framework on two diffusion driving VLAs across the Waymo E2E and nuScenes benchmarks. Our method improves validation RFS by up to 0.56 while also improving test performance, and achieves an end-to-end speedup of up to $6.0\times$ on Waymo E2E, while consistently reducing trajectory error on nuScenes. These results demonstrate that scene-level readout and decoding allocation improves planning quality while avoiding unnecessary decoding. Our code is available at this https URL

  • X-World 1.6: Building Controllable Virtual Cities with World Models AI
    XPeng, Boyang Wang, Boyi Sun, Chaoda Zheng et al. · arXiv:2611.00596
    Abstract

    Deploying generative world models for end-to-end closed-loop evaluation and policy learning requires precise scenario control, consistent observations, and sustained generation quality during extended interactions. We also introduce a dedicated evaluation suite to support systematic assessment and guide model iteration. Together, these upgrades expand the scenarios X-World can simulate and improve the efficiency and reliability of its generated observations, supporting broader use in autonomous-driving development.

  • Editing the Goal: Per-Token SAE Steering and Its Depth Limits in $π_{0.5}$ AI
    Rocio Perales Valdes, Animesh Garg · arXiv:2611.00602
    Abstract

    Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) have recovered interpretable, causally relevant features in robot foundation models (RFMs) such as $\pi_{0.5}$ (arXiv:2603.19183, arXiv:2603.19233). SAE features on individual instruction tokens are less interpretable than timestep-pooled ones (arXiv:2603.19183), and whether they can redirect a robot's goal remains untested. Adding a fixed difference-in-means vector at twice its natural magnitude redirects 8-10/10 rollouts.   • Robust Reward Machine Learning and Execution in Noisy Environments AI
    Roko Parac, Lorenzo Nodari, Daniel Furelos-Blanco, Leo Ardon et al. · arXiv:2611.01063
    Abstract

    Background: Reward machines (RMs) represent non-Markovian reward functions as finite-state machines over high-level propositional events. Traditionally, these events are generated by a deterministic labelling function that maps environment states to proposition sets. However, real-world labelling functions are inherently noisy. Objectives: Design and implement a method that learns RMs in the presence of noisy labelling functions and exploits them for policy learning in both discrete and continuous environments. Methods: We propose Prob-IRM, a method that learns RMs from noisy proposition histories using inductive logic programming (ILP) and exploits them for policy learning. Noisy proposition histories are represented as training examples for ILP, which learns an RM that maximises coverage of low-uncertainty histories. RM learning is interleaved with policy optimisation. Prob-IRM uses a belief over RM states to determine when to relearn the RM and to provide additional guiding rewards. Results: Prob-IRM is evaluated on environments with discrete and continuous state spaces under varying levels of labelling noise. Prob-IRM performs comparably to baselines using handcrafted RMs and outperforms approaches that use recurrent neural networks to encode history, despite operating with noisy labels. Conclusions: Prob-IRM represents a step towards deploying reward machines in noisy, real-world environments.

  • Xiaomi Auto World Model 2.0: Causal World Models for Real-World Driving and Embodied Intelligence AI
    Zhenxin Zhu, Zhijian Huang, Cheng Chi, Yuru Wang et al. · arXiv:2611.01677
    Abstract

    We present Xiaomi Auto World Model 2.0, a unified streaming causal world model for Physical AI. The architecture integrates View-Temporal Attention (VTA) to capture cross-view spatial dependencies and temporal dynamics, together with Multi-view Rotary Position Encoding (RoPE) to encode positional relationships across views and time. Conditioning inputs are fused through VAE latent concatenation in a shared representation space. We incorporate Gated Linear Attention (GLA) into the temporal layers of VTA for efficient streaming inference, reducing computation and KV-cache memory during multi-view rollouts.   • RubricWorld: World Model-Guided Rubric Optimization for LLM Graders AI
    Hongji Pu, Hang Li, Yucheng Chu, Jiliang Tang · arXiv:2611.01986
    Abstract

    Rubric optimization improves LLM grading by revising scoring instructions while keeping the grader's parameters fixed. Existing optimizers often judge edits by aggregate validation performance, limiting the supervision they retain about which errors an edit corrects or introduces. Testing each candidate also requires rescoring validation answers, which limits how many edits can be explored within a fixed budget. We propose RubricWorld, a framework that learns from previous edit evaluations to guide further search. Its world model predicts an edit's correction and degradation rates at score boundaries, conditioned on the current rubric state. The training signal comes from paired grading results: scores for the same answers before and after an edit. Predictions select one candidate for direct evaluation, and held-out confidence bounds determine whether it corrects enough target errors while keeping new errors within prespecified limits. Measured effects update the model even when the edit is rejected. Across EIR (National), ASAP2.0, and ASAP-SAS with three frozen LLM graders, RubricWorld achieves the highest score in 20 of 27 metric comparisons. On ASAP2.0, it raises gpt-5-mini accuracy from 18.20% with the original rubric to 46.70%, exceeding the strongest baseline, APO, by 22.60 percentage points. code will be released upon acceptance.

  • Creating Hybrid Interactive Narrative Systems with LLM: From Formal Description to Evaluation AI
    Jules Clerc, Domitile Lourdeaux, Mohamed Sallak, Marc Ravaine · arXiv:2611.02807
    Abstract

    Interactive narratives let users shape a story while an Experience Manager (EM) keeps it coherent, balancing player freedom against authorial control. Large Language Models now enable a new generation of hybrid EMs, which combine run-time generation from free-text input with symbolic narrative control. However, their heterogeneity makes them hard to compare: A manager's behaviour is generally tied to the unique and specific scenario in which it operates, and much of its decision-making takes place outside of any explicit and verifiable structure. We address this with an LLM-based narrative platform whose managers are interchangeable modules operating over a shared symbolic world model of facts, operations, and goals.   • Old Is Not False: Valid-Time Memory for Interactive Video World Models CV/AI
    Ziming Wang, Chuyue Gong, Hongliang Cao, Jingmin Zhu et al. · arXiv:2611.00758
    Abstract

    Interactive video world models draw each frame from a memory bank of earlier frames stamped with their capture times. With frames, weights and noise fixed, reversing the timestamps of two views flips the state that generators of two families draw: they take the newest timestamp as the truth. That reading is right as long as states never return. After an agent undoes an action, the newest view of a region is outdated and the only view of the present is older; memories for changing worlds delete that older view, and memories that keep it hand the generator the past. Temporal databases separate when a record is stored from when its content holds, its valid time. ReDate keeps every frame and dates each retrieved token by its valid time, read from the agent's action log. The frozen generator then reads a reverted region as one that has just reached a new state, so ReDate needs no training. We prove that trusting the newest timestamp is right exactly when every current view is dated after every outdated one, that without a view taken after the return only the action log tells a reverted region from an up-to-date one, and that a generator able to draw from its own prediction gains more from re-dating than from deletion when the moved tokens keep little weight. After an undo, ReDate raises the share of FAR's steps showing the current state from 0.36 to 0.62 and HY-WorldPlay's from 0.003 to 0.19. We also propose a calibrated state metric to test any memory after undos.

  • World Models Need Not Be Right to Rank Policies Right: Relative Bounds and Audit Limits AI
    Ahanaf Hasan Ariq, Hasaan Ahmad · arXiv:2611.01139
    Abstract

    Selecting a reinforcement-learning (RL) policy usually requires additional environment interaction, while learned world models offer cheap but potentially biased evaluations. This raises two distinct questions: can shared model errors preserve pairwise rankings despite poor absolute values, and do they make a small number of real audits more informative? We derive an exact pairwise simulation identity and a relative bound separating common-mode Bellman residuals from policy-specific error. The analysis motivates RAVEL (Relative Auditing of Value Errors in Learned models), a selective policy tournament whose eliminations use held-out real outcomes. We test 45 SAC, TD3, and PPO policies per task in DMC cheetah-run and walker-walk. At rollout horizon 500, model rankings remain strong (Kendall tau = 0.962 and 0.800), and same-run differential-error ratios are 0.674 and 0.106. Yet mean paired audit-error correlations are only 0.007 and 0.090, and pairing changes standard deviation by -2.5 percent and +1.9 percent. Simultaneous intervals attain 99.98 percent and 99.75 percent coverage, but certify only 13.2 percent and 0 percent of decisions at eight audits. RAVEL improves low-budget walker-walk regret, whereas real-only successive halving is better at the largest budget, rejecting the precommitted twofold efficiency target. Frozen-model optimization lowers real return without increasing predicted return on average. Thus ranking fidelity, useful audit covariance, and upward model exploitation are distinct properties, separated in part by closed-loop trajectory divergence.

  • AgiWorld: An Agentic Video World Model for Building Persistent Worlds CV/AI
    Shaoheng Fang, Yiqun Mei, Hanwen Jiang, Xin Sun et al. · arXiv:2611.02341
    Abstract

    Recent interactive video world models generate visually compelling videos from a single image, following user-provided camera motion and text prompts. However, such low-level control demands substantial user effort to realize a high-level intent, and the generated world struggles to remain persistent as the exploration proceeds. In this work, we present AgiWorld, an agentic video world model that explores and builds a persistent world from a single image and a free-form instruction. For high-level planning, our agent AgiWorld-Agent designs the world and the journey through it in a world state and directs the exploration. For low-level realization, our video world model AgiWorld-WM generates the video chunk by chunk and stores it with its geometry in a spatial memory, from which the agent updates its world state. This closed loop keeps the world faithful to the user's intent and keeps the agent's world state consistent with the generated content throughout the exploration. We co-design the two levels around the shared spatial memory and an interface of metric camera trajectories and structured prompts. In AgiWorld-WM, to handle occlusion and accumulated geometric errors in the spatial memory over long rollouts, we propose occlusion-aware history selection, which retrieves past views by surface visibility, and reliability-based memory maintenance, which prefers reliable geometry and earlier observations. AgiWorld-WM achieves state-of-the-art overall performance on world revisiting and exploration benchmarks, and qualitative results and ablations show that AgiWorld builds persistent worlds from free-form instructions over minute-long journeys. Persistence thus arises not from the video model alone but from its closed loop with the agent.

  • Training-Time Domain Transfer for Generator-Free Diagnostics on Novel Modalities in Computational Pathology CV/AI
    Julia A. Mielcarz, Andrea Camilloni, Nita Mulliqi, Jessica A. Frank et al. · arXiv:2611.02931
    Abstract

    Novel imaging modalities promise improved diagnostics, but AI development is constrained by scarce training data. Inference-time domain transfer can address this by mapping novel modalities to established ones, enabling reuse of existing diagnostic models. However, this introduces hallucination risks, computational overhead, and regulatory complexity. Inspired by sim-to-real transfer, we propose training-time domain transfer, generating synthetic target-domain training data from archived routine samples to enable direct diagnosis on real inputs without inference-time generation.   • FORCE: Factorized Object Relations for Counterfactual Editing in Video World Models CV
    Suraj K. Sood, Yugyung Lee · arXiv:2611.00023
    Abstract

    World models predict how scenes evolve, but they are difficult to interrogate: given a model that forecasts a video, one cannot easily ask what would have happened had a particular interaction between two objects been different. We present Factorized Object Relations for Counterfactual Editing (FORCE), a world model that infers pairwise interactions from raw video without supervision and represents each as an explicit latent per object pair, so that a relation can be removed, changed, transferred or introduced directly. Objects are extracted by slot attention over frozen self-supervised features; a relation encoder infers each pair latent from a short observation window, and an interaction head turns it into a force. The latent is held fixed across the rollout, so an edit persists rather than being recomputed, and every edit acts on the model's own quantities with no mapping to simulator parameters. We evaluate in a simulated environment where the true outcome of an edit is available, scoring the change each edit induces against the change the simulator produces under the same operation. On held-out clips, FORCE's edits made from video agree with the simulator's response at 0.19 to 0.37 on our effect-based score; trained and scored on exact object positions instead, the same architecture reaches 0.57 to 0.85. The gap therefore lies not in the learned relational dynamics but in perception: what bounds an edit is how precisely objects can be located from video, and the precision editing needs lies just beyond what current object-centric perception delivers. Closing that gap is a concrete target for making counterfactual world models work from video.

  • Spoofing the Latent State: Imperceptible Goal-Image Attacks Derail Planning in LeWorldModel CV
    Kunal Bhosikar, Nicole Meng, Charu Sharma, Yingjie Lao · arXiv:2611.00427
    Abstract

    Joint-embedding predictive architectures (JEPAs) have recently emerged as general-purpose visual world models for control. A prominent example is LeWorldModel (LeWM), which trains end-to-end from pixels using an isotropic Gaussian anti-collapse regularizer (SIGReg) and plans by minimizing the squared Euclidean distance between predicted and goal embeddings. In this work, we investigate the test-time robustness of this latent planning interface. Analyzing the learned metric on Push-T, we first find that task-relevant object state heavily dominates latent distance: shifting the block by a single pixel displaces the embedding $3\times$ further than an equivalent agent shift, and swapping the block pose alone accounts for $99\%$ of the distance between scene pairs. However, because this pose information is distributed across a curved, high-dimensional manifold rather than a fixed linear subspace, the encoder cannot be trivially edited post-hoc to isolate or redirect it. We next demonstrate that this geometry is highly vulnerable at test time. A white-box $\ell_\infty$ perturbation of just $\epsilon{=}4/255$ applied to a single input frame causes the encoder to register the block at an arbitrary, attacker-chosen pose in $73$--$95\%$ of scenes without disturbing the encoded agent state. Perturbing either the goal image or the live camera observation drops Push-T planning success from $87\%$ to $32\%$, whereas uniform random noise at twice the magnitude has negligible impact. This vulnerability persists across all four standard LeWM benchmarks (Push-T, Two-Room, Reacher, and OGBench-Cube). At $\epsilon{=}8/255$, the attack bridges $82$--$97\%$ of the squared latent distance to the target embedding in every environment, inducing planning degradation proportional to each task's reliance on the spoofed entity.

  • When Does Latent Prediction Error Support Physical Surprise Claims in Video World Models? CV
    Anton Hristov, Aleksandar Slavov, Vasil Vasilev · arXiv:2611.01815
    Abstract

    Prediction errors from video world models are often treated as physical surprise, but they can also reflect appearance, static content, motion, or ordinary prediction difficulty. We present an evaluation framework that separates what frozen representations contain, how they organize appearance and physical differences, and what changes a model's own prediction error. On IntPhys2, a benchmark of physically possible and impossible events, we compare representations from latent prediction, masked pixel reconstruction, and simple visual statistics. Appearance remains easy to decode, while V-JEPA2 exposes modest information about whether events are possible or impossible. Looking at the latent geometry, appearance changes become smaller compared with differences between videos, their directions line up more consistently across scenes, and their overlap with physical changes decreases in deeper layers. Across action-conditioned models, incorrect actions raise prediction error, while appearance changes also produce substantial responses. In Physion++ video clips, repeating a single frame can produce higher error than the original clip, while changing color throughout the clip can lower it. Using Kubric, a simulation and rendering framework, we find that impossible events can produce lower error than possible events. A fixed error threshold also incorrectly flags many possible events as impossible. A model can therefore represent physical information and respond to incorrect actions without its native prediction error reliably measuring physical surprise.

    Abstract

    Vision-Language Models (VLMs) and Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models process hundreds of visual tokens, making visual representations a major computational bottleneck. SVD-Prune reduces the visual token.   • INTERACT: Learning Interaction-Aware World Dynamics from Driving Videos for Autonomous Driving CV

    Abstract

    Safe autonomous driving requires more than recognizing the current scene: the ego vehicle must anticipate how surrounding traffic participants will evolve under its own motion.   • History Forcing: Distilling History-Dependent Continuations for Long-Horizon Video World Models CV
    Hangyi Kuang, Chengzhi Wang, Yefei He, Weijie Wang et al. · arXiv:2611.02782
    Abstract

    Few-step autoregressive video diffusion enables efficient generation, yet maintaining consistency over long rollouts remains challenging, especially when subjects or scenes reappear after going out of sight. Despite progress in fidelity and rollout robustness, existing distillation methods do not fully exploit richer historical information to constrain the student's current prediction. To address this gap, we introduce \textbf{History Forcing}, a unified on-policy distillation framework that strengthens historical supervision for few-step autoregressive video generators. The framework uses the residual between teacher predictions with and without informative historical context to decouple this supervision. Such a historical context can be obtained using earlier generated chunks with less accumulated drift or a fixed-view teacher rollout that keeps the scene visible while the student looks away. The residual is then weighted and added to the score or velocity prediction used for distillation, forming a corrected target. By aligning the student with this corrected target through distillation, History Forcing incorporates long-range historical constraints into autoregressive generation. Our method achieves state-of-the-art performance among 1.3B models, with a VBench quality score of 85.07 for 15s video generation and a VBench-Long aggregate score of 79.82 for 60s generation.   • Isekai3D SDG: A Platform for Diverse Synthetic Gameplay Visual Data CV
    Rafal Karp, Gene Liu, Shih-Hsin Li, Eric Courtemanche et al. · arXiv:2611.03626
    Abstract

    We present Isekai3D Synthetic Data Generation (SDG), a platform for simulating and capturing synthetic 3D gameplay for research in vision and world models, neural graphics, gameplay agents, and other applications. The platform combines reusable environment and character assets with Unreal Engine plugins in a two-stage workflow. First, world simulation generates gameplay while recording the world state, character movements, actions, and camera tracks needed for replay. Second, the recorded simulation is replayed to capture selected outputs, such as rendered video, images, and depth maps, at a specified resolution and frame rate under selected rendering and camera configurations.   • MirrorComm: A Communication-Efficient Cooperative Perception Framework via Mirrored World Models CV
    Shenghan Luo, Yin Xu, Cixiao Zhang, Yang Wang et al. · arXiv:2611.02405
    Abstract

    Cooperative perception (CP) requires substantial communication bandwidth to exchange sensing information among connected agents. A variety of compression techniques have been proposed to reduce transmission costs while retaining task-relevant information. However, most existing methods follow a frame-wise compression paradigm, overlooking temporal predictability and repeatedly transmitting information that could be inferred from historical context. To address these limitations, we propose MirrorComm, the first world model-based communication-efficient CP framework. Specifically, mirrored world models at the transmitter and receiver predict the transmitter's current observation from a shared history of its reconstructed observations.   • Scaling Trusted Autonomy: Towards Generally Capable Verifiable Robots RO
    Benedict Quartey · arXiv:2611.00158
    Abstract

    Robots powered by foundation models are increasingly able to interpret open-ended language, recognize unfamiliar objects, and operate across diverse environments. Yet broader capability does not establish that a robot has understood a request, that its actions will produce the intended effects, or that its internal representation still corresponds to reality. Classical symbolic planning provides explicit structure for composition and verification, but traditionally depends on hand-engineered skills and representations. Learned policies can broaden the robot's repertoire, but provide weak guarantees about what will happen and why. This thesis argues that scaling trusted autonomy requires a neurosymbolic approach that unifies the generality of learned models with symbolic structure for robust robot behavior. It explores three complementary directions. First, LIMP uses foundation models and spatial reasoning to ground natural-language instructions in explicit task constraints and instruction-specific semantic maps, enabling task-and-motion planning while exposing the robot's interpretation for inspection. Second, REALM maintains an editable, photorealistic 3D Gaussian scene, predicts the consequences of planned manipulation, and locally reconciles the scene so that subsequent instructions obtain their grounding and planning geometry from an updated state. Third, PACTS learns visuomotor skills that jointly generate action trajectories and predictions of their symbolic effects, supporting skill composition, progress monitoring, and recovery through replanning. Together, these contributions preserve explicit structure for reasoning and verification while broadening how it is obtained, providing a foundation for generally capable robots whose behavior can be assessed under the assumptions of their models.

  • SPARK: Self-Correcting Embodied Memory via Physical Re-Observation RO/AI
    Dmytro Kurdydyk, Aadi Palnitkar, Sebastian Scherer, Seungchan Kim · arXiv:2611.00344
    Abstract

    Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems have seen growing adoption in embodied settings as language-queryable memories of the environment. However, existing approaches often assume a reliable single-episode knowledge base (KB) or accumulate observations across episodes without correcting prior errors. Such errors can persist through incorrect semantic labels, inaccurate object positions, and inconsistent spatial relationships. To address this, we present SPARK, a corrective multi-episode framework for embodied RAG. After the first episode, a semantic critic flags regions of the KB, a targeted planner generates revisits, and a selective merge adds, removes, or replaces KB information based on the new observations. We evaluate SPARK on four outdoor AirSim maps under two degraded viewpoint conditions, using perception-built KBs, a visibility-constrained oracle used only for evaluation, and a fixed set of 158 embodied question answering (EQA) questions scored before and after correction. A single corrective episode improves downstream EQA on every map under both conditions while storing 13-41% fewer objects than append-only updating, with gains up to 2.7x on one map-condition pair. Extending correction through Episode 4 brings every map above the EQA performance of its undegraded reference. Ablations show that the critic's three signals each contribute to performance, that targeted revisits achieve a larger mean gain than untargeted ones under the same budget, and that selective merging achieves gains comparable to append-only updating with a smaller KB. Overall, SPARK demonstrates the value of correcting embodied memory through targeted re-observation, particularly as embodied systems scale and the risk of accumulated errors increases.

  • HALO-LP: A Homogeneous All-Shifted Non-Interior-Point Method for Linear Programming RO
    Jose Rojas, Ana Aguilar, Eusebio Bugarin · arXiv:2611.01038
    Abstract

    This letter introduces HALO-LP, a novel homogeneous all-shifted non-interior-point method for linear programming (LP). The proposed method combines a homogeneous self-dual (HSD) embedding with all-shifted nonlinear complementarity (NCP) functions and proximal primal-dual regularization, enabling arbitrary primal-dual warm starts without requiring strictly interior iterates. The resulting Newton equations admit a condensation based on a positive-definite matrix that can be efficiently factorized using Cholesky decomposition, while the HSD embedding enables primal and dual infeasibility detection through Farkas certificates. Numerical evaluations on perturbed LP instances derived from the Netlib test set demonstrate state-of-the-art warm-starting performance, outperforming the competing solvers in the evaluated setting. We further validate optimality and infeasibility detection on analytically characterized LP problems. Finally, we demonstrate HALO-LP's applicability to robotic optimal control through sequential linear programming (SLP) for centroidal walking and trotting of quadrupedal robots.

  • Back to the Future: Predicting Long-Horizon Dynamics with Retrospective Correction for Disturbance-Aware Retrieval RO
    Jiwoong Choi, Haoyu Li, Yiwei Lyu · arXiv:2611.02073
    Abstract

    Retrieving objects from densely packed storage requires anticipating how contact disturbs surrounding objects when limited clearance prevents lifting the target over clutter. However, contact can propagate beyond the target's immediate neighbors, while accumulated prediction errors make learned dynamics unreliable over long planning horizons. We introduce DARE (Disturbance-Aware REtrieval), a geometry-aware relational dynamics framework that uses retrospective correction to improve long-horizon prediction for disturbance-aware retrieval. DARE encodes compact object descriptors and predicts action-conditioned disturbance propagation through object-level message passing. When a sparse observation becomes available, a learned corrector uses the prediction discrepancy to amend an earlier predicted state and reruns the intervening dynamics, propagating the correction through the predicted contact chain. These predictions guide a receding-horizon planner that balances retrieval progress against displacement and rotation of surrounding objects, permitting necessary contact while discouraging harmful disturbance.
