  • When to Probe and When to Act: Clarity-Aware Risk-Adaptive Control for Interactive Driving RO
    Youran Wang, Devodita Chakravarty, Yiwei Lyu · arXiv:2611.02240
    Abstract

    Safe navigation in interactive traffic requires autonomous vehicles to reason about unobservable human characteristics and the reciprocal nature of multi-agent interactions. Traditional architectures often decouple prediction from planning, leading to the "Frozen Robot Problem" or unsafe maneuvers under high ambiguity because they treat risk assessment and information acquisition as independent objectives. We argue that the notion of safety should not remain static but must instead dynamically co-evolve with the system's internal confidence: the robot should actively clarify ambiguity when necessary while also recognizing the diminishing returns of information, and naturally transition toward task execution. We introduce a unified belief-space Model Predictive Control (MPC) framework that couples multistep information planning with a clarity-adaptive Conditional Value-at-Risk (CVaR) barrier mechanism. By treating clarity, a scalar informational state, as an explicit planning variable, the controller co-designs its probing strategy with its safety specification, tightening conservatism under high ambiguity and relaxing constraints as confidence improves. We further provide a performance analysis showing that the cost of information seeking and the expected planning-cost gap to a controller that knows the true human characteristic are bounded by residual ambiguity and solver error. We evaluate the framework in lane-merging and unsignalized-intersection scenarios against representative interactive-planning baselines. Across 5,000 simulations, the proposed method achieves the highest success rates (94.6% and 93.2%) and the lowest collision rates (1.2% and 1.4%) among the compared methods, providing a principled foundation for robust decision-making in human-centric environments.

  • Safe-Ballbot: Tilt-Consistent MPC with Control Barrier Functions for Ballbot Navigation RO
    Duc Cuong Vu, Tung Lam Nguyen · arXiv:2611.03307
    Abstract

    Ball-balancing robots (ballbots) must lean to accelerate, and the lean is limited by the wheel torques and by safety. Planners that use the full robot model are expensive, while planners that ignore the balance dynamics produce references that cannot be followed. This paper presents a two-layer navigation scheme for a three-omni-wheel ballbot. The upper layer is a model predictive controller on a \emph{jerk model} of the ball position, with discrete-time control barrier functions for static, moving, rotating and concave obstacles. The lower layer is a hierarchical sliding mode controller with an extended state observer and a robust exponential control barrier function on the tilt (HSMC-ESO-ECBF). The layers are linked by an acceleration bound derived in closed form from the largest steady tilt that the barrier function admits, so that the plan never asks for a lean the tracker refuses. For obstacles whose motion is only predicted, the obstacles are inflated by conformal prediction radii that bound the prediction error with a prescribed probability. In MuJoCo, with omni-wheel roller contacts and torque saturation, the scheme keeps at least $10$\,cm of clearance, tracks the plan within $2$\,cm RMS, keeps the tilt below the limit and plans in $9$--$12$\,ms, $10$--$15$ times faster than a full-model nonlinear MPC that enters the inflated obstacles; with predicted obstacles, the conformal inflation removes the collisions of a constant-velocity predictor in $30$ random crossing scenarios and reduces them in an endless run on a closed this http URL source code and video illustrations are available at this https URL

  • DriftingPlanner: Drifting Generative Models for One-Step Trajectory Planning in End-to-End Autonomous Driving CV/RO
    Ziming Liu, Yizhan Qu, Leichen Wang, Hao Sun · arXiv:2611.01283
    Abstract

    End-to-end planners decode the ego trajectory either by coordinate regression, which is fast but blends plausible maneuvers into one trajectory that may follow none of them, or by diffusion and flow matching, which model the trajectory distribution but need several inference steps. Drifting models move this iteration into training: a one-step generator learns to follow an attraction--repulsion field between generated and ground-truth samples. Drifting was developed for class-conditional image generation, where every class supplies many positive samples. In planning, every scene is its own condition. Pooling scenes would attract the generated trajectories of one scene to the ground truth of unrelated scenes, whereas computing the field per scene leaves a single positive, the expert trajectory. Mutual repulsion then holds the generated trajectories on a shell around it, so any single generated trajectory deviates from the ground truth. We present DriftingPlanner, a trajectory planner trained with the drifting loss alone, without any coordinate regression term. It computes the field per scene and removes the shell at inference: it decodes every noise vector together with its negation in one batched pass, and averaging each antithetic pair cancels the linear part of the displacement from the shell center. On NAVSIM navtest, DriftingPlanner reaches a state-of-the-art 93.75 PDMS, and with single-step inference its drifting decoder comes within 0.13 PDMS of the best diffusion or flow matching setting, which needs ten inference steps.

  • Learning to bid and bundle: a posterior-sampling agent for online retail AI
    Hadi Mohammadi · arXiv:2611.00165
    Abstract

    An online retailer launching a perishable product bids for sponsored-search clicks, discounts a bundle with an always-stocked staple and displays its remaining stock, which raises willingness to pay. It knows neither how clicks respond to its bid nor how strong and dispersed its market is, and learns both over repeated seasons from data its own decisions censor: a zero bid reveals nothing about paid clicks, and choices follow only clicks. We design a posterior-sampling agent for it. Each period it draws a market from an exact posterior and posts the bid and discount of that market's exactly solved season plan, so every action is one a fully informed seller could take. In a simulated online florist it has the lowest regret averaged over markets. In weak markets it gives up 12% to 23% of one season's revenue over 500 seasons, four to seven times less than drawing once per season. A planner acting on point estimates can stop bidding and never learn that bidding pays. The agent escapes after about three seasons on average, against fifty with one draw per season. Where displayed scarcity keeps willingness to pay above the posted price, the discount needs no learning. Customers waiting for the discount to return to its recent level look like lost sales, so frequent waiting makes the agent underrate its market. One discount per season removes waiting at a cost of 5.9% of clairvoyant revenue in a weak market and none in a strong one.

  • State-Aware Activation Recomputation with Replay Contracts and Selective Preservation AI
    Annan Zhang, Shengjie Song, Wenshuai Yao, Lihao Zhang et al. · arXiv:2611.00590
    Abstract

    Activation recomputation saves accelerator memory by replaying forward segments during the backward pass, but replay is only correct if it sees the state the original forward pass saw. Mutable buffers, aliased bytes and random-generator state can change in between, so re-executing unchanged code can differentiate a different computation. We make this state explicit. A versioned replay contract specifies what replay must restore, and a joint planner chooses, per segment, between activation retention, whole-buffer snapshots and selective-region preservation, charging each option its measured memory and recovery cost. Independent derivative and byte-level checks validate the contract, and a Torch/JAX cohort retains all 5,900 execution records, including matches, rejections and divergences. On frozen sparse-write H100 workloads, each measured over fifty fresh processes, joint planning reduces paired step time by 18.16% against a correctly costed eager/whole planner at a 44 GiB budget (approximate 97.5% ratio interval $[.8172,.8195]$). At 32 GiB, where the eager/whole planner has no feasible static policy, joint planning is 4.98% faster than fixed regions at 29.94 rather than 26.20 GiB. A complete CIFAR-100 dense-queue application runs seven methods for 100 encoder and 25 probe epochs at six training seeds, with byte-identical test scores within each seed. There, joint planning uses 22.41% more allocated memory than whole snapshots, with a process-time geometric-mean ratio of 1.0080 (seed range $[.9845,1.0191]$). Together, the results map where selective preservation pays off: sparse writes with large activations benefit, while in the dense-queue workload whole snapshots use less allocated memory.

  • \textsc{ChatDify}: Automating End-to-End Dify Workflow Synthesis from Natural Language via Multi-Agent Collaboration AI
    Tao Fan, Guoqiang Ma · arXiv:2611.01563
    Abstract

    The proliferation of Large Language Models has catalyzed powerful application development platforms like Dify, which enable users to build sophisticated LLM-powered workflows through modular node orchestration. However, manually configuring these workflows requires deep platform expertise and remains time-consuming and error-prone, creating a significant barrier for non-expert users. To address this challenge, we propose \textsc{ChatDify}, a novel multi-agent framework that automates end-to-end Dify workflow synthesis from natural language descriptions. \textsc{ChatDify} employs an Actor-Critic architecture comprising three specialized agents: the \textit{Planner Agent} that decomposes user requests into structured execution plans, the \textit{Executor Agent} that performs context-aware parameter synthesis and schema-augmented DSL compilation, and the \textit{Reviewer Agent} that ensures reliability through hybrid verification with closed-loop refinement. We evaluate \textsc{ChatDify} on a curated benchmark of 100 production-inspired tasks across three state-of-the-art LLM backends, achieving 0.96, 0.91, and 0.75 success rates with Qwen-3.7, GLM-5.1, and DeepSeek-v4, respectively.   • Fidelity Collapses with Table Size, Not Turn Count: Delivery Architecture in a Deployed Tool-Using Agent AI
    Tung Thanh Hoang · arXiv:2611.00442
    Abstract

    When a multi-turn agent delivers a tool's structured records into the model's context and re-emits them each turn, every turn can corrupt data the tool computed correctly. We measure this in a production award-travel agent: two architectures over identical frozen payloads, lineage-matched prompts and sampling, differing only in delivery architecture. SYNTHESIS feeds full record sets into the planner's context and answers follow-ups in-head; BYPASS, the deployed system, keeps records in session state, translates follow-ups into arguments for a deterministic filter, and delivers rows over a typed side channel. Across 24 dialogues (339 scored turns per condition), BYPASS scores 92.3% of turns fully correct, SYNTHESIS 45.7% (paired gap +46.6pp [+35.7, +57.1]). Varying delivered-table size by capture date, SYNTHESIS collapses from near-perfect at 10 rows or fewer to 8% or less beyond about 74 rows. On a content-controlled prefix ladder the collapse threshold orders by model capability, from under 10 rows (GPT-4o-mini) to about 198 or more (Gemini 2.5 Pro), while BYPASS stays size-flat for all four models. The collapse is expensive: beyond 100 rows, where almost no SYNTHESIS answer is correct, each correct one costs roughly 200x BYPASS's flat \$0.012. Practitioners converged on typed side-channel delivery for rendering, not correctness; we show it was, unknowingly, also a correctness decision.

  • Solver-Like Behavior in Large Language Models as Formalizers AI
    Rikhil Amonkar, Stuti Mohan, Kevin Nguyen, Ceyhun Efe Kayan et al. · arXiv:2611.02395
    Abstract

    In the LLM-as-formalizer paradigm, a large language model translates a problem into a formal program and leaves the search to a solver, gaining the solver's guarantees. Formalizers are now often reasoning models that write a long reasoning trace before the program, yet they are judged only by the program's output. We find that the trace routinely does the solver's job, assigning, testing and ruling out values until it often reaches the full answer; we call this solver-like behavior (SLB). We define an ontology of SLB over the trace, the program and its output, and use LLM judges, checked against human labels, to label 2,000 generations from three reasoning models on four constraint satisfaction datasets, each formalized in a declarative and an imperative formal language. SLB appears in 85% of traces and takes 24% of reasoning tokens, yet the program usually recomputes the answer, so the effort is redundant. The 9% of programs that inherit the trace's answer also inherit its errors, and pass output-based evaluation whenever that answer is correct. The amount of SLB is not associated with accuracy, and SLB is more common in imperative Python than in declarative Z3 or PDDL. Cautionary prompts reduce it by only a quarter, but hiding the problem's values behind placeholders, which the program receives only at run time, removes 97% of it without loss of accuracy. Formalizers should therefore be evaluated by how their programs are reached, not only by what they output.

  • Scientific Liquidity Agents: An Agent-Based Model of Knowledge Arbitrage in Scientific Communities AI
    Koutian Wu, You Liu, Yue Liu, Zhiping Li et al. · arXiv:2611.02539
    Abstract

    Scientific knowledge gains new uses when ideas, methods, and results move between research contexts. Understanding this process requires a model of both specialized knowledge production and the intermediary activities that connect it. We introduce Scientific Liquidity Agents (SLA), an agent-based model of knowledge arbitrage in scientific communities. SLA represents domain specialists and epistemic arbitrageurs within a shared environment of research problems, researcher interactions, and scientific artifacts. Individual decisions connect to collective state through a belief-desire-intention architecture, a prerequisite-based problem graph, a dynamic interaction network, and a persistent publication registry. The framework makes researcher roles and knowledge-transfer frictions explicit, providing a process-oriented account of scientific production and exchange. Two exploratory simulations with 10 and 18 agents produce 68 and 274 registered papers. In the smaller run, arbitrageurs take part in 16 of the 26 contact pairs that join this community into one connected component, while topical similarity alone splits it into seven. The same run yields three similarity edges, 26 contact pairs, and two coauthorship pairs, so measured community structure depends on what counts as a link. Paper production is abundant, but no recorded replication succeeds, which places the bottleneck in verification rather than production. Solved-problem sets correspond to the prerequisite reachability of the disciplines represented in each community. SLA makes knowledge liquidity, the movement and reuse of artifacts across domains, a quantity that agentic science can simulate and measure.

  • Spacetime Emergence in the Memory of a Learning Agent AI
    Philip A. LeMaitre, T. Rick Perche · arXiv:2611.03596
    Abstract

    We show that the causal structure of spacetime can emerge within the memory of a learning agent performing a quantum information task. We train a model-based reinforcement-learning agent to maximize the quantum mutual information between localized quantum probes interacting with a quantum field in Minkowski spacetime, without directly providing it with information regarding the spacetime metric or causal structure. In learning to accomplish this task, the reinforcement-learning agent constructs an internal predictive model whose planner develops the characteristic null structure of spacetime, allowing us to reconstruct the causal structure of the Minkowski metric with high accuracy. Within this task, spacetime geometry is obtained from an agent's successful predictions and actions, providing a first step toward showing that spacetime concepts emerge from operational quantum experience.

    Byunghyun Ko, Won-Ki Jeong, Chaoli Wang · arXiv:2611.01457
    Abstract

    Localized false-merge correction in connectomics requires converting an identified error into an executable native 3D partition.   • Robust Safety Assurance for Robotic Swarms Using Decentralized Collision Verification RO
    Grant Phillips, Hoang-Dung Tran, Justin M. Bradley · arXiv:2611.01081
    Abstract

    In this paper we present the methodologies, results, and analysis used to deploy a decentralized safety verification algorithm on a robotic swarm to prevent collisions at runtime in an outdoor environment. Typically, approaches toward verification of swarms of cyber-physical systems (CPS) are evaluated solely in numerical simulations or in highly controlled, noiseless indoor environments. Our efforts to implement collision avoidance on a real swarm system outdoors have revealed that the underlying algorithmic assumptions juxtaposed against the implementation details of the target system are critical factors in a method's efficacy in the real world. Here we modify, enhance, and analyze a decentralized reachability-based method for collision avoidance on a size, weight, and power-constrained uncrewed aerial vehicle (UAV) swarm with the objective of illustrating the challenges of real-world CPS deployments as well as provide guidance for researchers in this space. Verification performance, resource utilization, and timing data were gathered over the course of 140 total outdoor vehicle flights, including 6 swarm flights composed of 8 UAVs, and used to improve collision-avoidance performance as experimentation progressed. Our extensive experiments show that the proposed decentralized runtime verification approach can efficiently verify the safety of multiple UAVs in real-time and help all agents take safe actions to avoid collisions. On average, each agent, using our open-source safety_checker library, takes approximately 500-520 ms to verify its safety for the next 4 s into the future, in which the reachable set broadcast time dominates the total verification time (~300-500 ms).

  • Serving Agentic Robot Fleets: Workload Characterization, KV-Cache Thrashing, and Admission Control RO
    Sida He · arXiv:2611.01404
    Abstract

    Agentic robots combine a vision-language brain, a tool-using harness, and a vision-language-action (VLA) policy. Serving a fleet of these agents introduces two coupled resource constraints: the brain repeatedly revisits long, growing key-value (KV) cache states, while the VLA executes action chunks under tight control deadlines. We characterize 80 LIBERO-PRO episodes generated by GPT-6-Astra, RPent, and pi0.5 (1,862 brain turns and 1,582 VLA inference chunks), profile both layers on H800 graphics processing units (GPUs), and replay the resulting agent programs on a vLLM serving engine. Brain contexts reach 215k tokens; the median Astra request takes 7.78 s, while the intervening environment-step gap is only 1.0 s. Under a 0.25 s action-chunk deadline, one VLA GPU meets the 1% starvation target for one robot; under a 1.0 s deadline, its measured capacity reaches at least 32. In sustained brain replay, increasing concurrency from 20 to 32 robots reduces throughput from about 2.4 to 0.6 requests/s as repetitive prefill displaces generation. A static active-episode cap restores 2.24-2.25 requests/s and raises completed trace-program throughput from 1.30 to 5.00-5.55 episodes/min. Our measurements expose the workload-dependent shift between action deadlines and brain KV residency, and establish practical principles for capacity planning and admission in sustained agentic robot fleets.

  • Local Solution Repair for Multi-Agent Pickup and Delivery RO
    Ethan Schneider, Jingkai Chen, Seth Hutchinson, Sonia Chernova · arXiv:2611.02090
    Abstract

    Multi-robot systems in automated warehouses must efficiently attend to a constant stream of pickup-and-delivery tasks while avoiding collisions. Existing Multi-Agent Pickup-and-Delivery (MAPD) solutions generate plans that are globally feasible but often locally inefficient. In large-scale, long-horizon deployments, these inefficiencies, such as robots backtracking from target goal locations and idling in congested areas, accumulate over time and degrade performance. This paper contributes Local Solution Repair (LSR), a goal replanning approach that detects suboptimal behaviors at run-time and locally improves plans to reduce conflict between agents. We demonstrate the utility of our approach by repairing solutions generated by state-of-the-art MAPD baselines, M2M and LNS-PBS. Our results in long-horizon warehouse simulations over three environments show upwards of 5.9% improvement in task throughput over current state-of-the-art methods by reducing the number of suboptimal instances by ~36k or by 14.6% with minor additional computation time.

  • Late Autumn: LLM Agents Specialize a General Multi-Robot Planner for One Warehouse RO/AI
    Yimin Tang, Han Zhang, Shao-Hung Chan, Erdem Bıyık et al. · arXiv:2611.02391
    Abstract

    No algorithm is best on all problems, but a real warehouse is a narrow problem: its layout and processes rarely change. Operators still run one general multi-robot planner at many sites, because experts cannot tune it for each one. In this work, which we call Late Autumn, we explore whether LLM coding agents running autonomous research can specialize a general planner for one site, trading generality for throughput, much as post-training adapts a pretrained LLM to one task. We study six campaigns on a lifelong planner that combines task assignment with multi-agent path finding, on a floor modeled on an existing automated warehouse. We find that (1) the agents raise throughput by 22--60%, and the gains hold on unseen seeds; (2) they rediscover known MAPF mechanisms, such as corridor-exit priorities, local guidance, and rollouts, and independent campaigns find the same bottleneck; (3) the gains largely carry over to another layout, a larger fleet, and other service times, shrink with a smaller fleet, and mostly vanish under an outbound-heavy demand mix; and (4) each final patch (0.7k--2.4k lines) is a chain of scored commits with a median step of 27 lines, so experts can review it, and review finds problems that the score misses; some patches multiply planner compute by over 100x. LLM agents can therefore turn each site's bias into extra throughput at little engineering cost, with reviewable patches; we expect experts to shift toward designing insightful metrics and fast simulators that mirror the real problem.

  • Swarm Choreography Made Simple: Superposed Guiding Vector Fields for Rigid Formation Path Following RO
    Jo{ã}o Machado, Matthieu Verdoucq, Christophe Jouffrais, Murat Bronz · arXiv:2611.03016
    Abstract

    This paper presents a decentralized guiding vector field framework for rigid formation path following in multi- UAV systems based on the superposition of simple rule-driven vector fields. Each control objective is encoded as a dedicated vector-field component: a normalized tangential term to ensure progression along a prescribed geometric path at a desired speed, a convergence term driving the formation toward the path, a local coordination term that preserves the desired formation shape through a pseudo-path parameter, and a local separation term for inter-agent collision avoidance. The coordination mechanism requires the exchange of only a single scalar per agent, resulting in low communication bandwidth. Partial theoretical results establish convergence of the swarm to the desired path and formation in the absence of the sepa- ration term, while collision avoidance is formally guaranteed. The approach is experimentally validated using a swarm of five quadrotors executing two 3D choreographies. The first experiment involves tracking a helical path while maintaining a rigid V-shaped formation. The second demonstrates a spiral- like motion obtained by a rotating circular formation tracking a vertical line. The experimental results support the effectiveness and practical applicability of the proposed framework.

    Arihant Yadav, Sophie Jörg, Eakta Jain · arXiv:2611.03548
    Abstract

    This work investigates human-swarm interaction (HSI) for steering large collectives through an intermediary robot helper.   • XLA from the Trenches: Correctness on TPU Workloads at Scale AI
    Ignacio Cano, Amir Saadat, Rishipal Singh Bhatia, Samuel Ginzburg et al. · arXiv:2611.00624
    Abstract

    Accelerating modern foundational models has pushed domain-specific compilers toward increasingly complex, multi-tiered optimization pipelines. However, this heavy optimization focus introduces delicate tradeoffs between peak execution performance and correctness. Subtle codegen defects can trigger numerical degradation and memory or buffer corruption, invalidating multi-million-dollar training runs and degrading live serving quality without raising obvious compiler errors. In this paper, we present a battle-tested methodology for verifying and triaging Google's XLA:TPU compiler at hyperscale.   • Capacity Planning for Resource-Constrained Clinical LLM Serving AI
    Yi Tang, Geoffrey Martin, Yiye Zhang, Yifan Peng · arXiv:2611.01152
    Abstract

    Deploying large language models (LLMs) under a fixed accelerator budget requires jointly deciding how computational capacity is allocated across model sizes and how incoming requests are routed across that capacity. Although prior work has extensively studied LLM routing and serving efficiency, the relative importance of fleet composition and routing under resource constraints remains less understood. We investigate this question using a controlled four-GPU serving environment with heterogeneous clinical NLP workloads spanning short factual verification, biomedical semantic matching, and long-context clinical summarization. We systematically vary fleet composition, routing policy, and offered load while holding the workload trace and hardware budget fixed. Across 24 configurations, descriptive main-effect shares attribute more variation to fleet composition than to the evaluated routing policies in time to first token, latency, and long-context coverage. Within this measured regime, the results support prioritizing capacity allocation, with routing managing demand within the provisioned capacity.

  • LoRAForge: Co-Designing Post-Hoc Compression and Execution for Efficient Multi-LoRA Serving AI
    Shenghui Li, Thiemo Voigt · arXiv:2611.01256
    Abstract

    Multi-LoRA serving allows many task-specific adapters to share a common base model, but scaling to large adapter fleets introduces substantial storage, loading, and execution overheads. Post-hoc compression can reduce the memory footprint of trained LoRA adapters, yet smaller ranks do not automatically translate into faster serving. For instance, padding, factor access, and generic kernels can obscure the retained low-rank structure, while independently recompressing thousands of modules also incurs significant preparation overhead. We present LoRAForge, a system connecting batched factor construction to rank-specialized execution through an explicit retained-rank representation.   • Comparative study of global pooling for pre-departure ship fuel consumption prediction: Advantages over per-ship and adaptation-based approaches AI
    Heungjun Im, Bongmin Cha, Agung Nugraha, Donghyun Kim et al. · arXiv:2611.03015
    Abstract

    Most fuel oil consumption (FOC) models for voyage planning are per-ship models and thus generalize poorly beyond each vessel's records. Transfer and federated learning have been reported to perform better, but under different protocols that often use inputs unavailable before departure or random data splits. It therefore remains unclear which approach performs best. In this study, we compare 11 per-ship, adaptation-based, and global methods on 21 vessels. To prevent leakage, all methods receive identical inputs known or forecast before departure, excluding engine RPM and shaft power, and are tested on intact voyage legs after all training records of each vessel. Global pooling, in which one model describes each vessel by its ship identity and seven ship specifications, performs best overall in every setting examined. For in-fleet vessels, Global LightGBM reaches an overall MAPE of 8.92%, against 11.14% for Per-Ship LightGBM, and outperforms every per-ship and adaptation-based model. Global models of five architectures, including a Transformer, all outperform the per-ship and federated models. Fine-tuning a global model for each vessel, even by low-rank adaptation of the Transformer, adds no accuracy. Without any records of a vessel, the global model reaches a median MAPE of 11.20% and outperforms a per-ship model trained on that vessel's records for 14 of 21 vessels. Adding its first records to the pooled data is as accurate as vessel-specific adaptation. Ship identity and specifications are largely interchangeable for in-fleet vessels, but only the specifications support zero-shot prediction. Its error grows as a vessel's specifications differ from the fleet's. Per-ship models and vessel-specific adaptation are therefore unnecessary under fair comparison. One global model can serve a whole fleet, including newly delivered vessels with similar specifications.

  • Vision-based Sensing of Urban Environment using Hardware-Software co-design at Scale CV
    Akshit Gupta, R Venkatesha Prasad · arXiv:2611.00699
    Abstract

    Urban environmental monitoring is crucial for climate-adaptive cities, yet current approaches rely heavily on human inspection, overhead remote sensing, or limited terrestrial RGB imagery, thereby lacking comprehensive multispectral perspectives at street level.   • A Reproducible Reconstruction and Evaluation Framework for Photorealistic Lunar Analog Scene Reconstruction Toward Digital Twins RO
    Antar Mazumder, Ryan Hartzell, Luke Boyd, Alexander Robinson et al. · arXiv:2611.00870
    Abstract

    In this paper, we present a reconstruction pipeline that empirically quantifies the photorealistic scene reconstruction accuracy from both human-controlled and rover mast camera monocular feeds against physical ground truth as a function of Lunar traversal effects (e.g., motion blur, haze due to lofted regolith, trajectory bias, and sparse views). First, we benchmark six variants of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) across three Lunar analog scenes on held-out test sets using standard photometric metrics. We then establish how under traversal effects, this test-set-specific Novel View Synthesis (NVS) performance does not proportionally scale to the same photometric metrics in the reconstructed DTs. Our results quantify that splat training metrics alone are insufficient predictors of Cross-Reconstruction Photorealistic NVS Fidelity (CRPNVS), and that factors such as rover trajectory bias and geometric scene coverage play a critical role in determining the photorealistic quality of the reconstructed scenes targeting Lunar analog Digital Twins. Additionally, we pilot study Coverage Score ($S_c$), a geometry-based diagnostic derived from COLMAP poses and point cloud structure that provides a meaningful heuristic about the highest achievable CRPNVS structural fidelity, independently of splat training performance in the absence of a human-controlled reference. These findings establish a standardized, human control-referenced evaluation pipeline for photorealistic scene reconstruction for Lunar-analog Digital Twin development and future planetary exploration.   • AnchorHEC: Targetless Hand-Eye Calibration of Wrist and Fixed Cameras RO
    Guebin Hwang, Taeyeop Lee, Yeonseo Lee, Hyosup Shin et al. · arXiv:2611.02884
    Abstract

    Recent robot systems combine wrist and fixed cameras, requiring camera-to-robot transforms for both. Most hand-eye calibration methods rely on targets or robot-body observations. Scene-based methods using 3D reconstruction require neither but address only wrist cameras. We introduce AnchorHEC, a targetless hand-eye calibration framework for wrist and fixed cameras in a single procedure using RGB images, forward kinematics (FK), and known camera intrinsics. We initialize both camera types using FK and a shared 3D foundation model reconstruction. FK-constrained wrist-image bundle adjustment reduces the impact of reconstructed pose and scale errors. If this refinement fails or retains too few inliers, we relax the constraints on potentially inaccurate FK poses and lens parameters. We use the refined wrist transform and FK to triangulate metric points and estimate fixed-camera poses. Across three backbones, calibration errors in simulation decrease by 86.4% in translation and 71.6% in rotation, averaged over both camera types, relative to reconstruction-based baselines. We also validate AnchorHEC on a physical robot. Project page: this https URL

  • 3D Neural Rendering for Automated Ultrasonic Laser Scans CV/RO
    Thomas Pierson, Matthew Mitchell, Kira Perna, Anand Iyer et al. · arXiv:2611.00527
    Abstract

    Laser doppler vibrometer (LDV) scans can be used to detect defects and cracks in manufactured parts before they are deployed. This work presents a method for reconstructing the steady-state surface wavefield of a three-dimensional part by combining automated robotic manipulation with neural rendering. A six-degree-of-freedom (6-DOF) robotic manipulator (UFactory xArm6) with an embedded piezoelectric transducer presented an acoustically excited test article to a stationary LDV at a sequence of known orientations, yielding a set of complex-valued 2D scans with poses derived analytically from the arm kinematics rather than from image-based pose estimation. These scans were used to train a novel complex-nerfacto model, a modified Neural Radiance Field (NeRF) pipeline that replaces the standard three-channel RGB output head with a two-channel output. The complex-valued pipeline achieved a mean ZNCC of 0.60 at held-out viewing angles, compared with 0.28 for a baseline RGB nerfacto model trained on rendered images of the same data. The resulting surface wavefield reconstructions supported defect detection and characterization on complex components.   • SceneBound: How Much Should a Streaming 3D Reconstruction Model Remember? CV/RO
    Ziming Wang, Chuyue Gong, Chutian Wang, Jingmin Zhu et al. · arXiv:2611.00756
    Abstract

    Streaming 3D reconstruction models keep past video frames in a recurrent state, fast weights from test-time training, or a key-value (KV) cache. Each written frame shifts the coordinate frame of later cameras or occupies GPU memory. Training-free rules decide what to write from a signal of the current frame. How much they write accounts for their mean accuracy: spending the update budget of a gate at uniformly spaced frames, with no signal, matches nine published gates, and the budget bounds the drift of the state wherever the writes fall. We propose SceneBound, a training-free rule that writes a frame only when its predicted camera pose lies at least a fixed radius from every stored keyframe. Its keyframe count stays within a constant factor of the covering number of the visited poses, the fewest views that keep a keyframe near every camera, so the count grows with the places visited and stays flat when the camera returns. In large scenes, SceneBound closes a submap once decoding its first frame again reveals drift. SceneBound uses one radius across four released models and three memory types. In the recurrent state of CUT3R and the KV cache of StreamVGGT, it achieves the lowest mean trajectory error among the published training-free rules we evaluate, in one pass and over nine passes of a scene, where its keyframes grow by 3% to 6%.

    Shivangi Rai, Kunal Jangid, Vaibhav Kumar, Vinod K Kurmi · arXiv:2611.03588
    Abstract

    Robust 3D point-cloud recognition is critical for robotics, mapping, and urban sensing, yet point-cloud classifiers often suffer performance drops under domain shifts caused by sensor changes, environmental variation, and acquisition artifacts. Many existing adaptation methods are difficult to deploy in practice because they require access to source data or the internal parameters of the source model, which may be restricted by privacy, licensing, or security constraints. We present 3D-BBDA, a backpropagation-free black-box domain adaptation framework that adapts a source-trained predictor to unlabeled target data using only black-box outputs. Our method performs pseudo-label guided prototype-based alignment and employs a weighted prediction module to fuse source logits with alignment logits, reducing sensitivity to noisy pseudo-labels. To stabilize prototype memory when pseudo-label coverage is incomplete, we construct a Gaussian-perturbed prototype prior from unlabeled target predictions and use it as a fallback for missing or unreliable class prototypes during alignment. Experiments on four corrupted 3D benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements over strong baselines. On ModelNet-40C, 3D-BBDA achieves mean errors of 32.08% (PointNet) and 29.62% (GDANet), outperforming Source-only (35.88% / 34.03%) and BFTT3D (33.78% / 32.36%). 3D-BBDA requires only a single forward pass with no gradient updates making it suitable for real-time, privacy-preserving deployment in transferable 3D recognition pipelines. Project page is available at this https URL

  • Balancing Representations by Identifying and Generating Under-represented Data CV/AI
    Filipe Laitenberger, Hirokatsu Kataoka, Yuki Asano, Cees Snoek et al. · arXiv:2611.02952
    Abstract

    Self-supervised learning inherits the coverage of its source data. When an unlabeled corpus is long-tailed, the representation becomes dense around frequent modes and thin where coverage is weak. Objectives for long-tailed SSL reweight the images already in the corpus and add no new ones. We introduce BRIDGE (Balancing Representations by Identifying and Generating Under-represented Data), a label-free feedback loop around the source set. The current encoder scores every source image by its mean kNN distance, and a budgeted acquisition rule selects images from a band of that score distribution where support is thin and the generator still produces faithful variants. A frozen image-to-image diffusion model then adds local variants of the selected anchors, and pretraining continues on the enlarged source set. We formulate acquisition as recoverability-weighted allocation of local support and derive when a finite budget should favor a sparse band that excludes the extreme tail and when reallocation after each repair is optimal under a fixed objective. Across five long-tailed, web-scraped and synthetic source regimes, BRIDGE has the highest seven-task linear-probe and kNN average of the compared methods, and paired experiments extend the gain to ViT-S under contrastive and self-distillation objectives, to low-label fine-tuning and to semantic segmentation. The linear-probe gain is smaller under masked reconstruction. Generated repair matches an oracle that restores the withheld real images, and under a frozen encoder the inserted images shrink the neighborhoods of the selected anchors about twice as much as those of matched unselected images. BRIDGE needs no labels and leaves the pretraining objective unchanged, so it can be added to pretraining on uncurated web data, where the encoder decides which images to generate.

  • Selective Positional Allocation for Noise-Robust Query Retrieval in Small-Object Detection CV
    Yangchen Zeng · arXiv:2611.00035
    Abstract

    In aerial and remote-sensing imagery, detecting tiny targets is frequently hampered less by the representational capacity of the backbone network than by the manner in which positional priors are distributed across ill-defined foreground and background regions. We introduce SPAR (Selective Positional Allocation and Retrieval), a detection framework that re-conceptualizes positional cues as an allocatable resource: they are intensified at semantically meaningful locations while responses driven by scene clutter are actively attenuated. At the heart of SPAR lies Selective Positional Embedding (SPE), an interpretable embedding mechanism paired with an activation-bar visualization that facilitates direct inspection and hyper-parameter tuning. The encoder branch, termed MOHFE, fuses heatmap-modulated positional signals with category-specific and box-level evidence to yield more compact multi-scale features; the decoder branch, termed HIDQ, employs a foreground gating operation to discard background-dominated candidates prior to query decoding. To cope with the extreme sparsity of small-object evidence, we additionally design DPSConv (Dual-Path Sampling Convolution), which augments retrieval-oriented representations through two geometrically complementary sampling routes. Notably, all heatmap supervision is derived from existing detection objectives and consumed exclusively during training, requiring no auxiliary heatmap annotations and leaving inference as an ordinary forward pass with no additional gradient overhead. Comprehensive evaluations on DOTA, DIOR, VisDrone, PASCAL VOC, NWPU VHR-10, and AITOD demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed design: relative to the baseline, SPAR attains an improvement of up to 9.1 percentage points, shortens the decoder from eight layers to three, and reduces model parameters by 59.4% (66.3M versus 163M).

  • Comparing Monocular and Multi-View 3D Pedestrian Reconstruction Strategies for Urban Intersection Sensing CV
    Kauan Mariani, Matheus Carvalho, Joel Perca, João Rulff et al. · arXiv:2611.00066
    Abstract

    Understanding how pedestrians move and interact at urban intersections is central to traffic-safety assessment, mobility planning, and smart-city sensing. Such analyses rely on physically grounded descriptions of motion: 3D body pose, metric trajectories, and pose-derived descriptors such as stature and walking speed. Recovering these from fixed roadside cameras is difficult: viewpoints are oblique and long-range, occlusions are frequent, and neither ground-truth 3D pose nor cross-camera identity labels are typically available. We compare two strategies for deriving pedestrian motion descriptors from a multi-camera intersection dataset: a monocular learned pipeline that combines text-prompted segmentation, tracking, and learned 3D body estimation, and a calibrated multi-view geometric pipeline that combines 2D pose estimation, tracking, cross-camera association, and triangulation. Because 3D ground truth is unavailable, we evaluate both with reference-free criteria relevant to intersection analysis: pedestrian coverage, anthropometric plausibility, walking-speed distributions, computational cost, and failure modes. The two strategies exhibit complementary trade-offs. The learned pipeline is simpler to deploy and covers more pedestrians, but suffers from scale ambiguity and systematic height compression; the geometric pipeline yields metric reconstructions more efficiently, but requires calibration, discards pedestrians seen by only one camera, and can produce severe outliers in dense crowds. We distill these findings into practical guidance for selecting a reconstruction strategy and configuring camera infrastructure according to the target analysis task.

  • How Reliable Are Object Detection Models Beyond Clear Weather? A Deployment-Oriented Robustness Study for Autonomous Driving CV
    Munshaibur Rahman Mahin, Isha Das, Most. Sonia Islam, Mohammad Sakib Mahmood et al. · arXiv:2611.00071
    Abstract

    Autonomous driving perception systems must maintain reliable object detection under diverse environmental conditions, not only in clear daytime scenes. These findings offer deployment-oriented insights into detector robustness and confidence reliability for safety-critical intelligent vehicle perception systems.

    Abstract

    Future dynamic novel-view synthesis requires extrapolating multiple motion regimes within the same scene.   • Real-VAS: a Real-World Video Amodal Segmentation Dataset CV

    Abstract

    Amodal video object segmentation is fundamentally limited by the absence of datasets that combine real-world complexity with precise ground-truth annotations.   • Smooth bounding box for object detection CV
    Chao Zhou, Shenghua He, Fengchuang Xing, Han Yang et al. · arXiv:2611.00676
    Abstract

    Deep learning-based object detection models are highly dependent on the quality of annotated training data. However, large-scale datasets often contain inevitable label noise, with bounding box localization errors being a prevalent and impactful form in object detection. While label noise handling techniques are well-studied for image classification, their adaptation to the more complex problem of bounding box errors remains underexplored. To address this, we propose a novel Smooth Bounding Box (SBB) strategy to mitigate the adverse effects of bounding box annotation inaccuracies. Our key insight is that annotation errors are often linked to annotator factors rather than specific image content, allowing for a statistical correction approach. Based on object size distribution within datasets, we implement the SBB strategy through two methods: 1) Global Scaling Correction (GSC), which applies a uniform scaling factor to estimate and correct a global average error; and 2) Interval Scaling Correction (ISC), which categorizes objects by size into intervals and estimates a dedicated correction factor for each, enabling more precise, fine-grained calibration. For efficient optimization of multiple parameters in ISC, we design a tailored model fine-tuning strategy. Furthermore, we introduce a Normalized Residual Fluctuation (NRF) metric to quantitatively assess the smoothing effect and training stability. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that our method consistently enhances detection accuracy across various model architectures. Both GSC and ISC achieve significant performance improvements over strong baselines, including the latest YOLOv11n, without adding inference overhead. The codes are available at this https URL.

  • SHield: Watermarking 4D Gaussian Splatting via Spherical Harmonic Coefficient Perturbation CV
    Kunal Bhosikar, Prajas Wadekar, Venkata Sai Pranav Bachina, Ankit Gangwal et al. · arXiv:2611.01162
    Abstract

    4D Gaussian Splatting (4DGS) models are computationally expensive to train but distributed as easily copied parameter files. Existing watermarks protect these assets by fine-tuning the scene against a neural decoder to extract bit strings from rendered video, a heavy process vulnerable to the temporal dynamics of dynamic scenes. However, if the threat model targets the redistributed file itself, verification can be vastly simplified. Because the canonical Spherical-Harmonic (SH) coefficients of 4DGS are time- and view-invariant, parameter-space perturbations can be recovered via deterministic arithmetic. We propose **SHield**, the first non-blind, decoder-free watermarking approach that natively embeds full-resolution RGB images into 4DGS files. SHield tiles a zero-mean payload across the SH coefficients of a key-selected Gaussian subset, applying a brief anchored optimization to preserve visual fidelity. To verify ownership, it geometrically re-associates the key using canonical Gaussian centres to survive file tampering and recovers the payload in closed form. Across 30 scenes (D-NeRF, HyperNeRF, DyNeRF), SHield guarantees near-perfect payload recovery (Pearson $r \geq 0.991$) on all 150 tested models with a mean rendering penalty of $\leq 0.13$~dB. Held-out evaluation yields perfect detection on 400 true trials and zero false positives across $1{,}600$ wrong-subset null trials. We also strictly bound the method's robustness: SHield survives INT8 quantization and $30\%$ opacity pruning for the image payload, and on aggressive SH-degree-1 truncation bit payloads are still decodable.

  • AbacusGS: Gaussian Splatting without Per-Splat Sorting CV
    Jiacheng Ding, Yanliang Qi, Cong Guo, Peihua Mai · arXiv:2611.01534
    Abstract

    3D Gaussian Splatting sorts every splat by depth in every frame. Viewers that draw splats with the hardware rasterizer and sort them on the CPU, as most web and mobile viewers do, spend most of the frame time in this sort. We present AbacusGS, which stores the blending order of a 3DGS scene in the scene layout. AbacusGS groups the splats into octree boxes of at most 128 splats and stores each box in order along an axis chosen at write time. In each frame, AbacusGS sorts only the visible boxes, and one dot product per box decides whether the box is read forward or backward. A short order-aware fine-tuning converts an existing 3DGS model, and the over operator of 3DGS stays unchanged. On 13 standard scenes, AbacusGS loses 0.13 dB PSNR on average against an equally fine-tuned 3DGS model. In an OpenGL viewer that orders splats on the CPU, AbacusGS renders frames 4.5 times faster than per-splat sorting. We also present a compact format of 17 bytes per splat and a CUDA tile renderer that use the same order. The code will be released.

    Abstract

  • Unified Loss-Aware Density Control for 3D Gaussian Splatting CV
    Tae-Young Kim, Jiwoo Chung, Jae-Pil Heo · arXiv:2611.02044
    Abstract

    The efficiency and fidelity of 3D Gaussian Splatting critically depend on adaptive density control, which grows and suppresses Gaussian primitives throughout optimization. However, the standard pipeline still relies on heuristic view-space gradients, scale thresholds, and global opacity resets, rather than explicitly assessing the loss-reducing effect of each split, clone, or opacity update. This mismatch can allocate primitives to already-sufficient regions, miss under-reconstructed structures, and destabilize training through blanket opacity changes. In this work, we recast adaptive density control as a collection of local loss-reduction decisions. We derive efficient Taylor-based per-Gaussian surrogates that predict the objective change caused by three primitive-level operations: splitting, cloning, and opacity decay. This yields a unified loss-aware framework with three components. First, we extend second-order splitting to a normalized joint position-scale space, enabling children to adapt not only their locations but also their anisotropic extents. Second, we formulate duplicate cloning as a surrogate minimization problem, selecting only Gaussians whose duplicated contribution is predicted to improve reconstruction and assigning each child a principled opacity. Third, we replace global opacity reset with selective soft opacity decay that weakens harmful primitives while preserving useful ones. Across standard 3DGS benchmarks, our method produces more targeted primitive allocation and achieves improved rendering quality with comparable or smaller Gaussian budgets compared to baselines.

  • Depth Anyviewpoint? Evaluating Monocular Depth Over Viewpoint Sequences CV
    Theo Rode, Rui Zhang, Ryan Aubrey, Olivia Li et al. · arXiv:2611.02203
    Abstract

    Monocular depth estimation aims to predict depth maps from single RGB images (forgoing the need for stereo camera pairs) and has seen impressive leaps in performance in recent work due to the introduction of affine-invariant losses and the use of foundation model features. These monocular depth models predict affine-invariant depth, which describes the relative positions of objects in the scene instead of absolute depth. While these models demonstrate strong performance on single image benchmarks and qualitatively consistent predictions in video, their effectiveness at preserving consistent spatial structure has been less thoroughly explored. Many potential real-world applications, such as handling video streams as input, rely on coherent three dimensional consistency in scenes. We demonstrate this methodology over diverse, real-world video sequences where we are able to anchor depth model output to metric depth values using structure from motion. Our work demonstrates open challenges in the development of broadly applicable monocular depth foundation models, particularly with their ability to provide temporal consistency over long time frames. We also identify challenges in the straightforward adoption of structure-from-motion as a metric grounding tool for monocular depth estimation models.

    Wenbo Sui, Lars P. Mikkelsen, Jens W. Andreasen, Daniel Lichau et al. · arXiv:2611.02204
    Abstract

  • Lie Routing Vision Language Models for View-Robust 3D Understanding CV
    Jingjun Yi, Chen Hu, Qi Bi, Hao Zheng et al. · arXiv:2611.02306
    Abstract

    Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have made substantial progress in 3D scene understanding from multi-view RGB observations. Yet a fixed object and query can elicit inconsistent target-related responses across camera viewpoints, a phenomenon we characterize as viewpoint-induced semantic drift. We introduce LieVLM, a Lie routing framework for view-robust 3D understanding. Motivated by the rotational component of viewpoint change, Lie routing adapts visual representations by applying local $\mathrm{SO}(3)$ transformations to projected features within learned three-dimensional subspaces. Geometry tokens extracted by VGGT predict local rotation parameters and channel-wise routing coefficients. A closed-form Padé--Cayley map transforms projected visual features, whose identity-relative corrections are projected back and gated into the original visual and geometry features. This design retains the pretrained semantic and direct spatial context while learning view-dependent feature adjustments. Experiments on 3D captioning, grounding, detection, and spatial reasoning show consistent improvements over additive fusion across training scales and backbones. Controlled ablations examine the roles of geometric conditioning and transformation structure. Viewpoint analyses further reveal systematic variation in the predicted routing states alongside more localized target responses and stronger task performance across viewpoint changes.

  • IMUdrape: Generating Garment-Mounted Virtual IMU Data from Text through Physics-Based Cloth Simulation CV
    Zikang Leng, Thomas Plötz · arXiv:2611.02393
    Abstract

    Inertial sensors are moving into clothing, from sensorized sportswear to garments instrumented with hundreds of IMUs. Sewn into clothing, a sensor no longer moves rigidly with the body: loose fabric slides, twists and swings, so a garment-mounted sensor records the motion of the fabric as well as that of the body, and this extra motion differs from one garment to the next. Models for such sensors need training data recorded with the deployed garment, yet labeled data are scarce. Virtual IMU data generated from videos or text have eased this scarcity for body-attached sensors, but existing generators attach their virtual sensors rigidly to the body. We present IMUdrape, which generates garment-mounted virtual IMU data from text through physics-based cloth simulation. IMUdrape synthesizes motion from activity descriptions, reconstructs the target garment as a sewing pattern from photographs, places virtual IMUs at the deployment's real sensor positions, and simulates the garment with an intersection-free contact solver, so that every simulation yields paired garment and body signals. On four real datasets with clothing-mounted sensors, IMUdrape improves on existing virtual-data approaches for both activity recognition and full-body pose estimation. With one real subject, its data improve recognition macro-F1 by 7% to 46% over real data alone and by 2% to 11% over body-mounted virtual data from the same motion. When the garment is simulated on recorded motion, the pose error without any real training data falls by 30% to 33% relative to generated motion, and garment data remain 12% more accurate than body data. IMUdrape thus reduces the amount of real data that garment-mounted sensing requires.

    Abstract

    3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has shown considerable potential for novel view synthesis (NVS) and 3D reconstruction.     Abstract

    Shaocheng Shen, Zihan Zheng, Kejin Li, Lu Liu et al. · arXiv:2611.02767
    Abstract

    3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) enables photorealistic novel- view synthesis with real-time rendering, yet fine-grained di- agnosis of its rendering artifacts remains challenging. To ad- dress this limitation, we introduce GaussFlaw-80K, the first large-scale dataset for fine-grained perceptual diagnosis in 3DGS. These an- notations turn 3DGS artifact diagnosis into an explicit and measurable task. Beyond evaluation, we further demonstrate the downstream benefits of fine-grained diagnosis through three complementary targeted operations: Gaussian densification, attribute correction, and outlier prun- ing. Experiments show that GeoCritic achieves an AUC-J of 0.9507 and an Acc@1 of 80.08%, while diagnosis-guided re- finement improves all reported global and crop-level metrics across diverse datasets and 3DGS backbones.

  • Local-Global Joint Modeling for Point Cloud Super-Resolution Guided by Multi-Scale Spatial Priors CV
    Wang Yang, Wenyi Ge · arXiv:2611.02786
    Abstract

    Range-image-based point cloud super-resolution provides an effective way to reconstruct sparse outdoor point clouds by projecting low-resolution LiDAR scans into a regular 2D representation. However, current methods still face three challenges. First, range images exhibit pronounced vertical nonuniformity, with sparse observations in distant regions and denser observations in nearby regions. Some cross-scale fusion mechanisms do not account for the varying reliability of features at different vertical positions, allowing invalid values and noise from distant sparse regions to propagate into the decoder. Second, a single convolutional or sequence-modeling architecture often struggles to preserve both local high-frequency geometric details and global long-range dependencies, resulting in over-smoothed reconstructions. Third, smooth transition depths near foreground-background boundaries can produce spike artifacts when the 2D range image is back-projected into 3D. To address these issues, we propose a point cloud super-resolution method based on multi-scale spatial priors and joint local-global modeling. The design comprises a spatially gated fusion module, a multi-granularity local-global cooperative perception module, and 2D median-residual denoising. Experiments on KITTI-360 and nuScenes demonstrate effective reconstruction, with IoU scores of 0.4703 and 0.3593, respectively, supporting the feasibility of the proposed method for range-image super-resolution.

  • 3D Gaussian Splatting within Self-Learned Neural View-Dependent Color Fields CV
    Yixiao Yang, Yu-Shen Liu, Zhizhong Han · arXiv:2611.03114
    Abstract

    3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has become a vital tool in novel view synthesis. 3D Gaussian functions with attributes like color, opacity, and scale are stacked together to explicitly model a radiance field. To address this issue, we propose to learn a radiance field within a continuous view-dependent color field parameterized by neural networks.   • CL3DOD: Understanding the Stability-Plasticity Dilemma in Outdoor Class-Incremental 3D Object Detection CV
    Muhammad Ahmed Ullah Khan, Khalil Ahmed, Didier Stricker, Muhammad Zeshan Afzal · arXiv:2611.03229
    Abstract

    Class-incremental learning (CIL) for outdoor 3D object detection remains largely underexplored. Existing outdoor studies are limited to single-benchmark evaluations, with SDCoT++ considering KITTI and CL2OD considering nuScenes under different incremental settings. This makes it difficult to assess whether observed continual-learning behavior generalizes across autonomous-driving benchmarks. To the best of our knowledge, we provide the first unified cross-benchmark analysis of outdoor class-incremental 3D object detection across multiple autonomous-driving benchmarks, covering both two-task and multi-stage settings. Our code, evaluation setup, and incremental task splits will be publicly released.

    Manon Béchaz, Léo Buecher, Emanuele Dalsasso, Ciprian Tomoiaga et al. · arXiv:2611.03245
    Abstract

    Semantic change detection in remote sensing aims to identify and characterize land-cover transitions observed in pairs of satellite or aerial images. In particular, we show that progressively refining semantic categories, rather than considering them all directly, yields improved change detection, highlighting the benefits of incremental learning for semantic change detection.

    Monocular head pose estimation is usually formulated as absolute regression from a single face crop, yet many applications observe the same person over time and can supply an explicit anchor image. Recently, relative head pose estimation was proposed, which casts the problem as estimation of the relative geometric change between an anchor and a query image. However, existing methods still require the anchor image pose to be known at inference, significantly limiting their practical use. We first extend relative head pose estimation to full 6DoF by additionally predicting a depth-normalized relative translation, which composes with the anchor to recover metric query pose in the camera frame. The relative pose estimator is trained exclusively on synthetic face renderings, without real pose supervision, pseudo-labels, domain adaptation, or fine-tuning on the evaluation sets. On real 6DoF benchmarks, ReVA-HPE achieves state-of-the-art rotation accuracy on BIWI and, given a near-frontal enrollment image, on FaMoS, while remaining competitive in metric translation despite relying solely on synthetic pose supervision.   • Task-driven sparse-view planar X-ray computed tomography for axial metrology CV
    Yibin Tian, Shuming Yang, Wolfgang Heidrich · arXiv:2611.01537
    Abstract

    Computational imaging can reduce the acquisition burden when the system is designed around task-relevant information rather than complete image recovery. We investigate this framework for sparse-view planar X-ray tomography (XCT) of layered objects, using measurement of the axial termination gap (ATG) of back-drilled printed-circuit-board (PCB) vias as a representative task. Instead of using 3D reconstruction, the proposed method estimates ATG directly from polychromatic projection data through a physics-based sensitivity model. A gated hierarchical estimator first fits ATG together with radiometric perturbations and selectively introduces global geometry and specimen-warp models only when a reduced chi-squared adequacy test and Bayesian information criterion justify the added degrees of freedom. The same low-dimensional perturbation model is incorporated into marginalized Fisher-information analyses, yielding task-specific conditional and calibration-aware Cram'er-Rao bounds. Numerical experiments show that, under a fixed total photon budget, task-driven metrology changes little over 12-64 views, while reconstruction-driven metrology is strongly view-dependent with much larger errors. The direct estimator also follows the expected photon-limited scaling. These results demonstrate a computational sensing strategy in which XCT acquisition, physical modeling, and inference are evaluated according to the quantitative information required by the task rather than for general-purpose volumetric image fidelity.

  • Detecting Generated-Depth Injection Attacks on RGB-D Place Authentication CV
    Seigo Kawachi, Satoshi Matsuura · arXiv:2611.01550
    Abstract

    Advances in monocular depth estimation (MDE) now let an attacker generate depth from publicly available RGB images and submit forged RGB-D queries. Such attacks break a core assumption of RGB-D place authentication: that depth comes from a sensor. We study a presentation attack detection (PAD) classifier that uses geometric consistency between the enrollment and query views, as a software-layer defense. A three-stage evaluation shows how far this classifier detects software-injected depth, depending on how accurately the attacker knows the camera intrinsics. In the first stage, the attacker has only an estimate of the intrinsics, and the PAD classifier separates forgeries generated by all three MDE models with a validation area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) of 0.997-0.999. In the second stage, the attacker obtains the exact intrinsics, and the original PAD classifier falls to near chance (AUC 0.54-0.61); a retrained PAD classifier, however, still has the registration residual between enrollment and query as a cue. In the third stage, an oracle evaluation, in which the evaluator fits warps using the ground-truth 3D geometry, shows that detection falls to near chance only in its strongest case, where every valid query keypoint is given its ground-truth 3D coordinate. Of the detection cues, the reprojection error captures the attacker's misestimate of the intrinsics, whereas the registration residual remains even when the attacker knows the exact intrinsics.

  • Learning to Live: A Step Toward Lifelong Embodied Intelligence RO/CV
    Lirong Che, Peidong Wang, Yimeng Su, Jiong Wang et al. · arXiv:2611.00180
    Abstract

    Embodied agents are usually organized around individual tasks, yet the physical world keeps changing between tasks and during execution. We introduce LIFE (Lifelong Intelligence from Experience), a framework in which a robot observes continuously, remembers selectively, and turns experience into new capabilities. RoboBeats, a family of lightweight streaming gates, tracks conditions set by the agent and discovers new events, deciding when an observation warrants a response or a memory write. Selected observations enter Plastic Memory, a flat store of images and structured records that keeps the source evidence, so new fields and indexes can be added when later questions need them. When existing tools fall short, Explore-Distill-Mount (EDM) turns solutions found during problem solving into validated, reusable tools and skills. Under matched protocols, RoboBeats substantially improves condition tracking over its base models and, at the same writing budget, retains more useful evidence for memory question answering than uniform sampling. A case study of limited scale, replaying 24 hours of continuous video recorded by a real robot, illustrates how the integrated system builds memory from a day of experience and uses it to answer everyday questions. LIFE takes a step toward robots that become more familiar with their surroundings and more capable through experience: learning not only to complete tasks, but to live in the world.

    Abstract

    When searching for an object, humans naturally combine semantic intuition - reasoning about where the object is likely to be stored - with geometric awareness of what parts of the space are visible, occluded, or unexplored. Effective robot object search requires the same joint reasoning, yet existing methods rely on only one: geometric methods maintain principled spatial beliefs but lack deep semantic understanding, while language-model based methods leverage commonsense knowledge but maintain no explicit belief over space. We argue that jointly reasoning over semantic and geometric evidence in an explicit 3D belief enables human-like interactive object search that outperforms either modality alone. To test this claim, we build a system that accumulates per-object VLM plausibility scores and geometric occupancy evidence via Bayesian log-odds filtering into a unified 3D voxel belief field. High-belief regions in the combined belief field drive action selection at each step: taking a closer look from a different viewpoint, navigating toward an unexplored region of the workspace, or physically interacting with a closed cabinet. We deploy this system end-to-end on a bimanual mobile humanoid and find that it outperforms geometry-only search and LLM-based planning methods. Project website - this https URL

  • Vision-Language Models Are Joint Denoisers in Robotic Action Generation RO/AI/CV
    Xicheng Gong, Wenxuan Song, Chengze Liu, Qiwei Li et al. · arXiv:2611.01215
    Abstract

    Diffusion-based vision-language-action (VLA) models cascade pretrained vision-language models (VLMs) with diffusion action experts, but typically restrict the VLM to providing static conditioning. This separation limits how much pretrained perception and reasoning can guide action generation, especially in out-of-distribution settings. We introduce JADE, a Joint Action DEnoiser that enables the VLM and action expert to jointly refine actions. Our key insight is that structured action latents allow the VLM to read the current noisy action together with visual observations and language instructions. At every denoising step, the VLM contextualizes the noisy action and guides the action expert through gated cross-attention, so that its guidance evolves with the action being generated. We further supervise the VLM to recover clean action latents from noisy ones, which gives it a direct action-learning signal and couples perception with control. Additionally, joint denoising enables JADE to generate actions with fewer sampling steps, helping offset the additional cost of VLM participation. An adaptive step-size schedule further balances task performance and inference latency. On RoboDojo, JADE achieves an average success rate of 12.67%, the highest among the evaluated $\pi_{0.5}$-based methods, and is competitive with recent VLA and WAM foundation models pretrained at scale. It also improves performance on RoboTwin 2.0 and raises the average success rate across four real-world bimanual tasks by 20.8 percentage points compared with $\pi_{0.5}$.

  • Do Embodied VLMs Actually Use Their Scene-Graph Memory? RO/AI/CV
    Shubh Garg, Divyansh Pandey, Vedant Khandelwal, Niyati Rawal et al. · arXiv:2611.02056
    Abstract

    Embodied agents that answer questions about their route need a memory of what they saw, and scene graphs are a natural form for it. A vision-language model (VLM) can receive such a graph as text or through a graph encoder, but accuracy alone cannot show whether the graph holds the episode's facts, reaches the model, or is actually relied on. In this diagnostic study, we introduce a graph-swap test for the last of these: each episode is given another episode's graph, and we count how many answers change. On an embodied question-answering benchmark with routes through two to seven rooms, we use it to compare ways of delivering scene-graph memory to a fine-tuned VLM. As text, the memory is used strongly, adding 30 points on route questions. As a graph, it is not, and we trace why: the encoder preserves the information, but standard pooled and late fusion discard it. Giving each node its own input token recovers part of it, a gain of 8.3 points on route questions that the swap test confirms comes from the graph. The test also exposes a shortcut that accuracy hides: when the same facts appear in the text, the model ignores its graph. The test thus offers a cheap check of whether a VLM uses its scene-graph memory, and our analysis points to how that memory should be delivered.

  • D&A: Decoupling Description and Assessment Enables VLMs as Zero-Shot Robotic Reward Models RO/AI
    Marius Memmel, Shawn Yin, Urvi Rutia, Angad Wadhwa et al. · arXiv:2611.03622
    Abstract

    Vision-Language Models (VLMs) hold promise for zero-shot video task-progress estimation in robot learning, yet existing frameworks frequently suffer from confirmation bias, hallucinating progress in failed executions due to implicit success assumptions or relative temporal ranking designs. Naive prompting of frontier models collapses into a binary success detector that fails to capture nuanced, non-linear progress and failures. While post-training approaches can mitigate this through explicit training, they demand expensive real-world robotics data and require computationally intensive post-training for every individual base model. To resolve these limitations, we introduce D&A (Describe & Assess): our approach decouples progress estimation into an objective, task-grounded textual description of scene dynamics followed by an assessment phase using explicit rubrics. By compelling the VLM to synthesize an explicit, text-based definition of absolute success before scoring, the framework anchors evaluations to an external, global criterion rather than the video's internal timeline. Empirical results demonstrate that our method calibrates zero-shot estimates and mitigates confirmation bias, providing fine-grained, partial progress metrics that accurately penalize failed executions while correctly interpolating successful runs. Being purely prompt-based, D&A generalizes across tasks, embodiments, and benchmarks---matching or surpassing post-trained reward models---and its reward estimates enable efficient downstream reinforcement learning.

  • FOCUS: Closed-Loop Attention Feedback for Efficient Vision-Language Understanding AI/CV
    Akul Santhosh, Aastha Jhunjhunwala · arXiv:2611.01438
    Abstract

    Processing every video frame through Vision-Language Models (VLMs) adds significant inference cost and limits their ability to comprehend real-time video. Existing approaches either sub-sample frames uniformly, ignoring content and missing important events, or prune visual tokens inside the VLM to cut per-call cost while still invoking the model on every sampled frame. We introduce FOCUS, a training-free three-layer framework that reduces the number of invocations and steers each one towards what matters. Temporal gating measures inter-frame dissimilarity in the patch-embedding space of a smaller VLM from the same family, triggering burst captures only when the scene changes in a semantically meaningful way. Patch-level dissimilarity is then accumulated across the burst and fused with a top-down attention prior into a spatial focus map that encodes where motion and semantic relevance coincide. That map calibrates the VLM's value cache during generation, progressively amplifying vision tokens in task-relevant regions and attenuating off-target ones, without removing any token from the context. The loop closes after each response: text-to-vision cross-attention is extracted from the decoder and fed back as the prior for the next burst, letting the system converge on semantically important regions over successive observations. We evaluate FOCUS on the 500-video EgoSchema subset in streaming mode, with a single Qwen3-VL-8B backbone shared by every method. It reaches 74.8% accuracy using 25.4 VLM calls per clip, against 74.6% with 55 calls for uniform sampling and 74.2% with 55 calls for BOLT. On egocentric video captioning across four domains, it matches the best baseline's quality while issuing 57% fewer VLM invocations than uniform sampling.

  • Seeing What Matters, Verifying What Pays: Load-Adaptive Speculative Decoding for Vision-Language Models AI/CV
    Hong Nie, Zhangchi Gao, Yuqian Liang, Jun Yu et al. · arXiv:2611.01617
    Abstract

    Speculative decoding accelerates vision-language model (VLM) inference without altering the output distribution, yet two factors limit its gains. Existing drafters either lack the image evidence required for visually grounded tokens or pay to condition on every visual token, although a short draft block typically depends on only a few image regions. Moreover, the value of verifying additional candidates varies with GPU load, so no fixed verification structure is efficient across serving conditions. We present Uni-LaSpec, a block-parallel speculative decoding framework that addresses both factors. To improve draft quality, Uni-LaSpec reuses the verifier's own attention: the query retained from the preceding verification ranks the cached, int8-quantized visual keys, and a cross-layer vote selects a few visual tokens that the drafter fuses through gated cross-attention. Acceptance-aware training further weights each draft position by its contribution to the accepted prefix. To allocate verification, a marginal-cost criterion selects the structure per request and per round: draft trees exploit idle verifier capacity under light load, whereas a dynamic head truncates linear drafts under heavy load. Across six VLMs from three model families and 17 text and multimodal benchmarks, selecting eight visual tokens retains at least 98.6% of the acceptance obtained from the full image at sub-millisecond selection cost. With greedy decoding at concurrency one, Uni-LaSpec achieves up to 5.11x text and 3.24x multimodal throughput over autoregressive decoding. Across six load levels and three GPU types, it outperforms autoregressive decoding in every setting and matches or exceeds the best fixed verification strategy in 123 of 144 settings; under bursty arrivals, it reduces mean end-to-end latency by 47.6% relative to linear verification.

  • TriPhysBench: Evaluating Physical Intelligence for Embodied Agents AI/CV
    Zhongxiang Sun, Chenghao Yang, Haojie Ding, Boheng Zhang et al. · arXiv:2611.01934
    Abstract

    Embodied agents need physical intelligence to understand their surroundings through physical perception, physical knowledge retrieval, and physical reasoning. These capabilities must work together to interpret events and make quantitative judgments. Existing benchmarks advance physical understanding and general-purpose tool use, yet the coordination of these capabilities in physical scenarios remains insufficiently characterized. Inspired by Popper's Three Worlds, we introduce TriPhysBench, a benchmark of 410 questions from 200 videos that brings physical perception, physical knowledge retrieval, and physical reasoning into a shared evaluation setting. Its measurement-grounded and knowledge-grounded subtasks impose complementary demands on obtaining observations and acquiring external specifications, then applying them to quantitative problems. Among six representative vision-language models, the best achieves an answer accuracy of only 50.38%, revealing limited performance on tasks that require these capabilities to work together. We further introduce TriPhys, a framework that learns a unified policy for physical perception, knowledge retrieval, and quantitative reasoning from complete tool-integrated trajectories. The policy uses intermediate reasoning to guide retrieval and computation, then incorporates tool feedback into subsequent physical inference. On 110 questions from videos excluded from training, TriPhys improves answer and solution scores across three Qwen configurations, with a 17.7% relative gain in answer accuracy for TriPhys-27B over its Qwen3.6-27B backbone. This work provides a benchmark and a learning pathway toward embodied agents that move beyond recognizing events to understanding and reasoning about the physical processes behind them.

    Abstract
