Second, existing DM systems lack a natural language interface for driving risks. To close these gaps, we propose Safety4DM, a safety-oriented language system for VRC dynamic maps.   • GeoGuesser Bench: Benchmarking Vision-Language Agents on Interactive Geolocation AI
    Huanzhi Mao, Yichuan Wang, Michael Koo, Wenyuan Huang et al. · arXiv:2611.02698
    Abstract

    Vision-language model (VLM) agents that act in the open world need two abilities: world knowledge to interpret what they see, and exploration to find what they cannot yet see. We study how agents combine the two through interactive visual geolocation. GeoGuesser Bench contains 282 tasks in an offline Street View environment where agents rotate, zoom, and move between real panoramas. Its three task types shift the balance from knowledge to exploration: agents (1) report the coordinates of their starting location, (2) name the enclosing street, city, state, or country, or (3) find, read, and count nearby objects. The coordinate tasks in (1) also include 42 real GeoGuessr World Championship rounds, whose expert guesses provide a human reference. We evaluate 23 VLMs: the best accuracy is 62.5% on place naming and 35.0% on object finding, and the best agent averages 4352.4 GeoGuessr points on the championship rounds, against 4770.3 for the better expert in each round. Beyond these headline numbers, world knowledge and exploration are only weakly correlated across models: agents that localize well do not necessarily explore well. To understand where agents fail, we categorize their errors into vision, navigation, and tool-use failures. Further ablations show that the strongest agents, such as Claude Opus 4.8, still perform well with limited views, while weaker agents degrade; customizing the environment thus adjusts task difficulty. The environment's verifiable rewards and restorable state also support training, and our Planner-Worker-Compressor harness cuts planner tokens by 57-61% by compacting the interaction history. We hope GeoGuesser Bench and its environment serve as a high-quality foundation for simulating real-world exploration at scale.

    Guangyuan Dong, Lingdong Shen, Xudong Zhang, Haodong Jing et al. · arXiv:2611.02896
    Abstract

    Remote sensing image fusion and semantic interpretation are usually optimized separately, so reconstruction quality need not preserve downstream retrieval utility. We propose LANCE, which uses structured language as an explicit, indexable semantic state. A frozen vision-language model maps the input to a closed-vocabulary text record; a language-conditioned generator fuses the imagery; and a dense verifier with a differentiable continuous summarizer re-describes the output. Text-text consistency and anti-collapse contrastive text-mask losses with negative queries close the loop without dense annotations or VLM fine-tuning. A variational EM formulation yields a per-step contraction result under regularity assumptions, distinguishing semantic faithfulness from verifier overfitting. Across seven test sets on GF2, QuickBird, WorldView-III, and WorldView-II, LANCE improves reduced-scale reference metrics by up to 11.7% (median 2.1%) over the strongest learning baseline. Text perturbations, cross-verifier transfer, and pseudo-RGB ablations examine the mechanisms underlying these gains. The framework connects image fusion to verifiable semantic representations for multimodal retrieval and knowledge management.

  • Same Outputs, Different Scores: Auditing the Unchecked Graders of Multimodal LLM Benchmarks AI/CV
    Ahmad Jarjis Hasan, Jonathan Lwowski · arXiv:2611.03377
    Abstract

    Every multimodal-LLM multiple-choice benchmark ends with a scoring rule that converts a model's response, often free-form text rather than a clean option letter, into a right/wrong verdict, buried in its code. Unlike the dataset, this rule is chosen without justification and left unexamined, yet it sets the published number. We reproduce the official scorers of eight peer-reviewed benchmarks on five open vision-language models (VLMs) that fit a single 16 GB GPU, and introduce a blinded human-plus-LLM-judge adjudication (Cohen's kappa=0.956) that recovers what the model actually answered. The scoring rule alone moves accuracy by up to 22 points on identical outputs, yet exactly 0 wherever a model emits clean option letters, so it hides on strong-model leaderboards. It is the fallback and not the rambling that does the damage: the benchmark with the highest non-compliance we measure has an artifact of exactly zero, because its scorer abstains instead of guessing; a reasoning model with non-compliance 0.000 still swings 9.5 points with which regex match of its trace the parser keeps. No standard rule is reliable: snapping a non-answer to its closest option credits answers never given yet buries correct ones, abstention discards answers stated in words, and likelihood scoring follows answer-token priors, so a single grade conflates capability with luck rather than measuring either. We release all code, labels, and the protocol, and argue benchmarks should report a non-compliance rate and at least two rules, not one unexamined grade.

    Yiting Duan, Miaoxin Wang, Aoqing Guo, Bo Peng · arXiv:2611.00036
    Abstract

    Visual-prefill energy conclusions for edge vision-language models (VLMs) are commonly drawn under single-shot workloads; we revisit them under continuous perception. Profiling nine VLMs across three architecture families on Jetson AGX Thor and Orin (4,508 records, calibrated with a pure-text task), we find two mutually independent energy laws: the per-output-token decode cost grows monotonically with model scale, whereas the visual-prefill (image-encoding) cost is dominated by visual-encoder architectural efficiency, not scale. Under continuous perception ($N{=}10$) with sparse output, visual-prefill energy reaches 92--97\% of total. We derive optimization strategies---visual frame reuse, adaptive output truncation, task-aware routing, and encoder throughput optimization---for edge VLM deployment.

  • Think Before You Locate: Clinical Chain-of-Though Supervision for Sequential Medical Image Grounding CV/AI
    Adinath Madhavrao Dukre, Govinda Kolli, Yifan Lu, Ziyun Zou et al. · arXiv:2611.00041
    Abstract

    Clinicians rarely localize a finding from one image alone. They compare current and prior scans, follow structures across slices, and match regions across views or modalities. Yet vision-language models for sequential medical grounding usually learn only from bounding boxes. Boxes show where the target is, but they do not explain why that region in that image answers the question. We study whether explicit, image-grounded reasoning can improve localization, and which tasks benefit from it. We introduce method, whose core is MedChain, a corpus of 182,573 filtered rationales spanning eight sequential grounding tasks and ten imaging modalities. A medical VLM (FlemingVL-38B) first drafts a caption-like rationale, and a multimodal refiner (Qwen3.5-397B-A17B) then re-examines the images, question and annotated box to write a box-conditioned explanation with explicit cross-image reasoning. The 27B model reaches 77.24 mIoU and 85.20 Acc@0.5, improving over the strongest prior model by 4.69 and 5.49 points. In a paired comparison using fixed 4B checkpoints, dCoT-SFT improves over answer-only SFT by 3.56 mIoU (95% CI 3.13--4.00). The gains mainly come from change detection and object tracking, while performance drops on three appearance-matching tasks. Replacing a model's own rationale with a rationale from another task reduces mIoU by 12.2 points, showing that localization depends strongly on the reasoning context. Finally, adding GRPO with an IoU reward after dCoT-SFT gives only small and task-dependent gains, which we analyze through controlled multi-seed development experiments.

  • DTP-OPSD: Reinforcement Learning with Distractor-Aware Temporal-Privilege On-Policy Self-Distillation for Temporal Video Grounding CV/AI
    Zhaoyu Chen, Yongwei Nie · arXiv:2611.00120
    Abstract

    Temporal Video Grounding (TVG) aims to localize the start and end times of an event described by a language query in a video. The task becomes particularly challenging when the same video contains multiple repeated, similar, or opposite events. On these hard examples, all responses sampled by Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) may miss the target and receive zero temporal Intersection-over-Union (tIoU). Their group-relative advantages are then all zero, leaving no useful policy gradient signal. To address this, we propose DTP-OPSD, a reinforcement learning framework with distractor-aware temporal-privilege on-policy self-distillation. The student observes the clean video and generates responses on-policy, while a shared-weight teacher observes a privileged video in which the target window is marked by red full-frame borders and same-video distractor windows by blue full-frame borders. For the distillation loss, the teacher evaluates student-generated prefixes and provides dense token-level Jensen-Shannon divergence supervision. This supervision remains informative even when GRPO provides no policy gradient signal. We suppress distillation when the teacher does not outperform the student and strengthen it for groups where the teacher performs better but all student rollouts receive zero tIoU. We also construct a compact hard-interference training set through VLM screening and human verification. With only 300 additional post-training examples, DTP-OPSD achieves the highest mIoU among the compared open-source 8B models on Charades-TimeLens, ActivityNet-TimeLens, and QVHighlights-TimeLens, reaching 55.93, 54.77, and 66.71, respectively. Compared with Pure GRPO trained on the same data, DTP-OPSD improves all 12 reported metrics, with R1@0.7 gains of 1.28, 1.62, and 1.78 points on the three benchmarks. Code will be released at this https URL

  • LF-VideoQA: Evaluating Long-Video Understanding with Long-Form Theory-of-Mind Questions CV/AI
    Shaden Shaar, Bradon Thymes, Majd Aldaye, Claire Cardie et al. · arXiv:2611.00383
    Abstract

    The hardest part of understanding a long video is not pinpointing any one moment but synthesizing many, e.g., answering a question that requires tracking what develops across hours of content and articulating how the pieces fit together. To date, most long-video QA datasets require only a short answer or a multiple-choice selection, oversimplifying the long-video understanding task to retrieval. We introduce LF-VideoQA, a dataset for long-form, open-ended question answering over long narrative videos built on theory-of-mind questions: a character's belief depends on everything they have seen or been told, so each question demands assembling and reasoning from evidence distributed across the video. The dataset contains more than 6,300 questions on 94 recent films; each requires reasoning over scenes spanning an average of 23 minutes and comes with a question-specific rubric for recall and precision scoring. The dataset is created entirely automatically by annotating screenplays with scene events and each character's first-order beliefs and meta-beliefs, then generating (question, answer, rubric) triples through LLM-based question generation and verification; human studies validate their quality. We evaluate four families of state-of-the-art open-source VLMs across three settings and find the task highly challenging: (i) even an oracle with access to the screenplay falls well short of complete answers; (ii) the best non-oracle model attains barely half of the recall level of the oracle; (iii) unlike on prior long-video benchmarks, captions and subtitles underperform the video itself, indicating that the questions need visual evidence; and (iv) recall is the predominant bottleneck, with answers omitting many required rubric claims.

  • Words Before Pixels: Selective Modality Routing for Vision-Language Model Unlearning CV/AI
    Laura Yao, Haochen Zhang, Jinhao Duan, Sijia Liu et al. · arXiv:2611.00608
    Abstract

    Vision-language model (VLM) unlearning is often treated as an objective-design problem: given multimodal forget data, the goal is to optimize a loss that removes undesirable behavior while preserving retained capabilities. We argue that this view overlooks an equally important data-centric question: how should each forget example be represented before optimization? Existing methods typically keep image-conditioned failures in their original image-text form, implicitly assuming that pixels are the right route for unlearning whenever pixels appear in the input. We challenge this assumption by constructing modality-decomposed versions of VLM benchmarks, enabling controlled comparisons among image-only, text-only, and multimodal representations of the same examples. Our modality-mixing experiments show that unsafe instruction-following behavior is more effectively suppressed through textual representations than through images alone, while purely text-only unlearning can still weaken visual grounding when the target behavior depends on visual evidence. Motivated by this trade-off, we introduce ModRoute, a gradient-guided per-sample modality routing strategy that selectively converts high visual-pressure examples to text-only form while keeping the remaining examples multimodal. Notably, on VLGuard with LLaVA-1.5-7B, ModRoute has the strongest composite unlearning-utility score of 0.847 at $\gamma=0.5$, a 2.5% improvement over the best random-switching score and $\geq$46% better than pure multimodal or text unlearning. Overall, our results show that effective VLM unlearning depends not only on the objective, but also on the composition and per-sample modality routing of the unlearning data.

  • Ripple: Deadline-Aware CPU-GPU Sharing for Embodied Inference AI
    Jiadong Zhang, Xiaosong Ma · arXiv:2611.01067
    Abstract

    Unified-memory edge devices increasingly run LLM/VLM inference alongside time-critical CPU tasks for perception and control. GPU memory traffic can overwhelm these tasks, yet platforms such as DGX Spark expose neither bandwidth counters nor direct bandwidth regulation. We present Ripple, a CPU-GPU co-scheduler that combines model-derived GPU memory volumes with CPU completion feedback to regulate interference. Ripple divides inference into pausable, layer-based units and translates each CPU job's deadline slack into a GPU-byte budget, admitting work within the budgets of all overlapping jobs. Online updates track changing interference, while deadline-aware pacing and judicious SM footprint narrowing preserve inference progress. Implemented with SGLang engine hooks and a central scheduler, we evaluate Ripple on DGX Spark with four robotic CPU task mixes and LLM/VLM inference. It is the only evaluated approach that consistently meets the 2% CPU deadline-miss target and VLM deadlines across the tested deadline settings while retaining best-effort LLM throughput.

  • See, Think, Use: Diagnosing Perception and Reasoning Failures in VLMs CV/AI
    Jiacheng Ding, Yanliang Qi, Cong Guo, Peihua Mai · arXiv:2611.01467
    Abstract

    We present See, Think, Use, a protocol that traces each answer of a vision-language model to perception, reasoning or the use of perception. The diagnosis shows a developer which stage to improve and directs test-time compute to the stage that fails. Benchmarks score the final answer, and an answer that rests on the image earns the same credit as a guess or a memorized prior. The protocol asks the model two extra questions per item and needs no access to its weights. A localization probe asks for a box around every instance and measures seeing, and a text-replacement probe answers from a description and measures thinking. Use is the rate at which the answer follows the model's own perception across counterfactual image pairs. The rate needs no answer labels, because the question and its prior are the same on both images. On two model families and six datasets the protocol shows that the knob that helps follows the task family. Answers follow the model's perception except on images that contradict a common prior, and asking the model to localize first repairs this failure when its boxes are right. Allocating compute by the measured stages comes close to the best uniform policy at lower cost and beats a random allocation of the same compute wherever a knob raises the short stage. We will release the code, the prompts and the per-question outputs.

MR safety status of devices from AccessGUDID device records; 5. line and tube position on RANZCR CLiP chest radiographs; 6. motion-artifact severity on MR-ART T1-weighted brain MRI and 7. sequence identification on BraTS 2021 brain MRI. The primary endpoint was AUROC. There was no fine-tuning of the models on the tasks. Results: All models completed all tests without a failed request. All retrieved findings from reports almost perfectly (AUROC >0.95). Clef ranked higher than its Qwen backbone on CT-RATE volumes and RANZCR lower on MR-ART. The two did not differ on BraTS, GUDID or PadChest, and differed by 0.002 on CT-RATE reports. On BraTS, Clef correctly identified 90 of 100 FLAIR volumes while Qwen never chose it. d1 ranked lower than Clef on six of seven tasks. No model performed well on motion severity. Clef was faster per request than Qwen on tasks with many questions per input. d1 used 3 to 9 times less energy per request than Clef. Conclusions: System 1 models can perform important tasks in radiology with high accuracy, at lower compute cost than LLMs used in the usual way. When an LLM is constrained to a single answer token, its cost and performance is similar to System 1 models unless multiple questions are asked. A 3B System 1 model was close to the 27B models on report text but not on images. No model performed well on motion severity.

  • Benchmarking Vision-Language Models for Urdu Nastaliq Handwriting Recognition CV/AI
    Haseeb Yaqoob, Fatima Azfar, Emaan Arif Khan · arXiv:2611.01635
    Abstract

    Handwritten Urdu is hard to recognize because the cursive Nastaliq script has no fixed baseline and no clear character boundaries. We benchmark six systems on the Urdu Nastaliq Handwritten Dataset (UNHD): Tesseract (default configuration), a script-specific recognizer trained on printed Urdu (UTRNet-Large), two open-source vision-language models (VLMs), and two commercial VLMs. All six systems are evaluated without fine-tuning on 2,465 single-line images (Part A), and the four VLMs receive the same fixed prompt (decoding uses temperature 0 where configurable, except for Claude Sonnet 5, whose decoding cannot be configured). Both commercial VLMs reach lower error than the printed-Urdu recognizer and the open-source VLMs. We do not compare against handwriting-trained recognizers, which would likely do better in-domain; we characterize zero-shot behavior, not state of the art. We also describe three distinct failure modes among the ranked systems. In one of them, a commercial model explicitly declines to transcribe a few very hard-to-read images instead of guessing. In a second study (Part B), VLMs correct the output of a separate OCR system using both the source image and the noisy text. Once separated from each model's own reading ability, the benefit is small on Tesseract's output (0.7% to 7.4%) and larger (17.9% and 21.9%) only for the two commercial correctors given UTRNet's text. It appears to depend on how legible the supplied text already is, and a weaker corrector can make a more legible text worse.

  • Reconstruction as Spatial Evaluation: Diagnosing Cross-View Spatial Intelligence in Large Vision-Language Models CV/AI
    Junfei Wu, Qiuyue Wang, Xuhong Huang, Qihang Peng et al. · arXiv:2611.02026
    Abstract

    The spatial intelligence of large vision-language models (LVLMs) requires the ability to combine local visual observations into a geometrically consistent representation of the physical world. Many static benchmarks assess isolated spatial judgments, such as relative direction or distance, while interactive embodied evaluations combine perception with exploration, planning, control, and simulator-specific execution. To bridge this gap, we introduce RECONSPATIAL, a benchmark centered on multi-view 3D scene reconstruction across indoor, tabletop, and outdoor environments. Given real multi-view images and target-object queries, LVLMs are required to jointly predict oriented 3D bounding boxes in a shared, camera-anchored metric coordinate frame. Reconstructions are evaluated along two complementary axes: Structure for relative layout and Metric for physical scale and camera-anchored placement. Nine atomic spatial QA tasks, grouped into Geometric Perception and Spatial Relations, probe spatial capabilities involved in joint reconstruction. Offline navigation extends evaluation to path planning. Evaluation of 18 frontier LVLMs reveals a consistent gap between relative scene structure and metric reconstruction; even GPT-6-Astra scores 81.4% on Structure but only 58.7% on Metric. Tracing this gap, diagnostic analyses link atomic spatial capabilities to reconstruction quality and show that relational consistency alone does not ensure metric accuracy. Experiments with 3D map injection further separate scene construction from path planning, revealing how these geometric errors limit navigation success. Finally, spatial fine-tuning yields broad transfer across both spatial and general vision-language benchmarks.

  • EarthJev: Calibrated, Non-Generative Answers to Natural-Language Questions about Sentinel-2 Land Cover CV/AI
    Domenico Benfenati, Antonio Maria Rinaldi, Cristian Tommasino · arXiv:2611.02096
    Abstract

    Vision-language models for remote sensing answer questions about satellite images by generating text: every answer costs a decoding loop, has to be parsed back to an option, and comes without a usable probability. We present EarthJev, a 0.8B-parameter model that answers typed questions about multispectral Sentinel-2 patches without generating any token. All 12 bands enter the vision encoder of Qwen3.5-0.8B through a widened input layer initialised from the RGB kernels, and, following the open OneJev recipe, the answer is read from the logits of the option letters at a single position and trained with cross-entropy plus Brier score. EarthJev answers a question in 19.9 ms, 4.3 to 8.7 times faster than the multimodal language models and 6.8 times faster than its own backbone used generatively; when all the questions about an image are asked together, it answers them in 42.8 ms, 3.7 to 10.1 times faster than the batched generative models. On 36,028 test questions it reaches 86.0% accuracy (three seeds), 0.6 and 1.2 points below the two 2B multimodal models and 3.5 points below the remote-sensing EarthDial-4B, with answers that are valid by construction and calibrated probabilities (ECE 0.006). Replacing the nine non-RGB bands with their mean costs 4.5 points, so the model uses the multispectral input, and in an ablation training the whole vision encoder raises accuracy to 88.4% at the same cost.   • Exploiting Textual Semantics for Robust Cross-View Object Correspondence CV/AI
    Bing Fan, Yunhe Feng, Yan Huang, Heng Fan · arXiv:2611.02356
    Abstract

    Cross-view object correspondence (CVOC) aims to establish associations of the same target between query and search videos captured from different viewpoints. Existing methods often rely on visual cues for CVOC. Despite recent progress, these methods struggle in complicated scenarios with substantial viewpoint changes and appearance variations, due to lacking sufficient target information. Addressing this, we introduce TeSCo, a framework that exploits rich Textual Semantics of an object, in addition to its visual cues, for cross-view object Correspondence. Our key insight is, textual description of a target, by capturing diverse attributes such as category, texture, and color, provides viewpoint- and appearance-invariant semantics, which are complementary to visual cues and can hence enhance correspondence robustness in complicated scenes. Inspired by this, TeSCo first generates a textual expression of the target from the given query view using a vision-language model, and then extracts textual feature to enhance visual features for localization in the search view. To realize this, we introduce a text-conditioned cross-modal fusion (TCF) module that incorporates the textual feature into visual features with text-guided modulation, producing more robust multimodal target representation for object correspondence. Since not all textual cues are equally compatible with the target in current search-view frame, we propose an iterative textual feature refinement (ITFR) module that progressively adjusts textual feature using search-view information before the TCF module, enabling search-view-aware textual semantics to be fused into visual features and leading to better performance. In extensive experiments on Ego-Exo4D and HANDAL-X, TeSCo demonstrates state-of-the-art results and largely outperforms other models, validating its efficacy. Our code is released at this https URL.

  • PlantVLM-Edge: Quantized Vision-Language Models for On-Device Crop Health Diagnosis CV/AI
    Gaurab Wagle, Bishal Adhikari, Jiajia Li, Xiaolei Huang et al. · arXiv:2611.03557
    Abstract

    Timely and accurate crop health diagnosis is essential for sustainable agricultural production, yet accurate diagnosis often requires expert knowledge that is not readily available. Recent vision-language models (VLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in visual understanding, reasoning, and natural-language interaction, creating new opportunities for intelligent crop health assessment. However, deploying VLMs on resource-constrained devices remains challenging due to their substantial computational and memory requirements. This paper presents PlantVLM-Edge, a quantized vision-language framework for conversational crop health diagnosis that can run entirely on resource-constrained edge devices. PlantVLM-Edge combines agricultural domain adaptation, post-training quantization, and on-device serving with an offline chat interface, allowing growers to ask open-ended questions and receive diagnoses, symptom explanations, and management guidance without cloud connectivity. The framework is backbone-agnostic: we validate it with two different VLM types with varying scale and architecture (Qwen3-VL-2B and LLaVA-1.5-7B), four quantization schemes, and two diagnostic domains (plant diseases and insect pests), and we deploy all variants on an NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin. Eight-bit quantization affects answer quality minimally relative to the full-precision model, and 4-bit activation-aware weight quantization (AWQ) retains 98.6% of full-precision answer quality on both domains while reducing the weight memory of the 2B backbone by 49%. In its most compact form, PlantVLM-Edge requires 2.01 GiB of weight memory, returns its first token in about 0.12 s, and completes a 64-token diagnostic response in 0.78 s.   • The Margin Knows the Background: Uneven Abstention in Frozen Vision-Language Models CV
    Satwik Sai Prakash Sahoo, Bibhor Puhan, Shubhankar Mahanta · arXiv:2611.00026
    Abstract

    Selective prediction allows a model to abstain on uncertain inputs, but a single confidence threshold can disproportionately reject examples from particular groups. We study how confidence scores produce this imbalance in frozen vision-language models (OpenAI CLIP, LAION CLIP, SigLIP). We express a family of scores, including the margin and maximum logit, through the average and the difference of the two highest class logits, and ask which carries group information. We show that equal group coverage at every threshold is equivalent to independence between the score and group membership. A randomized margin baseline tests whether a reduction in coverage disparity beats what adding noise achieves. With score choices fixed before testing, the effect of choice depends on the dataset. On Waterbirds, the margin creates coverage gaps 2.5 to 5.5 times larger than maximum logit at 80% coverage, its group signal is in the difference component, and paired background swaps show the background moves it, although maximum logit increases overall error. On NICO++, maximum logit instead increases worst-context error by 5.4 to 6.6 points and offers no improvement over the randomized baseline for OpenAI CLIP. Across both datasets, how far a score pushes a group down its ranking tracks the coverage gap (Spearman 0.88, 0.92) better than plain group identifiability (0.61, 0.65). These results explain why a score that abstains more evenly on one dataset may be less effective on another and motivate judging group coverage and selective risk together against randomized baselines.

  • SIMIT: Self-Improving Vision-Language Models via Imagination at Test-Time CV
    Mehmet Onurcan Kaya, Desmond Elliott, Dim P. Papadopoulos · arXiv:2611.00053
    Abstract

    We introduce SIMIT, a test-time self-improvement framework in which a vision-language model creates its own query-specific training data before answering an unlabeled test query. Our goal is to improve performance on a given target test set without annotations or external models. To pursue this goal, some test-time methods improve answers through longer reasoning or repeated sampling but leave the model unchanged. Others update the model using rewards based on majority agreement among multiple candidate answers or the model's own judgments of correctness, but such rewards can reinforce existing mistakes. SIMIT instead imagines similar problems whose solutions it controls: a model need not correctly solve the target query to construct useful practice examples. It synthesizes diverse (question, answer, image) triplets by specifying each answer before realizing its image. To support diverse multimodal tasks, SIMIT combines native image generation with an extensible skill library for structured visuals, including documents, charts, and diagrams. Within this pipeline, multi-stage verification checks that images support their assigned answers, while difficulty filtering retains informative examples. Adaptive budgeting further targets this practice by allocating more samples to queries the model is less confident in answering. The resulting data can improve the model in-context (SIMIT-ICL) or in-weight (SIMIT-FT). Across 17 diverse benchmarks, SIMIT outperforms existing self-improvement methods. Its best configuration achieves a +7.20% mean relative gain over BAGEL-7B, while training-free SIMIT-ICL achieves +6.98%.

  • CausalPrune: Counterfactual Causal Token Pruning for Efficient Large Vision-Language Models CV
    Pengcheng Zheng, Chaoning Zhang, Ya Wen, Jiaxin Huang et al. · arXiv:2611.00058
    Abstract

    Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) have achieved remarkable progress in multimodal understanding, but their practical deployment is still limited by the substantial computation introduced by excessive visual tokens. Visual token pruning offers an effective solution by removing redundant visual tokens during inference. However, existing methods typically estimate token importance from correlational statistics, which cannot distinguish tokens that causally determine the model output from those spuriously associated with it. To address this issue, we propose \textbf{CausalPrune}, a counterfactual causal token pruning framework for efficient LVLMs. We formulate LVLM inference as a Structural Causal Model and introduce the Causal Token Importance Score (CTIS), which estimates the Average Treatment Effect of intervening on each visual token. To make causal estimation practical, we derive an efficient first-order approximation of CTIS and incorporate backdoor adjustment to mitigate latent cross-modal confounding. Within this causal formulation, layer-wise causal mediation decomposes token importance into direct and indirect effects through intermediate hidden states, enabling progressive pruning across transformer layers in a principled manner. Extensive experiments on various LVLM architectures, such as LLaVA-1.5 and Qwen2.5-VL, demonstrate that CausalPrune maintains comparable or even superior multimodal performance while achieving substantial reductions in FLOPs and prefill latency, consistently outperforming existing pruning methods.

    Abstract

    Training-free adaptation of medical vision-language models (LVLMs) is a deployment requirement, yet visual in-context learning (ICL) injects modality- and style-mismatched demonstrations into medical contexts, teaching a frozen model the wrong answering style. MADS selects modality-anatomy aligned demonstrations that cover complementary interpretation dimensions; W2C curates them with weak retrieval signals and a strong verification vote that checks demonstrations rather than the model's own outputs; ECC answers with W anchored demonstration sets, aggregates by abnormality-aware signatures, and re-answers once on conflict.   • OCRia-1 Technical Report CV
    Aman Rangapur, Antonio Jimeno Yepes, Daniel Bustamante, Nutchapol Dendumrongsup et al. · arXiv:2611.00092
    Abstract

    We introduce OCRia-1, a 4B vision-language model (VLM) for document parsing that excels at preserving table structure, extracting chart data and retaining text formatting. OCRia-1 handles content that has traditionally been challenging in document parsing: merged cells and spanning headers, tables with thousands of cells, chart data, and meaningful text formatting such as bold, italics, subscripts and superscripts, preserving details needed for business-critical document analysis. At the time of this report, it achieves the highest scores on tables, charts and text formatting among the systems evaluated, including a 35B model and Mistral OCR 4.1. Its mean ParseBench score over the four dimensions we evaluate is 0.87, against 0.75 for the next-best system. We emphasize data quality and reward design in training OCRia-1. Synthetic pages provide exact ground truth for long tables and rich formatting, while real pages are transcribed using a pipeline grounded in the PDF text layer. We attribute OCRia-1's performance primarily to careful data curation that prioritizes quality and diversity over quantity. Dimension-specific rewards evaluate table reconstruction, chart-data extraction and text-formatting fidelity. We train the model on the curated data using supervised fine-tuning (SFT), then refine it through reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). To our knowledge, OCRia-1 is the fastest published document-parsing VLM in its class, decoding at about 4,700 tokens per second with a single request on one B200 GPU, about nine times faster than the base model on stock vLLM. This is achieved through a block-diffusion speculator, quantization and custom kernels.

  • SpatialVec: A Unified Representation Learning Framework for Geospatial Objects with Boundary-Aware Sampling CV
    Samya Acharja, Kanchan Chowdhury · arXiv:2611.00111
    Abstract

    Spatial representation learning is essential for Geospatial Artificial Intelligence (GeoAI) applications, enabling machine learning models to capture the geometry, location, and spatial relationships of geographic entities such as points, polylines, and polygons. Existing spatial encoding methods often struggle to preserve fine-grained topological information, particularly for interactions involving complex geometries such as polylines and polygons. These approaches inadequately capture boundary structure and local spatial context, causing topology-sensitive relationships near geometry boundaries to be underrepresented in the learned embedding space. Consequently, accurately modeling complex spatial interactions remains a significant challenge for downstream spatial reasoning tasks. To fill the gap, we introduce SpatialVec, a boundary-aware spatial representation learning framework that adaptively samples geometry boundaries and their surrounding regions, capturing fine-grained geometric structures critical for topology-aware spatial reasoning. A transformer-based encoder-decoder trained through geometry reconstruction produces compact, geometry-aware, and unified representations for points, polylines, and polygons. Empirical evaluation on two real-world datasets shows that SpatialVec substantially improves the preservation of challenging topological relationships involving polygonal interactions and outperforms state-of-the-art baselines significantly on downstream prediction and classification tasks.

  • SIEVE: Preserving Cross-Image Evidence via Depth-Aware Token Compression CV
    Nitish Shukla, Surgan Jandial, Nhat-Tan Bui, Arun Ross · arXiv:2611.00351
    Abstract

    Multi-image vision-language models (VLMs) incur substantial computation when processing long visual sequences. Compressing these sequences must preserve complementary evidence across images and keep tokens from different images separate. Global selection can underrepresent question-relevant images, and pruning in early decoder layers risks removing relevant content before sufficient multimodal processing, yet existing methods spend most of the budget on these early layers. We propose SIEVE, a training-free, single-pass framework that jointly allocates visual tokens across images and decoder depth. SIEVE first pools each image into a compact spatial grid to reduce early processing cost. It then progressively selects representatives using prompt attention under equal per-image quotas and aggregates unselected tokens into similar representatives from the same image. These operations keep every image represented and its tokens separate from other images, while refining the retained representations. A closed-form schedule sets retention counts to target a prescribed average token budget across decoder layers. Across four VLM backbones, SIEVE achieves the highest average at a 6.25% budget on five multi-image benchmarks and four single-image benchmarks, and the highest mean on two video benchmarks. On LLaVA-OneVision-7B, it outperforms the strongest baseline by 9.96 and 9.03 points at 6.25% and 12.5% budgets, and at 12.5% it exceeds the uncompressed model by 2.01 points with 6.6x fewer prefill FLOPs.

  • PRISM-Net: Dynamic Routing Network with VLM-Guided Disentangled Attribute Distillation for Explainable Fine-Grained Product Retrieval CV
    Chung-Ming Lo, Che-Wei Chang · arXiv:2611.00458
    Abstract

    Fine-grained visual product retrieval in large-scale e-commerce multimedia systems demands discriminative representations capable of simultaneously resolving macro-level category semantics and micro-level visual attributes such as structural contour (line) and chromatic appearance (color). However, conventional backbones including convolutional neural networks (CNNs) and vision Transformers (ViTs) inherently suffer from feature entanglement and shortcut learning. To learn the micro-level visual attributes without costly manual attribute annotations, we propose phase-routed interpretable semantic mixture-of-experts network (PRISM-Net), an explainable multi-expert architecture that disentangles holistic visual features into disentangled line and color expert pathways governed by a conditional meta-router. In phase 1, we introduce an external VLM-Guided sigmoid semantic distillation mechanism with gradient-detached routing gates, utilizing zero-shot text prompts from SigLIP to supervise specialized structural and chromatic experts. In phase 2, the disentangled expert embeddings are adaptively fused with macro base features via a learned meta-router gate to calibrate category boundaries. Extensive 5-fold cross-validation across 275,454 e-commerce images (18 categories) and full-scale retrieval benchmarks (55,091 queries against a 220,363-image gallery) across SwinV2, ConvNeXt-V2, and DINOv3 demonstrate substantial gains.   • Selecting or Solving? What Best-of-N Verification Delivers in Video Reasoning CV
    Martin Q. Ma, Yuxiao Qu, Ruslan Salakhutdinov, Louis-Philippe Morency et al. · arXiv:2611.00507
    Abstract

    Answering a complex question about a long video is a grounding problem: the answer turns on a few seconds of evidence, and vision-language models remain well below human accuracy at finding them. The standard way to spend extra test-time compute on this kind of video reasoning is best-of-N selection: sample several chain-of-thought answers from a fixed model, then let a second model re-watch the video and pick one. Such pipelines are reported by a single headline number, their margin over a majority vote. That number conflates two effects with opposite deployment consequences: the benefit the second model adds by selecting among candidates, and the benefit it would have supplied by solving the question itself. We separate them, measuring the Selection Increment (SI) -- selection accuracy minus the same verifier's accuracy with the candidates withheld -- alongside the margin over self-consistency, across five long-video benchmarks. The Selection Increment is largest for the weakest verifiers: a verifier that solves poorly on its own leaves the candidates the most room to help. Beating the majority vote follows the opposite profile: where the vote already sits near its ceiling no verifier we test improves on it; below that ceiling only the strongest do. On Video-Holmes, the two come apart on a single pool of candidates -- a weak verifier gains +19.3 points from the candidates yet lands 7.1 points below the vote, while the frontier verifier gains the least and beats the vote. The weakest verifier also shows a failure mode: on EgoSchema it picks by where an option sits in the list rather than by what the video shows. Read as a merit score, SI puts a verifier that fails to beat a majority vote at the top of its ranking; read as an audit, it says which of the two effects a headline number delivers. Code is available at this https URL.

    T N Rickesh, Pranav Vinodh, Sowmya Kamath S · arXiv:2611.00828
    Abstract

    Multi-camera driving question answering provides a discrete test of vision-language models, but post-training gains can reflect changed selected answers or improved answer extraction. A zero-shot diagnostic on 5,519 test questions spans effective-small, mixture-of-experts, unified, and dense checkpoints, with serving differences disclosed rather than interpreted as architecture effects.   • Fewer Tokens, More Answers: Verifiable-Reward Tuning of an Ornith-Style VLM for Multi-View Driving QA CV
    T N Rickesh, Pranav Vinodh, Sowmya Kamath S · arXiv:2611.00833
    Abstract

    Reasoning-enabled model derivatives must turn intermediate deliberation into a usable answer within a deployment budget. Paired archived outputs show that, on a selected 400-question development screen with a 256-token cap, GRPO increases answer extraction from 78.25% to 90.25% and accuracy from 55.50% to 63.75%. Of 39 wrong-to-correct transitions, 33 begin with an unextractable base response. The base's 87 unextractable responses all reach the token cap.   • Toward Counterfactual Learning for Selective Re-Examination in Agentic Visual Search CV
    Ao Chen, Shengchao Chen, Yan Luximon · arXiv:2611.00848
    Abstract

    Visual search over high-resolution images requires a vision-language model (VLM) not only to determine where to inspect, but also whether another observation is warranted before committing to an answer. Existing prompting, demonstrations, and outcome-based reinforcement learning can encourage re-examination, yet do not directly supervise whether it is beneficial at a particular search state. We introduce ReSee, a benchmark of re-examination decisions comprising 3,227 visual-search trajectories and 8,827 explicit commit-or-re-examine decisions, with counterfactual adjudication that distinguishes states where additional inspection is warranted from those where commitment is preferable. Building on this formulation, we propose CARE (Counterfactual Advantage for Re-Examination), a decision-level reinforcement learning method that compares paired continuations under commit and re-examine verdicts from the same search state. Their cost-aware return difference estimates the counterfactual value of an additional observation and is assigned only to the corresponding verdict tokens, providing localized credit for whether search should continue. Experiments expose limited re-examination selectivity in existing VLMs and show that CARE significantly improves accuracy and decision selectivity over GRPO across two backbones, with higher accuracy and fewer visual requests on six unseen OOD benchmarks.

  • Context-Shielded Backdoor Attack against Large Vision-Language Models CV
    Zhifang Zhang, Qiang Huang, Chenpeng Wang, Weitong Chen et al. · arXiv:2611.01252
    Abstract

    Large vision-language models (LVLMs) routinely use inference-time visual-token compression to prune hundreds of patch tokens down to a compact budget. Existing LVLM backdoors fire whenever a triggered image appears, whereas prior deployment-triggered attacks activate only when model weights are quantized or pruned. However, conventional LVLM backdoors exhibit nearly 100% attack success rate (ASR) under full-token inference, immediately exposing compromised checkpoints during pre-deployment auditing. We reveal context stripping: surrounding clean visual tokens suppress an embedded trigger at full length, whereas token pruning strips this context away and activates the backdoor. Motivated by this finding, we propose StripWake, a projector-only backdoor that remains dormant under full-token auditing yet activates across unseen compressors. Specifically, StripWake pairs clean-supervised full-token views with target-supervised randomized compressed views, and aligns early visual attention with a frozen benign teacher to preserve clean utility. On LLaVA-1.5-7B across nine unseen compressors, StripWake stays dormant at 0.4% full-token ASR yet achieves 94.0%-99.7% compressed ASR across five primary compressors without CIDEr loss.

  • StreamClarify: Proactive Clarification for Grounded Streaming Video Question Answering CV
    Zhuchen Shao, Bo Chen, Klara Nahrstedt · arXiv:2611.01263
    Abstract

    Streaming video question answering (QA) requires real-time reasoning over continuous video streams under strict latency and active-context budgets. Current streaming vision-language models (VLMs) often give confident yet ungrounded answers when queries are ambiguous or have false premises, or when relevant visual evidence is no longer in the active context. Proactive clarification can help, but must satisfy the same latency and memory constraints. We present StreamClarify, a training-free framework for grounded streaming QA that combines a pre-answer uncertainty check with hierarchical memory. The check uses the entropy of a binary pre-answer judgment as a lightweight uncertainty score to decide whether to answer directly or request clarification. To support re-answering, a temporal-pyramid KV cache maintains a bounded active visual context, while a CPU-side semantic retrieval archive provides access to historical frames. We further construct StreamClarify-QA, a diagnostic benchmark of 1,837 QA pairs from StreamBench and OVO-Bench, spanning standard, referential ambiguity, and unanswerable scenarios. Across three VLM backbones, StreamClarify improves Macro accuracy by up to 8.2 points over backbone-matched baselines. At comparable trigger rates, entropy-based selection achieves more than 18 points higher precision than random selection in identifying initially incorrect answers. Under oracle user guidance, clarification improves Macro accuracy by up to 3.5 points. We also report a controlled, oracle-assisted Qwen3-VL simulation on Ambiguous QA with generated questions and referent-only user replies, where the entropy-gated condition improves accuracy by 8.3 points.

  • Label-Free Localization and Test-Time Suppression of CLIP's Typographic Circuits CV
    Bohan Liu, Wenqian Ye, Guangzhi Xiong, Zhenghao He et al. · arXiv:2611.01513
    Abstract

    CLIP-based Vision-Language Models have become cornerstones of modern multimodal systems. The cross-modal contrastive objective equips the CLIP vision encoder to both see objects and read text. These two abilities induce an entanglement between the encoding of lexical and semantic shapes, creating a distinctive vulnerability to typographic attacks, in which rendered text overrides an image's semantic content with its literal meaning. The black-box nature of these encoders has prevented a mechanistic interpretation of such entanglement. We introduce a head-decomposed Sparse Dictionary Learning (SDL) architecture that, by design, decomposes individual attention heads into interpretable, head-specific latents. Based on this inductive disentanglement, we re-purpose Clarity Score and propose normalized Text Attribution Score, which are both label-free, to identify attention heads that carry typographic information. As a causal validation of successful circuit separation, we perform a mechanistic intervention that reweighs attention away from the patch tokens in the identified lexical heads at test-time. Experiments show that the intervention effectively recovers object classification under typographic attack and, across five CLIP variants and four typographic benchmarks, achieves up to 46.9% improvement while incurring up to 1% accuracy loss in general image perception, outperforming prior supervised and training-free methods. Expanding on the mechanistic intervention, we propose a test-time adaptive attention head routing to further reduce the impact on performance at matched robustness, pushing beyond the Pareto frontier of current robustness-perception tradeoff.

  • VLMs Read Aloud: The Gap Between Recognizing and Using Visual Text CV
    Zhiyun Shi · arXiv:2611.01517
    Abstract

    Visual-text compression renders long contexts as images and is engineered around how much of the text a vision-language model (VLM) can recover. Yet recovered text is not usable text. VLMs can transcribe the text in an image word for word and still fail to answer from it; given back their own transcript, they answer nearly as well as from the original text. We call this difference the read-use gap. Recognizing and using visual text have different resolution requirements: Qwen3-VL-8B's recognition saturates at 15-pixel characters while use catches up only at 24 pixels, and scaling from 2B to 32B raises recognition without narrowing the gap. Putting the evidence into words closes it: thinking VLMs recite the evidence in their thinking, and their own transcript, written without seeing the question, recovers most of what thinking adds. In a preregistered held-out test, the gap ranks how much eight VLMs gain from their own words over pixels (Spearman $\rho$ = 0.86), which recognition rates do not. We propose ALOUD, which spends visual resolution on finding and recognizing evidence and language tokens on using it. Without keeping the source text, it doubles the accuracy of a frozen Qwen3-VL-8B on long documents compressed 5x and 10x: at 5x, from 27.7% to 54.8%, near the original text's 58.5%, with a context smaller than the compressed pages themselves. For less than the cost of reading the original text, it leads at equal budget every method that does without it, at every level, and beats AGAR and VERA, which paste the original text back; at 5x it also beats LensVLM-9B, which is trained and keeps the original text. Visual-text compression is limited not by how much text a model can recover, but by how much it can use.

  • AR-RRG: Anatomy-Guided Retrieval-Driven Radiology Report Generation CV
    Xiang Zhang, Shijie Song, Wanqing Zhao, Wei Zhou et al. · arXiv:2611.01529
    Abstract

    Radiology report generation (RRG) streamlines clinical workflows and alleviates the burden of radiologists' documentation. Standard vision-language models capture global visual cues and lack fine-grained alignment between anatomical regions and diagnostic text. Most retrieval-augmented methods retrieve complete historical reports, introducing redundant noise and severe vision-text mismatches that trigger clinically risky hallucinations and missed lesions. In this paper, we introduce AR-RRG, an anatomy-guided retrieval-driven method for the generation of radiology reports. AR-RRG consists of two core modules: the anatomy-aware self-training lesion detection network (ASLDNet) and the retrieval-driven radiology report generation (RRRG). ASLDNet leverages anatomy-aware spatial verification (ASV) and class-adaptive dynamic thresholding (CDT) via self-training, delivering accurate lesion localization to support subsequent generation. As the generative branch of AR-RRG, RRRG eliminates clinical hallucinations through dual-granularity retrieval (DR) and complements missing lesions with a neighborhood consensus (NC) mechanism. A parallel continuous visual perception (PCVP) stream converts radiographs into visual tokens through fixed medical encoding and resampling. Multi-scale text and visual features are fused, followed by LoRA-efficient large language model tuning to generate clinically consistent, visually grounded radiology reports. Extensive evaluations on MS-CXR, MIMIC-CXR, and IU X-Ray demonstrate that AR-RRG consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods across mainstream metrics. Our code will be made publicly available.

  • SurgQ-VLM: Question-Conditioned Dual-Stream Compression and Answer-Type-Aware Reinforcement for Surgical Video Question Answering CV
    Dhruv Makwana, Ananya Mantravadi · arXiv:2611.01913
    Abstract

    Reasoning about surgical video -- which phase is underway, which instruments act, and when events occur -- underpins operative documentation, workflow analysis, and skill assessment. Yet surgical visual question answering is still largely built on single frames and closed labels that cannot express a duration, an ordering, or a count, and the video models that lift the frame limit typically compress every clip the same way regardless of the question, discarding the temporal evidence the answer needs. Worse, the answers are structurally heterogeneous, spanning five incompatible structures -- binary, classification, multi-label, counting, and sentence-temporal -- that no single training signal scores uniformly well. We propose SurgQ-VLM, a two-stage model for surgical video question answering. Stage 1 trains a question-conditioned dual-stream (slow/fast) compressor for a LoRA-adapted Qwen2.5-VL-7B: cross-attention conditions both streams on the question, and each clip's visual tokens shrink 49x from the raw ViT patches (11x vs. post-merge backbone tokens). Stage 2 post-trains with GRPO under an answer-type-aware reward that scores each question on the metric matching its answer structure (micro-F1 for label sets, normalised error for counts, a content-timing blend for temporal windows). On a sealed 21,840-question test split over Cholec80 and CholecT50, SurgQ-VLM reaches 0.779 keyword accuracy and a 10x BLEU-4 gain over zero-shot; GRPO adds 2.5 pp K-Acc (2.0 pp attributable to the answer-type-aware reward), concentrated in the structurally hard types, with answer format unchanged (ROUGE-L). Code is available at this https URL.

  • Vision-language classification of Torsades de Pointes risk from pseudo-coloured ECG images: do T-wave morphology and colour context help or hinder? CV
    Dario Labrador Alonso, Alaa Alahmadi · arXiv:2611.01921
    Abstract

    Contextual prompting is widely presented as a cheap route to better performance from vision models, but the evidence comes largely from benchmarks in which an attribute named in a prompt is also present in the image, confounding the two channels. We investigate the significance of context for a semantically complex biomedical signal classification task using a domain-adapted vision-language model, BiomedCLIP. Building on our previous work that renders electrocardiogram (ECG) signals as colour-enhanced images to flag Torsades de Pointes risk using the QT-nomogram and pseudo-colouring technique, we cross four context strategies with four T-wave morphology label sets, evaluated zero-shot and with a class-balanced linear probe, under five-fold stratified cross-validation on 9,789 images. Zero-shot inference is unusable. Once trained, the colour-enhanced images outperform the colourless ones on every metric: balanced accuracy 0.903 versus 0.825, sensitivity 0.914 versus 0.827, specificity 0.892 versus 0.823, ROC-AUC 0.955 versus 0.888 and Matthews correlation coefficient (MCC) 0.437 versus 0.311. Crossing colour-in-image with colour-in-prompt separates the confounded channels: the image contributes +0.126 MCC (+0.078 balanced accuracy, +0.067 ROC-AUC) whether or not the prompt names colour, whereas naming it changes nothing under any metric. Context is two-sided: the best prompt set gains 0.008 MCC where colour is present and 0.016 where it is not, while the worst loses 0.017 and 0.013. For a signal rendered as an image, the leverage lies in how the signal is drawn rather than in how it is described - and where the endpoint is an interval measurement, prompts naming T-wave morphology describe the wrong quantity.

  • NAS-PO: Native Attention-Strategy Policy Optimization for Vision-Language Models CV
    Ke An, Shizhe Xiang, Yue Liu, Liang Chen et al. · arXiv:2611.02008
    Abstract

    Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has become an effective post-training paradigm for vision-language models (VLMs). Standard RLVR, however, assigns identical outcome-derived credit to all generated tokens regardless of their perceptual relevance. Recent perception-aware methods estimate visual reliance through image perturbation or masking. These indirect proxies require perturbation branches, while corruption sensitivity does not necessarily identify the visual evidence used for reasoning. We instead shape model perception through native attention, an intrinsic model signal. Rather than treating attention as a post-hoc explanation, we formulate it as a reward-anchored, token-aligned routing signal that encourages attention patterns supporting visually grounded reasoning. To this end, we propose NAS-PO, a Native Attention-Strategy Policy Optimization framework. NAS-PO analyzes native attention with a visual mass policy that quantifies visual allocation and a conditional visual policy that captures spatial routing over visual tokens, making the optimization well-posed. Based on this, Native Attention-Strategy Advantage Shaping (NAS-AS) draws on the visual-mass policy to redistribute signed policy-gradient credit for visually engaged rollouts. The Adaptive Attention-Coupled (AAC) loss then aligns the visual-mass policy on all response tokens and the conditional visual policy on visually active ones, consolidating the visual-routing patterns that drive successful trajectories. Experiments demonstrate that NAS-PO substantially surpasses leading RL-tuned VLMs across 7 out-of-domain reasoning and perception benchmarks, with its effectiveness consistently validated across 8B and 32B. Visual-Routing Sensitivity (VRS) analysis further indicates that the optimized attention prioritizes visually load-bearing regions.

  • Learning to Look: A Foundation for Visual Understanding CV
    Wenshuai Zhao, Yihao Wang, Benjamin Robson, Juho Kannala et al. · arXiv:2611.02053
    Abstract

    Vision-language models (VLMs) suffer from visual hallucination, such as saying an object that is not present in an image. We ask a more fundamental question: whether a VLM is even aware of where it is looking before understanding the semantic meaning in the image. We formulate this question as a LOOK task: identifying which parts of its visual input were available to the model. We construct the task in a self-supervised way without manually labeling the available region to the model. Our experiments show that existing VLMs fail to localize the visible region. We further show that fine-tuning VLMs on the proposed LOOK task improves a model's awareness of visual input.

    Abstract

  • PriCBMs: Multi-granular Concept Bottleneck Vision-Language Models for Visual Privacy Recognition CV
    Shenglei Pei, Haotian Sun, Pengfei Li, Bingxu Lu et al. · arXiv:2611.02661
    Abstract

    Widespread image sharing on social media has increased the need for visual privacy recognition to identify inadvertently exposed privacy information. However, multi-label visual privacy recognition presents two challenges. First, privacy categories represent high-level semantic attributes and do not have a one-to-one correspondence with visual objects. Different visual objects may involve the same privacy category, while the same type of object may contain different types of privacy-sensitive information. In this setting, direct prediction methods generally lack explicit guidance for identifying category-relevant privacy cues, leaving this process implicit in the learned image-to-label mapping and potentially limiting classification performance. Second, privacy cues are often distributed across image regions and may not be adequately represented by whole-image features. To address these challenges, we propose Privacy Concept Bottleneck Models (PriCBMs). By introducing privacy-related concepts as intermediate semantic representations, PriCBMs first identify category-relevant privacy cues through visual-concept matching and then predict privacy categories based on concept responses. This process makes privacy predictions more interpretable. To improve the quality of these concepts, we design a concept selection strategy based on target-category relevance, cross-category discriminability, and semantic diversity. Meanwhile, we introduce a global-region modeling module to capture localized privacy evidence while retaining global context. Experiments on VISPR and FineViP show that PriCBMs outperform evaluated competitors across all four multi-label metrics. Ablation studies and concept-quality analyses support the effectiveness of the proposed design. Code is available at this https URL.

    Wooseok Jang, Seungryong Kim, Yan Lu, Lei Ji · arXiv:2611.02759
    Abstract

    Understanding how objects and their spatial relationships evolve in 3D space over time remains challenging for vision-language models (VLMs), particularly under changing viewpoints. Recent approaches incorporate geometric information into reasoning, but explicit supervision for temporally ordered 4D state evolution and its use as intermediate evidence remains limited. We introduce \textbf{Trace4D}, which trains VLMs to predict 4D state sequences and reason over their inter-timestep changes alongside temporal visual observations. Its 4D-grounded chain-of-thought (CoT) explicitly represents 4D states, analyzes temporal visual context, and connects both as evidence for the answer.     Abstract

    Misleading charts can lead vision-language models to conclusions that conflict with underlying data.   • VLM-as-a-Judge for Person Re-Identification CV
    Bessie Dominguez-Dager, Miguel Cazorla, Vincent Lepetit · arXiv:2611.03027
    Abstract

    Cloth-changing person re-identification (CC-ReID) is still very challenging, and no method clearly dominates: methods that lead on one benchmark collapse on the others, because they rest on different identity cues. We show that a general-purpose vision-language model (VLM), used zero-shot as a judge for reranking a short candidate list, beats the expert retrieval models it reranks by a wide and consistent margin. On the four scenarios of CHIRLA, a long-term CC-ReID benchmark that no publicly released Re-ID model is trained on, reranking the candidates of two frozen experts raises rank-1 on every scenario, by 12.9 points on average over the best expert and 9.4 over the best naive ensemble of the two, with no component fine-tuned on the target data. Merging both experts' shortlists matches or beats reranking the stronger expert's shortlist alone, at lower cost. Introducing a VLM does, however, come at a cost: almost all of the pipeline's FLOPs are spent on it. We therefore borrow a criterion from ensembling and invoke the VLM judge only where the two experts disagree at rank 1. This threshold-free gate removes 40% of the calls, and a comparable share of total compute, with no significant drop in rank-1, trading 1.8 to 3.8 points of mAP. Judging remains the dominant cost, but on a benchmark where dedicated experts stall, we find a frozen general-purpose model to be the strongest judge of identity. The code is available at this https URL.

  • VL-Dedup: Graded Multimodal Entity Resolution for Image-Text Data Deduplication CV
    Zhenxi Liu, Guanze Chen, Dehui Yu, Xingyu Wang et al. · arXiv:2611.03080
    Abstract

    Image-text corpora contain copies of one image, captions that describe one image in different ways, captions reused for different images, and captions that do not match their images. Each case calls for a different curation action. Deduplication and multimodal entity resolution (ER) only decide whether records are the same, and they are evaluated on labeled pairs. Yet pair scores do not tell which records end up in the same cluster or which useful captions get deleted. We therefore formulate image-text deduplication as graded multimodal ER, which decides visual identity, caption handling, and mismatches for each pair, and we evaluate it at three levels: pairs, clusters, and the retained set. Our method, VL-Dedup, groups records of the same visual entity into clusters and caps the size of each cluster. Within each cluster, a vision-language model (VLM) and CLIPScore decide which captions to keep and which to flag. We also build VL-DedupBench, with 50,000 COCO and SOP records and 66,788 labeled pairs. On it, a classifier that selects no false edge among the labeled pairs still joins millions of record pairs from different entities. Spending VLM calls on the pairs inside clusters, rather than on pairs with uncertain scores, gives better caption decisions from the same model. Even when almost all clusters are correct, keeping one record per cluster loses facts in many deletions, while the deletions of VL-Dedup lose much less information.

  • DR Pixel: Page-Native Deep Research with Vision-Language Models CV
    Tiansheng Hu, Yichuan Wang, James V. Talwar, Nicholas Tomlin et al. · arXiv:2611.03097
    Abstract

    Complex documents such as scientific papers contain rich information in tables, figures, and equations; however, existing deep research (DR) agents typically reduce papers to OCR-extracted text. We instead ask whether vision-language models (VLMs) can enable page-native deep research: directly retrieving and reasoning over rendered document page images. We introduce DR Pixel, the first open-source end-to-end multimodal DR agent that uses rendered page images as its sole retrieval and reasoning unit, operating over a corpus of 1.06M pages. To evaluate this setting, we construct DRPixelBench, a benchmark on scientific papers requiring reasoning over text, figures, tables, and equations. Surprisingly, page-native DR already works well without training, outperforming OCR-based baselines across state-of-the-art models like GPT-5.4, Gemini-3.5-Flash, and Qwen3.6-27B, while requiring fewer tool calls. We next turn to DR training. A key challenge is that teacher trajectories for agentic supervised finetuning are noisy, mixing critical reasoning and action steps with redundant or failed attempts. To address this, we introduce counterfactual credit reweighting, a fine-grained credit assignment method that weights individual reasoning and action steps by their counterfactual contribution to the final answer. Our training improves retrieval recall by about 15 points and final accuracy by over 10 points on DRPixelBench, and likewise outperforms the untrained baseline on four existing benchmarks. On out-of-distribution ViDoRe-V3, DR Pixel comes within 1.8 points of an oracle that is given the gold pages directly. Our results suggest that the conventional OCR-then-retrieve pipeline is not only unnecessary but suboptimal: directly retrieving and reasoning over visual document pages makes complex DR both more effective and more efficient.

    Junxi Huang, Wenshuo Chen, Xiucheng Wang, Meitong Shen et al. · arXiv:2611.03132
    Abstract

    An image that is hard to classify on its own may have a helpful neighbor in the same unlabeled batch. Zero-shot vision-language models (VLMs) score images independently and leave this evidence unused. We study small unlabeled query batches with no labeled examples. Visual similarity can reflect pose or background, so it may connect images from different classes. Each image's scores over the class vocabulary offer another way to choose neighbors. We introduce Behavioral Affinity Transduction (BAT), which builds a sparse graph from these class-response profiles. The graph supplies neighbor evidence during prediction.   • EO-Jev: Decision Model for Earth Observation CV

    Abstract

  • CheXPoT: Post-Hoc Visual Verification and Correction of VLM-Generated Radiology Reports CV
    Joey Guo, Rayan Ansari, Alaa Youssef · arXiv:2611.00785
    Abstract

    Vision-language models (VLMs) have shown promise for chest radiograph report generation, but adoption remains limited because of hallucinated findings that are not grounded in the image. Correcting these hallucinations and improving overall report quality remains a significant challenge. Prior work has addressed this problem by refining models with reinforcement learning, preference optimization, or chain-of-thought supervision, often at substantial compute cost. To address this gap, we introduce CheXPoT, a program-of-thought (PoT) framework that verifies each generated finding against image features and removes those it cannot ground. CheXPoT decomposes generated reports into predicates with RadGraph and an LLM, grounds these predicates against a DenseNet-121 and Grad-CAM visual evidence table, executes PoT verification code, and rewrites reports after removing unsupported predicates. On a 4,145-image held-out split, CheXPoT yields statistically significant precision gains on 8 of 14 CheXpert classes and F1 gains on 5, with no significant decreases in either metric. CheXPoT lifts micro-average precision from 19.2% (MedGemma-4B) to 46.8% (MedGemma-4B + CheXPoT) and micro-average F1 from 23.0% to 49.4%, absolute gains of 27.6% and 26.4% respectively. We also tested CheXPoT on other VLM report generators. On LLaVA-Med, CheXPoT lifts micro-average precision from 4.5% to 17.7% and micro-average F1 from 6.3% to 19.4%. On MAIRA-2, CheXPoT lifts micro-average precision from 15.6% to 42.1% and micro-average F1 from 21.0% to 45.3%. Our results suggest that inexpensive post-hoc visual verification may improve VLM trustworthiness by correcting unsupported findings from generated reports. CheXPoT lays a foundation for tools that help validate VLM-generated radiology reports and reduce unsupported findings. These innovations may subsequently help increase trust and adoption of VLM tools in Medicine.

  • Control Barrier Functions for Safety-Aware Latent-Space Motion Planning RO/AI
    Chandrakiran Ravula, Siddhanth Ramesh · arXiv:2611.01814
    Abstract

    Latent-space path planning (LSPP) compresses the configuration space of a robotic manipulator into a compact, differentiable manifold learned by a variational autoencoder, enabling fast gradient-based planning without explicit inverse kinematics. Existing LSPP frameworks enforce obstacle avoidance with a binary collision classifier, whose saturated gradients near obstacle boundaries and lack of temporal structure cause the optimizer to stall and provide no forward-invariance guarantee. We replace the classifier with a neural control barrier function (CBF) learned directly in the latent space. The barrier is trained with a three-term objective that combines safe and unsafe sign constraints with a discrete-time decrease condition evaluated on transitions of an obstacle-blind nominal planner, and it is enforced at planning time by a closed-form, first-order correction that projects each goal-directed latent step back onto the CBF constraint. On a 7-DoF Franka Panda manipulator, the CBF planner achieves a 75.6\% success rate on a fixed-obstacle task, 12.0 percentage points above the best of 35 tuned classifier configurations, and 83.8\% on a multi-position task. A sweep of the decay rate $\alpha$ characterizes the safety--performance trade-off, with the best operating points in $\alpha \in [0.10, 0.20]$. In a fully general random-obstacle setting, success drops to 33.0\%, which we trace to covariate shift between the obstacle-blind training transitions and the states visited by the corrected planner.

  • Neural Integral Control Barrier Functions for Agile Collision Avoidance RO
    Dasari Raj Vamsi, Vrushabh Zinage, Efstathios Bakolas, Rohan Chandra · arXiv:2611.02626
    Abstract

    Control barrier functions (CBFs) are a standard way to keep a planner inside a safe set, but they assume control affine dynamics, a barrier function given in closed form, and a known nominal controller. Integral control barrier functions (ICBFs) remove the first assumption and absorb input constraints, yet they still assume a given nominal controller and a barrier function. In this work, we present an approach to learn all three from data, using neural ordinary differential equations (ODEs) for the dynamics, imitation for the nominal controller, and supervised regression for the barrier function. The learned dynamics, nominal controller, and the barrier function are used to build the ICBF filter constraint analytically. Furthermore, the barrier is trained on viability rather than geometry, meaning that a state counts as safe only if some emergency maneuver can still avoid the constraint under the actuator limits, a correction that geometric labels miss entirely and that grows with speed. Actuator limits then enter the online program as hard bounds while the learned barrier rows may be relaxed, which keeps the program solvable at every state and the actuator limits exact, and we bound how far the input it returns can drift from the one the true models would give. On agile racing with a 6-D dynamic bicycle, the filter removes all 12 unsafe episodes from 200 held out layouts at an unchanged success rate and above 100 Hz on one CPU core, holds the input constraint on every episode of a benchmark where a jointly trained baseline holds it on 53.38%, and keeps that advantage under large errors in the plant friction.

  • Route-Guided CBF-QP Repair for Safe Vision-Language-Action Models AI/RO
    Chan Park, Sunwoo Hong, Kirak Kim, Yoonjeong Park et al. · arXiv:2611.01713
    Abstract

    Vision-language-action (VLA) models can generate task-oriented robot actions, but they do not guarantee safe execution in obstacle-rich manipulation scenes. Although CBF-QP safety filters can locally project unsafe actions into a safe set, such correction may push the robot away from the learned task trajectory and leave the VLA policy without a reliable recovery direction. We propose Route-Guided CBF-QP Repair, a post-hoc repair module that uses the short-horizon action chunk predicted by the VLA to detect blocked nominal trajectories and generate entry-exit-rejoin guidance around obstacles. The selected route is added to the CBF-QP objective as a soft guidance term, while the CBF safety constraint remains hard. On SafeLIBERO, our method improves collision avoidance, task success, and SafeSuccess compared with AEGIS. Our project page is available at GitHub.

  • Automated Theorem Generation in First-Order Logic by Construction from Triangular Standard Contradictions AI
    Yang Xu, Shuwei Chen, Hailin Guo, Jun Liu · arXiv:2611.00269
    Abstract

    Automated theorem generation (ATG) seeks to construct new theorems rather than merely prove given conjectures. Existing approaches typically generate candidate statements that require subsequent proof or formal verification. We propose a constructive framework for ATG in first-order logic based on triangular standard contradictions. The method constructs minimally unsatisfiable clause sets from selected first-order literals, then forms theorems by retaining some clauses as premises and taking the negation of one clause, or a conjunction of clauses, as the conclusion. Minimal unsatisfiability ensures that the remaining premises are satisfiable, avoiding trivial inference by explosion, while unsatisfiability of the full set guarantees the resulting entailment. Theorems are therefore correct by construction, without a separate proof procedure for each instance. Extending the previously developed $\Delta_1$ generator, we introduce seven methods, $\Delta_2$--$\Delta_8$, forming a unified family of eight theorem-generation methods. Four generators, $\Delta_1$--$\Delta_4$, have been implemented and released. We also formulate a general $\Delta$-class method encompassing the family. Combinatorial analysis demonstrates the capacity to generate large numbers of distinct theorems systematically. This framework offers a principled foundation for automated theorem discovery and the construction of logically guaranteed knowledge.

  • Benchmark of THReD: Evaluating an LLM Agent and Its Preprocessing Tools for Turning Electronic Health Records into AI-Ready Datasets AI
    Javad Mohammad Alizadeh, Huanmei Wu · arXiv:2611.02484
    Abstract

    LLM agents are increasingly built into data-analysis software, yet little evidence shows how reliably they preprocess clinical data or whether the tools they invoke give correct, reproducible output. We report a benchmark of THReD, a web-based system that turns electronic health record (EHR) extracts into AI-ready datasets through a chat agent whose proposed steps run only after user confirmation. The agent benchmark has 141 tasks (100 templates) in seven categories, including goal-level requests, failure recovery, requests calling for no action, schema variation and prompt injection through the data, on synthetic tables calibrated to a regional health information exchange; success is judged by checks on the produced data. Over 2,550 episodes with Claude Haiku 5.5 in four context configurations, the deployed agent completed 92.7% (95% CI 88.1-96.4) of tasks on its first answer, abstained on every advice request, recovered from every injected fault, and never proposed an action requested by planted text. Without the data profile, completion fell to 58.0%; goal-level requests were hardest (80.6%).
