  • CRaFT-XDet: Causal and Robust Fusion Transformer for Dual-Energy X-ray Prohibited Item Detection CV/AI
    Moinul Hossain, Munshaibur Rahman Mahin, Taskin Khaleque, Mohammad Sakib Mahmood et al. · arXiv:2611.00072
    Abstract

    Automated prohibited item detection in X-ray security imagery is fundamentally constrained by severe object overlap, scanner-dependent domain shifts, and spurious correlations induced by cluttered baggage composition. Existing detectors largely rely on appearance-driven supervision, limiting their robustness and cross-scanner generalisation. We introduce CRaFT-XDet, a causality-grounded and physics- motivated detection framework that reformulates X-ray threat detection as invariant representation learning under attenuation-driven modality variation. Our approach is grounded in a Scanner-Invariant Causal Detection formalisation (Theorem 1), which establishes that minimising the proposed Causal Invariance Regularisation (CIR) objective across partitioned scanner environments approximates the interventional distribution P (Y | do(Xobj)) under standard identifiability assumptions, providing a principled causal formulation for dual-energy X-ray detection. CRaFT-XDet integrates: (i) causal feature disentanglement to suppress context-induced shortcuts; (ii) physics-motivated regularisation to enforce material-consistent embeddings; and (iii) transformer-based cross-energy interaction modelling for structured reasoning in cluttered scenes. With 29.2 M parameters, 89.5 GFLOPs, and 345 FPS (1.7 ms inference), CRaFT-XDet is real-time deployable. Trained on SIXray with five-fold cross-validation, the framework achieves 0.9389 mAP@0.5 and 0.8021 mAP@0.5:0.95. Without domain adaptation, CRaFT-XDet maintains strong cross- dataset performance on OPIXray and HiXray, indicating robust generalization across heterogeneous scanner distributions. In category-agnostic binary threat detection, the model attains 93.92% recall and 0.9031 mean IoU, highlighting reliability in safety-critical screening where missed detections are costly.

  • VerusWeave: A Benchmark for Proof Repair in Concurrent Verus Programs AI
    Pedro Ribeiro, Martim Afonso, André Vasconcelos, João F. Ferreira · arXiv:2611.00287
    Abstract

    Concurrent software underpins critical infrastructure, including operating systems and databases, yet thread interactions can have bugs that bypass conventional testing. Verus extends Rust with formal verification capabilities, enabling developers to specify, implement and prove the correctness of concurrent code in the same language. Although coding agents have been explored for automated proof construction, existing Verus benchmarks offer limited coverage of concurrency-specific examples that connect atomic operations to abstract protocols. To address this gap, we introduce VerusWeave, the first benchmark dedicated to concurrent proof repair, derived from four open-source projects. It comprises 75 focused tasks and 17 full-module variants that exercise features used to verify low-level concurrent code. The evaluation harness validates repairs using Verus and a change checker, recording solutions, execution traces, and resource usage. An initial evaluation of four general-purpose coding-agent harnesses achieved 75-85% success on focused tasks and 29-47% on full-module variants, highlighting the challenge of autonomous module-wide repairs. VerusWeave provides a reusable foundation for evaluating automated proof construction for concurrent software.

  • ERIA: An Instruction-Optimized Agent for Clinical EEG Reporting AI
    Ahmed Sameh, Gayal Kuruppu, Neeraj Wagh, Bridger Brundy et al. · arXiv:2611.01394
    Abstract

    Clinical EEG interpretation requires integrating technical quality, background and behavioral states, interictal and ictal abnormalities, and overall clinical context across long recordings.   • Online Active Learning Architecture for Safety-Critical Control Systems CV
    Shuiwen Shen, Jinhua Zhang, Yan Wang, Huanqiang Zeng et al. · arXiv:2611.01907
    Abstract

    The transition of information-centric artificial intelligence to safety-critical applications is impeded by the limitations of conventional offline-training paradigms in open-world environments. This paper presents Control AI, an architecture for Online Active Learning (OAL) inspired by the continuous Prediction-Evaluation-Correction process of biological intelligence. The framework combines the hierarchical representation capacity of deep neural networks with the real-time error-feedback adaptation of adaptive control. A prediction model maps sensory input into a latent manifold, an optional latent model represents transitions in that space, and a reconstruction model maps the latent state back to observable signals. Residuals between predictions and measurements provide the internal learning signal. Spatial and temporal cross-reconstruction architectures prevent OAL from collapsing to a trivial identity mapping, while coupled, dual, and localized variants shorten error-transmission paths. Localized predictive coding style OAL is implemented through guidance-backpropagation and target-backpropagation architectures, and hybrid and scaled variants support high-dimensional deployment. The paper focuses on the architectural rationale and the progression from a conceptual feedback loop to practical large-scale configurations.

  • End-to-End Stability of Online Active Learning in Large-Scale Neural Systems CV
    Shuiwen Shen, Jinhua Zhang, Chenlei Hou, Peng Shi et al. · arXiv:2611.02488
    Abstract

    Online Active Learning (OAL) aims to extend deep learning from static inference after offline training to continuous adaptation during real-time interaction with a changing physical environment. This transition is essential for safety-critical applications, but it raises a central theoretical question: whether the online parameter update process remains stable when scaled to multilayer neural architectures. This paper develops a stability-focused OAL framework. The framework uses prediction error as a feedback signal to update a structured deep model, thereby linking online learning with Lyapunov-based energy minimization. We show that prediction error convergence alone is insufficient for global parameter convergence because multilayer networks may admit high-dimensional manifolds of equivalent output solutions. The analysis therefore separates output error regulation, internal zero dynamics, interlayer coupling, and parameter convergence. Persistency of excitation and active damping are then used to remove the residual null-space modes that remain after output error convergence. A cascaded Lyapunov analysis establishes asymptotic stability of the overall OAL learning process, while monotonic activation functions and diffeomorphic layer mappings provide the uniqueness condition for the target parameter trajectory. The resulting proof provides a theoretical basis for applying online adaptive learning to large-scale neural systems in nonstationary and safety-critical settings.

  • Programmable Droplets as Active Matter in a Reconfigurable Environment RO
    Ivan Shmarov, Abhishek Sharma, Michael Jirasek, Kristine Laws et al. · arXiv:2611.01277
    Abstract

    Exploring systems that bridge the gap between non living and living matter requires a reconfigurable environment coupled to synthetic systems capable of autonomous motion, adaptive behaviour and persistent dynamics. Self propelled droplets provide a minimal platform for such life like behaviour, but quantitative measures capturing both persistence and adaptability to enable closed loop reconfiguration remain lacking. Here we introduce an autonomous closed loop robotic platform to characterise and dynamically program the autonomous behaviour of multicomponent oil in water droplets. We explored two chemical composition spaces, with and without external pH perturbations, analysing droplet behaviour in real time and iteratively selecting formulations. To quantify this behaviour, we introduce two descriptors, persistence and diversity. Persistence is quantified by mapping temporal dynamics in a speed shape complexity space, where we identify a persistence domain that distinguishes active from inactive droplets. Diversity captures spontaneous or environmentally induced variation in motion and the propensity of droplets to reorient or adapt under perturbation. We show that persistence and diversity are constrained by a behavioural trade off: high persistence limits diversity, whereas high diversity reduces persistent activity. Our results provide a quantitative framework for navigating and programming life like behaviours in active droplets and show how closed-loop experimentation can uncover organising principles in complex active matter.

    Abstract

For example, a service may be rerouted while its latency-monitoring traffic continues along the original path, producing measurements that no longer reflect the service's performance. We propose Closed-Loop Dependency Assurance (CLDA), an assurance mechanism that augments agentic AI-enabled NDTs with dependency-aware validation. In our framework, an agentic AI planner generates and refines network plans, while the NDT evaluates the projected network outcomes of these plans. CLDA extends this analysis using a Loop Dependency Graph (LDG) that connects service requirements to monitoring mechanisms, forwarding paths, failure domains, and recovery prerequisites. CLDA projects candidate actions onto these dependencies, checks intermediate states of multistep plans, and supports deterministic make-before-break repairs using temporary monitoring mechanisms. Unlike the Large Language Model (LLM)-based planner, CLDA uses explicit, deterministic rules for assurance decisions and repair. We evaluate CLDA through controlled reconfigurations, a 240-trial LLM-based planning campaign, and live network execution. A separate synthetic study examines deterministic assurance scalability across network models representing up to 1,024 routers.

  • Knowledge-Guided Floodwater Level Reasoning for Cloud-Edge Collaborative Monitoring CV/AI
    Zhengshu Zhou, Shaoyu Wang, Ziyi Geng, Lu Tao et al. · arXiv:2611.01970
    Abstract

    Frequent urban floodwater poses a serious threat to the safety and efficiency of intelligent transportation systems. Existing vision-based methods for floodwater level detection face two main limitations. First, the lack of explicit reasoning leads to poor detection performance and little interpretability. Second, these methods struggle to generalize to the complex and varied real-world road scenes seen from different viewpoints. To address this, we reformulate the task as an explicit visual reasoning problem and propose HydroReasoner, a floodwater level reasoning framework designed for cloud-edge collaboration. HydroReasoner builds on Qwen2.5-VL and is trained with Group Relative Policy Optimization. In addition, we build HydroLevel, a floodwater benchmark dataset of 3,000 images, for training and evaluating the model.   • Semantic Interoperability in Federated Digital Twin Systems: A Systematic Review Through the Levels of Conceptual Interoperability Model AI
    Manonmani Sekar, Nasim Nezamoddini · arXiv:2611.02125
    Abstract

    When two companies connect their digital twins, software models of their plants kept up to date with live data, each twin must translate the other's data into its own terms, and that translation rule, called a mapping, can stop being correct when one company changes the meaning of its data without telling the other. This PRISMA 2020 systematic review screened 5,833 records and read 119 full texts to determine whether any published system detects such a broken mapping and repairs it while the twins operate. In all 32 included studies the mapping is fixed before operation, and the two ingredients of run-time repair appear only separately: cross-company matching without any check that the match still holds, and detection and repair of changed meaning within a single company. The review turns this gap into six requirements, each illustrated on one running example, that a system claiming run-time semantic interoperability between independently owned twins should meet.

  • OpenULF: A Modular Toolkit Enables Data-Driven Ultra-Low-Field MRI with both Classical and Quantum Sensors CV
    Hanhan Wu, Yuchen Yuan, Weiwen Jiang · arXiv:2611.01511
    Abstract

    Ultra-low-field (ULF) Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) offers a portable, low-cost alternative to conventional scanners by operating at field strengths below 10 mT. At these fields, two sensor technologies compete: quantum SQUID (Superconducting Quantum Interference Device) sensors, which provide high sensitivity but require cryogenic cooling, and classical induction coils, which are inexpensive and room-temperature but produce weaker signals. With conventional physics-based imaging, both suffer from low signal-to-noise ratios (SNR). Data-driven reconstruction has overcome similar limits in related inverse problems, but its use for ULF-MRI has been hindered by a lack of open datasets, benchmarks, and flexible simulators. To close this gap, we present OpenULF, a modular, open-source simulation toolkit that generates paired training data and supports end-to-end machine-learning (ML) reconstruction. OpenULF integrates a Bloch-equation-validated physics simulator, calibrated SQUID and coil receiver models, reconstruction ML networks, and an automated design-space exploration engine. Architecturally, a receiver abstract base class, a typed HDF5 contract that decouples physics from learning, and a resumable sweep engine make sensors, models, and datasets independently substitutable. Using OpenULF, we train 774 models across four anatomical datasets and conduct three design-space explorations. The effectiveness of ML reconstruction depends strongly on the dataset and the sensor. We identify a SQUID-to-coil crossover near 450 $\mu$T and demonstrate that sensor hardware and ML model capacity can be traded off during system design. Sensor and reconstruction choices must therefore be co-designed: the best sensor is set by the dataset and field strength, not by SNR.

  • Camera-Free Object Discovery and Interaction with GazeTags CV
    Vimal Mollyn, Chris Harrison · arXiv:2611.02599
    Abstract

    We present GazeTags, a camera- and computer-vision-free approach for worn head-gaze object awareness. Our small electronic tags cost roughly \$2 each and can operate for more than a year on a coin cell battery. They work in concert with a user-worn device (smartglasses, hearables, etc.) that periodically emits a focused infrared signal aligned with the user's head direction. Only GazeTags within that narrow head-oriented beam wake and respond, providing their identity and optional payloads such as object state or live sensor readings. By avoiding cameras and computer vision pipelines, our approach is very energy efficient, able to operate continuously even in battery-constrained wearables, and sidesteps non-trivial privacy issues. We describe our hardware design, characterize our system performance and operating tradeoffs, and demonstrate how GazeTags can enable practical gaze-aware environments.
